Poisson Manifolds of Compact Types with an appendix by Joshua Mundinger Thanks: The authors were partially supported by NWO Vici grant no. 639.033.312, NSF grant DMS-2303586, MCIN-AEI grant PID2022-139069NB-I00 and CNPq grant 304049/2018-2.
Marius Crainic, Rui Loja Fernandes, David Martínez Torres

Abstract
. We develop the theory of Poisson and Dirac manifolds of compact types, a broad generalization in Poisson and Dirac geometry of compact Lie algebras and Lie groups. We establish key structural results, including local normal forms, canonical stratifications, and a Weyl type resolution, which provides a way to resolve the singularities of the original structure. These tools allow us to show that the leaf space of such manifolds is an integral affine orbifold and to define their Weyl group. This group is a Coxeter group acting on the orbifold universal cover of the leaf space by integral affine transformations, and one can associate to it Weyl chambers, reflection hyperplanes, etc. We further develop a Duistermaat-Heckman theory for Poisson manifolds of s-proper type, proving the linear variation of cohomology of leafwise symplectic form and establishing a Weyl integration formula. As an application, we show that every Poisson manifold of compact type is necessarily regular. We conclude the paper with a list of open problems.



1.
Introduction

This paper concerns an important class of Poisson and (possibly twisted) Dirac manifolds, generically called of proper type. These objects are analogous to compact Lie algebras and Lie groups in Lie theory, or foliations whose leaf spaces are orbifolds. We show that, in many respects, the theory of Poisson and Dirac manifolds of proper type closely parallels the classical theory of compact Lie algebras and Lie groups. In fact, one of the key takeaways of this paper is that many fundamental aspects from the theory of compact Lie groups are purely Poisson geometric.

For the purposes of this introduction, we will focus primarily on Poisson manifolds of proper type, though many results also hold for twisted Dirac structures of proper type. A Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
is said to be of
proper type
if there is a proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
with connected source fibers integrating
(M,\pi)
. This class was first introduced in
[
11
]
under the broader designation of
Poisson manifolds of compact types
(PMCT). That work established fundamental properties of PMCTs and provided various examples and constructions. Further examples and constructions of PMCTs were obtained by Álvarez
[
4
]
, Martínez Torres
[
30
]
and Zwaan
[
47
]
.

In
[
12
]
, we focused on regular PMCTs, i.e., those whose symplectic leaves have the same dimension. We showed that regular PMCTs possess a rich transverse geometry, where various structures – both classical and novel – interact in intricate ways. These include orbifold structures, integral affine structures, symplectic gerbes, and more. In this paper we consider arbitrary PMCTs, regular or not.

For a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
of proper type, we will begin by showing that:

\bullet

It admits two
canonical stratifications
: one of an infinitesimal nature, and a second one, associated with a choice of proper integration, which refines the former. Both are smooth stratifications by Poisson submanifolds, and these strata exhibit remarkable properties (Section
3
).

\bullet

It has a canonical
Weyl resolution
, defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{M}

\displaystyle:=\{(x,\mathfrak{t})\,|\,x\in M,\,\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{x}\,\mathrm{maximal\ torus}\},

\displaystyle{\mathscr{R}}

\displaystyle:\widehat{M}\to M,\quad\ {\mathscr{R}}(x,\mathfrak{t}):=x,

where
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is the isotropy Lie algebra of
(M,\pi)
at
x
. The resolution
(\widehat{M},\widehat{L}_{\pi})
is a
regular
Dirac manifold and
{\mathscr{R}}
is a forward Dirac map that is a diffeomorphism over the regular locus
M^{\mathrm{reg}}
(Section
4
).

For instance, when
M=\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the dual of a compact Lie algebra, its Weyl resolution is diffeomorphic to
G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*}
, where
T\subset G
is a maximal torus with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
, and
W
is the classical Weyl group. The resolution map is then
{\mathscr{R}}([gT,\xi])=\Ad_{g}\xi
.

The Weyl resolution allows to reduce questions about the
singular
Poisson geometry of
(M,\pi)
to questions about the
regular
Dirac geometry of
(\widehat{M},\widehat{L}_{\pi})
. Moreover, the map
{\mathscr{R}}
induces an identification of the leaf spaces of
(\widehat{M},\widehat{L}_{\pi})
and
(M,\pi)
, along with their algebras of smooth functions. This generalizes the well-known identification
\mathfrak{t}^{*}/W\cong\mathfrak{g}^{*}/G
and a related classical theorem due to Chevalley, and leads to the following (see Section
5
):

Theorem 1
.

Each proper symplectic integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
of a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
induces an integral affine orbifold structure on its leaf space
B
. Moreover:

(i)

The underlying classical orbifold structure is independent of the choice of integration and is a good orbifold;

(ii)

The smooth stratifications of
M
induce orbifold stratifications of
B
.

We recall that a classical good orbifold is one that can be realized as the quotient of a manifold by a discrete group acting properly and effectively. In particular, for any such orbifold, its orbifold universal covering space is a manifold. In our case, much more holds, as demonstrated by the following result (Section
6
):

Theorem 2
.

The orbifold universal covering space
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
of the leaf space
B
of a Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)
is an integral affine manifold. The orbifold fundamental group
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
acts on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
by integral affine transformations and fits into a split short exact sequence:

(1.1)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 69.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 133.37051pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 133.37051pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 208.12994pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 208.12994pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
B^{\mathrm{reg}}
is the leaf space of the regular locus
M^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

We call the kernel
\mathcal{W}(M,\pi):=\mathcal{W}
of the sequence (
1.1
) the
Weyl group
of
(M,\pi)
. We also call the connected components of the regular locus of the action of
\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
the
Weyl chambers
of
(M,\pi)
. In the next statement, to be proved in Section
7
, by a
geometric reflection
we mean an involution whose fixed point set is an integral affine submanifold that separates
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
:

Theorem 3
.

The Weyl group
\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
of a Poisson manifold of proper type is generated by geometric reflections and acts transitively and freely on the set of Weyl chambers. In particular,
\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
is a Coxeter group.

When
M
is the dual of a compact Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, equipped with the canonical linear Poisson structure, the group
\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}})
coincides with the classical Weyl group (Section
8.1
). When
M
is a compact connected Lie group
G
, equipped with the Cartan-Dirac structure, the group
\mathcal{W}(G,L_{G})
coincides with the affine Weyl group of
G
(Section
8.2
). These facts, along with the many similarities between
\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
and the classical Weyl groups, justify our use of the term “Weyl group”. It should be noted, however, that the definition of the Weyl group of a Poisson manifold of proper type does not rely on classical Lie theory, and there exist many examples of Poisson manifolds whose Weyl group is not a classical Weyl group (Section
8.3
).

All the results stated so far have analogs for twisted Dirac structures. In fact, the main tool used in their proofs is the local normal form around leaves for symplectic, and more generally, twisted presymplectic groupoids, as discussed in Section
2
. Next, we turn to results that are specific to Poisson manifolds.

We recall from
[
11
]
that a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
is of
s-proper type
if it admits a symplectic integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
whose source map is proper and has connected fibers. It is of
compact type
if it admits a symplectic integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
that is compact. One has obvious implications

\text{compact type}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{s-proper type}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{proper type}.

A Poisson manifold of s-proper type has compact symplectic leaves. The classical
Duistermaat-Heckman theory
generalizes to these type of Poisson manifolds as follows.

First, using the Weyl resolution and the integral affine structure on the leaf space, we will show that for an s-proper Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
, the cohomology class of the symplectic forms
\omega_{b}
of the leaves
S_{b}
varies linearly. More precisely, one has a flat integral orbivector bundle over the leaf space
B
with fibers the second cohomology groups of the symplectic leaves:

\mathcal{H}\to B,\quad\mathcal{H}_{b}:=H^{2}(S_{b}),

and we have (Section
9
):

Theorem 4
.

For any s-proper Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
with leaf space
B
, the map

B\ni b\mapsto[\omega_{b}]\in H^{2}(S_{b})

defines an integral affine section of
\mathcal{H}\to B
. Moreover, the (signed) leafwise symplectic volumes define a polynomial function
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to\mathbb{R}
on the integral affine manifold
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
, whose square descends to a smooth function
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}^{2}:B\to\mathbb{R}
.

Next, an s-proper integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
induces a
Duistermaat-Heckman measure

\boldsymbol{\mu^{DH}_{B}}
on
B
by first pushing forward the Liouville measure
\frac{\Omega^{\mathrm{top}}}{{\mathrm{top}}!}
along the source map, and then along the quotient map
p:M\to B
. On the other hand, the integral affine structure on
B
mentioned previously gives rise to a
Lebesgue measure

\boldsymbol{\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{B}}
.
In Section
10
we will show that the classical Duistermaat-Heckman and the Weyl integration formulas extend to our setting (we refer to that section for explanations of notation).

Theorem 5
.

For any s-proper integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
of
(M,\pi)
one has

\boldsymbol{\mu^{DH}_{B}}={\mathscr{V}}^{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{B}}.

Moreover, for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(M)
one has

\int_{M}f(x)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}\mu^{DH}_{M}}(x)=\int_{B}\left(\int_{\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)}f({\mathscr{R}}(t(g)))\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}(g)\right){\mathscr{V}}^{2}(b)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{B}}(b).

In Section
11
, we deduce structural results about Poisson manifolds of s-proper type and compact type. For example, just as the cohomology of a symplectic form on a compact manifold is constrained, the existence of a multiplicative symplectic form on a compact groupoid forces the groupoid to be regular. In fact, we will show the following.

Theorem 6
.

Any Poisson manifold of compact type is regular. Equivalently, every compact symplectic groupoid is regular.

We emphasize that the proof of this result is non-trivial, involving virtually all the previous theorems, along with some new facts about polynomial functions on integral affine orbifolds.

Finally, in Section
12
, we conclude the paper by enumerating several open problems that arise naturally from the theory developed here.

2.
Local Normal Forms

In this section we describe two normal forms around symplectic leaves for Poisson manifolds of proper type and their associated proper symplectic groupoids. These normal forms yield important tools for our study of non-regular PMCTs.

The first normal form is the
linear normal form
which we have already encountered in the first paper of this series
[
11
, Section 8]
. It is a normal form which holds on any small enough tubular neighborhood of a symplectic leaf of a Poisson manifold of proper type. It linearizes, in an appropriate sense, the Poisson structure around the leaf.

The second normal form is the so-called
Hamiltonian normal form
which holds on an entire saturated neighborhood of a symplectic leaf of any Poisson manifold of proper type. This saturated neighborhood may fail to retract on the leaf and the Poisson structure in this model is not linear. Still, it exhibits the Poisson structure as a symplectic quotient of a Hamiltonian
G
-space and this will turn out to be useful for us.

We also discuss suitable generalizations of these normal forms to the Dirac setting.
These will be needed later when we study the desingularization of PMCTs.

2.1.
The Hamiltonian normal form

Consider a (right) Hamiltonian
G
-space

\mu:(Q,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}.

If the action is free and proper, then
Q/G
inherits a
reduced Poisson structure
\pi_{\mathrm{red}}
uniquely characterized by requiring
p^{*}:C^{\infty}(Q/G)\hookrightarrow C^{\infty}(Q)
to be a morphism of Poisson algebras, where
p:Q\to Q/G
is the quotient map. Geometrically,
\pi_{\mathrm{red}}
is the Poisson structure with symplectic leaves the connected components of the symplectic quotients
\mu^{-1}(\xi)/G_{\xi}
(see, e.g.,
[
13
, Section 1.5]
). In our set up,
0
is in the image of
\mu
and we focus on and around the symplectic quotient
\mu^{-1}(0)/G
, which we denote by
(Q//G,\omega_{\mathrm{red}})
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(S,\omega_{S})
be a symplectic leaf in a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
of proper type. Then there exists a saturated open neighborhood
U
of
S
in
M
that is Poisson diffeomorphic to the reduced space
Q/G
associated to some connected, free, Hamiltonian
G
-space

(2.1)

\mu:(Q,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*},

where
G
is a compact Lie group,
\mu
has connected fibers and
0\in\mu(Q)
. Under this diffeomorphism
(S,\omega_{S})
corresponds to
(Q//G,\omega_{\mathrm{red}})
.

Letting
V=\mu(Q)\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, the Hamiltonian normal form will also be pictured by the diagram

(2.2)

\textstyle{(Q,\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{(U,\pi)}
\textstyle{(V,\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}

This indicates that we think of
(Q,\omega)
as defining, locally around
S
, a
dual pair
(see, e.g.,
[
13
, Section 6.4]
) between
(M,\pi)
and the linear Poisson structure
(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\pi_{\mathfrak{g}}^{*})
.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

The Hamiltonian space (
2.1
) arises from a proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
integrating
(M,\pi)
. Namely,
G
is the isotropy group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
,
Q
is the preimage
\mathbf{s}^{-1}(T)
of a transversal
T
to
S
through a base point
x\in S
,
\omega
is the restriction
\Omega|_{\mathbf{s}^{-1}(T)}
and
U
is the saturation of
T
.
In order to describe the
G
-action on
Q
, by general facts about symplectic groupoids (see Remark
2.2
below), we have a symplectic Morita equivalence:

\textstyle{(\mathcal{G}|_{U},\Omega|_{U})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(Q,\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{t}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{s}}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{G}|_{T},\Omega|_{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(U,\pi)}
\textstyle{(T,\pi_{T})}

where
\pi_{T}
is the Poisson structure induced by
\pi
on the transversal. Since
(\mathcal{G}|_{T},\Omega|_{T})
is proper, by the Weinstein-Zung Linearization Theorem
[
43
,
45
]
if
T
is taken sufficiently small it follows that
(\mathcal{G}|_{T},\Omega|_{T})
is isomorphic to
(T^{*}G|_{V},\Omega_{\mathrm{can}})
, where
V\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a
G
-invariant neighborhood of
0
. Since
T^{*}G|_{V}
can be identified with the coadjoint action groupoid
G\ltimes V
, we obtain an action of
G
on
Q
.

Under this isomorphism,
(T,\pi_{T})
is identified with
(V,\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}})
, where
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
denotes the linear Poisson structure on the dual of the Lie algebra. One then obtains the groupoid version of the diagram (
2.2
)

\textstyle{(\mathcal{G}|_{U},\Omega|_{U})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(Q,\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p=\mathbf{t}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{s}}
\textstyle{(T^{*}G|_{V},\Omega_{\mathrm{can}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(U,\pi)}
\textstyle{(V,\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}})}

showing that
(Q,\omega)
is a symplectic Morita equivalence between
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
and
T^{*}G|_{V}
. Using the identification
T^{*}G|_{V}\simeq G\ltimes V
, the statement follows setting
\mu:=-\mathbf{s}
.
∎

Notice that the proof also gives a local normal form for the symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
. The symplectic Morita equivalence yields an identification between
(\mathcal{G}|_{U},\Omega|_{U})
and the “gauge groupoid”

(2.3)

((Q\times_{V}Q)/G,\underline{\Omega})\rightrightarrows Q/G

where
\underline{\Omega}
is the symplectic form corresponding to the basic form

(2.4)

\textrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\omega-\textrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\omega\in\Omega^{2}(Q\times_{V}Q).

See
[
11
, Proposition 5.6]
for more details.

Remark 2.2

(Morita equivalences)
.

Throughout the paper we will make use of Morita equivalences and their basic properties in various contexts. For a brief introduction and further references please see
[
12
, Section 3.1 and Appendix A3]
. For later use we recall here that any Morita equivalence between groupoids,

\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{1}}
\textstyle{M_{2}}

gives rise to a homeomorphism between the the spaces
M_{1}/\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
M_{2}/\mathcal{G}_{2}
and isomorphisms between the isotropy groups of the two groupoids and their isotropy representations. We need here a more precise description of these isomorphisms, as well as of the general philosophy of using Morita equivalences to pass from one side to the other.

First of all, we say that that two saturated subspaces
N_{1}\subset M_{1}
and
N_{2}\subset M_{2}
are
Q
-related
if
p_{1}^{-1}(N_{1})=p_{2}^{-1}(N_{2})
. The homeomorphism
M_{1}/\mathcal{G}_{1}\to M_{2}/\mathcal{G}_{2}
is the correspondence that assigns to an orbit
\mathcal{O}_{1}
of
\mathcal{G}_{1}
the
Q
-related orbit of
\mathcal{G}_{2}
.

Secondly, we will say that a point
x_{1}\in M_{1}
is
Q
-related to
x_{2}\in M_{2}
if the orbits through them are
Q
-related. That is equivalent to the existence of
q\in Q
with
p_{1}(q)=x_{1}
,
p_{2}(q)=x_{2}
. The choice of such a point
q
gives rise to:

(i)

an isomorphism between the isotropy groups,
\psi_{q}:\mathcal{G}_{x_{1}}\to G_{x_{2}}
, uniquely determined by

g\cdot q=q\cdot\psi_{q}(g),\quad\forall\ g\in\mathcal{G}_{x_{1}}.

(ii)

an
\psi_{q}
-equivariant isomorphism
\nu_{x_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{x_{1}})\to\nu_{x_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{x_{2}})
between the representations of the isotropy groups on the normal spaces to the orbits. This isomorphism is uniquely determined by the condition that, for any
v\in T_{q}Q
, the class of
\mathrm{d}p_{1}(v)
is sent to the one of
\mathrm{d}p_{2}(v)
.

In the case of symplectic groupoids and symplectic Morita equivalences, we have
\nu_{x_{i}}=\mathfrak{g}_{x_{i}}^{*}
, and the normal isotropy representations of
G_{x_{i}}
are identified with the coadjoint representations of
G_{x_{i}}
. In this case, the isomorphisms from item (ii) are dual to the ones from item (i).

2.2.
The linear normal form

Given
any
Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
and fixing a symplectic leaf
(S,\omega_{S})
, Vorobjev
[
40
,
41
]
has constructed a
linear local model
which is a certain Poisson structure
\pi_{\mathrm{lin}}
on an open neighborhood of the zero section in the normal bundle
\nu(S)
with the following two properties:

•

(S,\omega_{S})
, identified with the zero section of
\nu(S)
, is a symplectic leaf of
\pi_{\mathrm{lin}}
;

•

on each fiber
\nu(S)_{x}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{x}
,
\pi_{\mathrm{lin}}
restricts to the linear Poisson structure
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{x}}
.

In general, the germ of
\pi
around
S
may fail to be isomorphic to the germ of
\pi_{\mathrm{lin}}
around the zero section. When they are isomorphic one says that
(M,\pi)
is
linearizable
around
S
. If, additionally, one can find arbitrary small saturated neighborhoods of
S
where
\pi
and
\pi_{\mathrm{lin}}
are isomorphic then one says that
(M,\pi)
is
invariantly linearizable
around
S
.

For PMCTs we have (see
[
11
, Section 8]
):

Theorem 2.3
.

If
(M,\pi)
is a Poisson manifold of proper (respectively, s-proper) type then it is linearizable (respectively, invariantly linearizable) around any symplectic leaf.

For a general Poisson manifold the local linear model
(\nu(S),\pi_{\mathrm{lin}})
is not so simple to describe and depends on an auxiliary choice of an IM-connection (see
[
24
]
for a modern approach). However, for a PMCT (in fact for any integrable Poisson manifold) one can describe the linear local model very explicitly. This was already observed in
[
11
, Section 5.8]
but we review it here in greater detail, since it will be needed in the sequel.

The starting data consists of:

(i)

A symplectic manifold
(S,\omega_{S})
;

(ii)

A principal
G
-bundle
p:P\to S
, with
P
connected and
G
compact;

(iii)

An auxiliary principal connection 1-form:

\theta\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g}).

The group
G
acts diagonally on
P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, where on the second factor one considers the coadjoint action. One has a 2-form

(2.5)

\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}:=p^{*}\omega_{S}-\mathrm{d}\langle\theta,\cdot\rangle\in\Omega^{2}(P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}),

where
\langle\theta,\cdot\rangle\in\Omega^{1}(P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})
is the form given by

\langle\theta,\cdot\rangle_{(p,\zeta)}(v,w)=\langle\theta_{p}(v),\zeta\rangle.

The 2-form
\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
is closed,
G
-invariant, and it is non-degenerate at points of
P\times\{0\}
.

Hence, there is a
G
-invariant open set
P\times\{0\}\subset\mathcal{V}\subset P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
such that
\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
restricts to a symplectic form on
\mathcal{V}
.
It follows that
\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
induces a Poisson bivector
\pi^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
on the quotient:

\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{V}/G.

The pair
(\mathcal{M},\pi^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})
is the desired
linear local model
.
Notice that when
S
is compact the space
P
is compact, and one can take
V
of the form

\mathcal{V}=P\times V,

where
V\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a
G
-invariant neighborhood of
0
. In this case the projection in the second factor defines a Hamiltonian
G
-space with connected fibers

(2.6)

\textrm{pr}_{2}:(P\times V,\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})\to\mathfrak{g}^{*},

hence, the corresponding symplectic quotients

S_{\xi}:=P\times_{G}\mathcal{O}_{\xi}\simeq P/G_{\xi},

form the symplectic foliation of
(\mathcal{M},\pi^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})
.

For a PMCT
(M,\pi)
the data needed to construct the linear local model around a symplectic leaf
(S,\omega)
is obtained from a symplectic integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
. Fixing a base point
x\in S
, the relevant principal
G
-bundle
p:P\to S
is

(2.7)

\mathcal{G}_{x}\curvearrowright\mathbf{s}^{-1}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}S.

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.

The proof consists of 4 steps:

Step 1:
Consider the groupoid

(2.8)

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}:=(P\times P)\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rightrightarrows P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}^{*},

the quotient of the product of the pair groupoid
P\times P\rightrightarrows P
with the identity groupoid
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rightrightarrows\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. It carries a closed,
multiplicative form
\Omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
induced by the basic 2-form

(2.9)

\widetilde{\Omega}^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}:=p_{1}^{*}\omega_{S}-p_{2}^{*}\omega_{S}-\mathrm{d}\langle\theta_{1},\cdot\rangle+\mathrm{d}\langle\theta_{2},\cdot\rangle\in\Omega^{2}(P\times P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})

where
p_{i}:P\times P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to S
denotes the composition of the projection on the i-th factor with the bundle projection
p:P\to S
, while
\langle\theta_{i},\cdot\rangle
denotes the 1-form

\langle\theta_{i},\cdot\rangle_{(p_{1},p_{2},\zeta)}(v_{1},v_{2},w)=\langle\theta_{p_{i}}(v_{i}),\zeta\rangle.

The form
\Omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
restricts to a symplectic form on
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}|_{\mathcal{M}}
, and this yields a symplectic groupoid integrating the linear local model
(\mathcal{M},\pi^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})

Step 2:
If
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
integrates
(M,\pi)
and we let
p:P\to S
be the principal bundle (
2.7
), the groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}
is isomorphic to the action groupoid formed from the action of the restricted groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{S}:=\mathcal{G}|_{S}
on the normal bundle
\nu(S)=P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
:

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}\simeq\mathcal{G}_{S}\ltimes\nu(S).

The right-hand side is the linearization of
\mathcal{G}
at
S
, see
[
16
,
20
,
43
]
.

Step 3:

If
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
is a proper groupoid, then by step 2 and the Weinstein-Zung linearization theorem
[
16
,
20
,
43
,
45
]
there are open sets
S\subset U\subset M
and
S\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subset\nu(S)
such that we have a groupoid isomorphism

\mathcal{G}|_{U}\simeq\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}.

If
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
is a s-proper groupoid, then we can take
U
saturated and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=P\times_{G}V^{\prime}
, with
V^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
a
G
-invariant open neighborhood of
0
.

Step 4:
The isomorphism in the previous step provides a second multiplicative symplectic form on
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
, besides
\Omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
. Since these two forms agree on
\mathcal{G}_{S}
, by a multiplicative version of Moser’s method, possibly after possibly shrinking
U
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, one obtains a groupoid automorphism which pulls back the second symplectic form to
\Omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
, so that we have an isomorphism of symplectic groupoids

(\mathcal{G}|_{U},\Omega|_{U})\simeq(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}},\Omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}).

See also
[
11
, Section 8]
.

A different proof of this result, using the Hamiltonian local form, follows from the proof of Theorem
2.5
(the case
\beta=0
).
∎

Remark 2.4
.

The construction of the linear local model depends on the choice of connection
\theta
. Indeed, both
\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
and the invariant open set
\mathcal{V}\subset P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
will depend on this choice, and hence also
(\mathcal{M},\pi^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})
. However, two different choices of connection lead to isomorphic local models on a possibly smaller invariant neighborhood of
S
in
P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. In fact, if
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are two connections with open invariant sets
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, then on the overlap
\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
equation (
2.9
) shows that the symplectic forms
\Omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
and
\Omega^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{lin}}
differ by
\mathrm{d}\beta
, where
\beta
is a multiplicative 1-form vanishing on
\mathcal{G}_{S}
. Hence, a multiplicative version of Moser’s method implies that the corresponding symplectic groupoids are isomorphic around
S
. It follows that the corresponding linear local models
(\mathcal{M},\pi^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})
and
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\pi^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{lin}})
are also isomorphic around
S
.

2.3.
Local models in the Dirac setting

The previous constructions of the Hamiltonian local model and the linear local model extend to Dirac manifolds and presymplectic groupoids. We will be interested in this more general setting because we will introduce later a resolution of non-regular PMCTs and proper symplectic groupoids that produce regular DMCTs and proper presymplectic groupoids.

The extension of the Hamiltonian local model to the Dirac setting is as follows:

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
(S,\omega_{S})
be a presymplectic leaf in a proper Dirac manifold
(M,L)
. Then there exists a saturated open neighborhood
U
of
S
in
M
that is Dirac diffeomorphic to the reduced space
Q/G
associated to some connected, free, Hamiltonian presymplectic
G
-space

(2.10)

\mu:(Q,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*},

where
G
is a compact Lie group,
\mu
is a submersion with connected fibers and
0\in\mu(Q)
. Under this diffeomorphism
(S,\omega_{S})
corresponds to
(Q//G,\omega_{\mathrm{red}})
.

Here by a Hamiltonian presymplectic
G
-space we mean a
G
-manifold
Q
together with a
G
-invariant, closed 2-form
\omega
, and a
G
-equivariant map
\mu:Q\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
satisfying the usual moment map condition. We will always assume that the action if free and proper, and the moment map is a submersion – in the presymplectic setting the latter is not implied by the former. These conditions guarantee that
Q/G
inherits a reduced Dirac structure
L_{\mathrm{red}}
uniquely determined by imposing that the quotient map is forward Dirac (see, e.g.,
[
5
]
).

The Hamiltonian presymplectic space in the theorem is constructed similarly to the Poisson setting. Using a proper presymplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
integrating
(M,L)
, one sets
Q:=\mathbf{s}^{-1}(T)
and
\omega:=\Omega|_{T}
, where
T
is a small enough transversal
T
to the presymplectic leaf
(S,\omega_{S})
.

The proof is also similar to the Poisson setting where
(Q,\omega)
gives a presymplectic Morita equivalence with
(\mathcal{G}|_{T},\Omega|_{T})
. Here we need to apply the theory of presymplectic groupoids and presymplectic Morita equivalences (see
[
11
, Section 3.2]
,
[
12
, Appendix A]
and
[
44
]
). Note that the Dirac structure induced by
L
on
T
is actually the graph of a Poisson structure. Hence, the restriction
(\mathcal{G}|_{T},\Omega|_{T})
is a symplectic groupoid and the Weinstein-Zung linearization theorem can still be applied.

Remark 2.6
.

Similarly to what we observed after the proof of Theorem
2.1
, one also deduces a normal form for the proper presymplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
. One obtains a presymplectic groupoid isomorphism with the gauge groupoid (
2.3
), equipped with the presymplectic form induced by (
2.4
), formed from
(Q,\omega)
above.

Let us now turn to the linear local model. The Dirac setting actually makes the construction of the model simpler, since one does not need to restrict to a
G
-invariant neighborhood of the origin in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Furthermore, the linear model also works for non-compact Lie groups, so we momentarily drop that assumption.

For the starting data one now assumes that
(S,\omega_{S})
is just a presymplectic manifold. Then, given a principal
G
-bundle
p:P\to S
with
P
connected, and an auxiliary principal connection 1-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})
,
the coadjoint bundle

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}}:=P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}^{*},

carries a natural Dirac structure
L^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
. Namely, the quotient Dirac structure induced by the graph of the closed form
\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}\in\Omega^{2}(P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})
given by (
2.5
). Explicitly, this Dirac structure is given by

L^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}:=\big\{(\mathrm{d}q(v),\eta):q^{*}\eta=i_{v}\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}\big\},

where
q:P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}}
denotes the quotient map. Here, just as in the Poisson case, the projection

\textrm{pr}_{2}:(P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*},\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})\to\mathfrak{g}^{*},

defines a Hamiltonian presymplectic
G
-space, and the presymplectic foliation of
L^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
consists of the presymplectic quotients

S_{\xi}:=P\times_{G}\mathcal{O}_{\xi}\simeq P/G_{\xi}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}},

where now
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is arbitrary.

The construction of a canonical presymplectic groupoid integrating
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}},L^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})
is as in Step 1 in the proof of Theorem
2.3
.
The Dirac manifold
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}},L^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})
will be called the
(Dirac) linear local model
associated
to
p:P\to(S,\omega)
and
\theta
, while
(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}},\Omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})
will be called the
linear groupoid local model
. We will often drop the connection from the notation, for the reason explained in the next remark, and write simply
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}},L_{\mathrm{lin}})
and
(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}},\Omega_{\mathrm{lin}})
.

Remark 2.7
.

1)

The choice of connection
\theta
affects the linear local model as follows. Given two principal connections
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
, the closed 2-form

\mathrm{d}\langle\theta,\cdot\rangle-\mathrm{d}\langle\theta^{\prime},\cdot\rangle\in\Omega^{2}(P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})

is a basic form, so induces a closed 2-form
\beta\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}})
. The Dirac structures are related by a gauge transformation in the sense of
[
9
]
:

L^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{lin}}=e^{\beta}L^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}.

Similarly, using
2.9
, at the groupoid level the presymplectic forms change by a gauge transformation

\Omega^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{lin}}=\Omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}+\mathbf{t}^{*}\beta-\mathbf{s}^{*}\beta.

2)

Assume that in the preceeding discussion the principal bundle is over a symplectic base
(S,\omega_{S})
. Then
S
admits a
Poisson neighborhood

\mathcal{M}
, i.e., there is an open neighborhood

S\subset\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}},

where
L^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
is defined by a bivector
\pi^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
. Indeed, the set of points where a Dirac structure is the graph of a bivector is open and, since
\omega_{S}
is non-degenerate, the “Poisson support” of
L^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
contains
S
. The restriction of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}
to
\mathcal{M}
is then a symplectic groupoid and one recovers in this way the Poisson linear local model and its integration.

3)

We will be interested in the case where
G
is compact. In that case,
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}
is a proper groupoid, so the Dirac linear local model is always a Dirac manifold of proper type. It is of strong proper type iff
P
is 1-connected,
and it is of s-proper type iff
P
is compact (i.e.,
S
is compact).

Finally, let us turn to the Dirac analogue of Theorem
2.3
. Linearization, in general, can only be achieved up to gauge transformation. This is in agreement with the fact that in the Dirac category an isomorphism consists of a diffeomorphism composed with a gauge transformation.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a proper presymplectic groupoid which integrates the Dirac manifold
(M,L)
and let
(S,\omega_{S})
be a presymplectic leaf. Let
G
be the isotropy group of
\mathcal{G}
at some base point
x\in S
, let
p=\mathbf{t}:P=\mathbf{s}^{-1}(x)\to S
be the isotropy-principal bundle at
x
and let
(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}},\Omega_{\mathrm{lin}})
be the resulting linear Dirac local model. Then there are arbitrarily small neighborhoods
S\subset U\subset M
and
S\subset U_{\mathrm{lin}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}}
and an isomorphism of presymplectic groupoids:

\textstyle{(\mathcal{G}|_{U},\Omega|_{U})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}|_{U_{\mathrm{lin}}},\Omega_{\mathrm{lin}}+\mathbf{t}^{*}\beta-\mathbf{s}^{*}\beta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{U}
\textstyle{U_{\mathrm{lin}}}

for some exact 2-form

\beta=\mathrm{d}\gamma\in\Omega^{2}(U_{\mathrm{lin}}).

Under this isomorphism,
S
is mapped diffeomorphically to
\mu^{-1}(0)/G
, and the following can be arranged:

(i)

If
\mathcal{G}
is source proper, then
U
and
U_{\mathrm{lin}}
can be chosen saturated;

(ii)

If
\mathcal{G}
is source 1-connected, then one can choose
\gamma
whose pullback to
S
is zero.

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

By Theorem
2.5
we may assume that:

1)

we have a Hamiltonian presymplectic
G
-space
\mu:(Q,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, with
\mu
a submersion;

2)

the Dirac manifold and presymplectic leaf are the quotients

M=Q/G,\quad S=Q//G=\mu^{-1}(0)/G;

3)

The integrating groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is the associated gauge groupoid

\mathcal{G}=Q\times_{G}Q\rightrightarrows M.

After possibly replacing
Q
by a smaller
G
-invariant neighborhood of
P:=\mu^{-1}(0)
in
Q
, one may assume that we have a
G
-equivariant submersive retraction
r:Q\to P
, and that
(r,\mu):Q\to P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a diffeomorphism onto an open subspace of
P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. In other words, we may assume that
Q\subset P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is an
G
-invariant open subset and
\mu
is the projection
\textrm{pr}_{2}
.

We then have two multipicative presymplectic forms
\Omega
and
\Omega_{{\mathrm{lin}}}^{\theta}
on the same gauge groupoid, and both can be recovered from their pullbacks to
Q
,
\omega
and
\omega_{{\mathrm{lin}}}^{\theta}
(see Remark
2.6
).
These forms are
G
-invariant and their difference
\omega-\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
is
G
-basic since (i) each makes
\mu:Q\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
into a presymplectic
G
-Hamiltonian space and (ii) the moment map conditions give, for any
v\in\mathfrak{g}
,

i_{v_{Q}}(\omega-\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})=\mathrm{d}\langle\mu,v\rangle-\mathrm{d}\langle\mu,v\rangle=0,

where
v_{Q}
is the fundamental vector field of the action of
v
on
Q
. Moreover,
the difference
\omega-\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}
pulls back to zero along
P
. Therefore, there exist
\beta\in\Omega^{2}(U_{\mathrm{lin}})
satisfying

\omega-\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}=\mathbf{t}^{*}\beta,

where
\mathbf{t}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}}\to U_{\mathrm{lin}}
is the target map,
and such that its pullback to
S=S\times\{0\}\subset U_{\mathrm{lin}}
vanishes.
Since we can assume that
S
is a deformation retract of
U_{\mathrm{lin}}
, we conclude that
\beta=\mathrm{d}\gamma
,
\gamma\in\Omega^{1}(U_{\mathrm{lin}})
. This proves the first half of the theorem.

If
\mathcal{G}
is source proper, then in the Weinstein-Zung linearization theorem we can take arbitrary small saturated neighborhoods, and item (i) follows.

If
\mathcal{G}
is source 1-connected, then the long exact homotopy sequence for the projection
p:P\to S
implies that
S
has finite fundamental group, and therefore trivial first de Rham cohomology group. Hence, the pullback of
\gamma
to
S
is exact with primitive
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
. If we let
F\in C^{\infty}(U_{\mathrm{lin}})
be an extension of
f
, then

\gamma-\mathrm{d}F\in\Omega^{1}(U_{\mathrm{lin}})

is a primitive for
\beta
whose pullback to
S
vanishes. This proves item (ii).

∎

Remark 2.9
.

Theorems
2.5
and
2.8
have
\phi
-twisted versions. If on a
\phi
-twisted Dirac manifold
(M,L)
one fixes a leaf
(S,\omega_{S})
, then one obtains:

(i)

A Hamiltonian local model, as in Theorem
2.5
, where now we have a Hamiltonian twisted presympletic
G
-space
(Q,\omega)
, with
\mathrm{d}\omega=p^{*}\phi
;

(ii)

A linear local model, where in the starting data one has a twisted presymplectic manifold
(S,\omega_{S})
. The resulting linear local model has a twisted Dirac structure
L_{\mathrm{lin}}
with twist
\mathrm{d}({\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\omega_{S})
, where
{\mathrm{pr}}:P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to S
is the (tubular neighborhood) projection. Theorem
2.8
still holds, where the integrating groupoid is
(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}},\Omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})
, with
\mathrm{d}({\mathrm{pr}}^{*}\omega_{S})
-twisted presymplectic form 2-form still given by formula (
2.9
).

There are also linear local models and linearization results for Poisson (respectively, Dirac) manifolds along Poisson (respectively, Dirac) submanifolds, generalizing the ones along leaves (see
[
24
]
).

3.
The Canonical Stratifications

This section is the starting point of our investigation of the geometry of the leaf spaces of possibly non-regular PMCTs. We shall define two canonical stratifications, we will analyze the Poisson geometric properties of their strata, and we will describe the structure induced on the leaf spaces of the strata by a choice of a proper symplectic integration.

The stratifications can be introduced right away. Recall that any Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
induces an algebroid structure on
T^{*}M
, with anchor
\pi
interpreted as a map
\pi^{\sharp}:T^{*}M\to TM
.
The corresponding symplectic foliation is

(3.1)

\mathcal{F}_{\pi}:={\mathrm{Im}}(\pi^{\sharp}),

and the isotropy Lie algebras coincide with the conormal spaces to the foliation

(3.2)

\mathfrak{g}_{x}(M,\pi):=\Ker(\pi^{\sharp}_{x})=\nu^{*}_{x}(\mathcal{F}_{\pi}):=(\mathcal{F}_{\pi})^{o}\subset T^{*}_{x}M.

When no confusion arises, we will use the simpler notation
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
for the isotropy Lie algebras.

We define a first equivalence relation on
(M,\pi)
by setting

(3.3)

x\underset{{\mathrm{inf}}}{\sim}y\quad\textrm{iff}\quad\mathfrak{g}_{x}\simeq\mathfrak{g}_{y}.

We define a second equivalence relation, using the linear holonomy representation of the symplectic leaf
S
through
x\in M
on the center of the isotropy
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})
– see (
3.18
) – by setting

(3.4)

x\underset{{\mathrm{hol}}}{\sim}y\quad\textrm{iff}\quad\begin{cases}\mathfrak{g}_{x}\simeq\mathfrak{g}_{y}\\
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{\Hol}\simeq\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{y})^{\Hol},\end{cases}

where the superscript denotes the fixed-point set.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold of proper type.

(i)

Its
canonical infinitesimal stratification

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
is the partition of
M

by the connected components of the equivalence classes w.r.t
\underset{{\mathrm{inf}}}{\sim}
.

(ii)

Its
canonical stratification

\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
is the partition of
M

by the connected components of the equivalence classes w.r.t
\underset{{\mathrm{hol}}}{\sim}
.

For any proper Lie groupoid there exist two canonical stratifications (see
[
14
,
37
]
), which generalize the well-known
orbit type
and
infinitesimal orbit type
stratifications of a proper Lie group action (see, e.g.,
[
22
,
36
]
). We will see that the canonical stratifications of a Poisson manifold of proper type coincide with the stratifications obtained from
any
proper symplectic integration.

We emphasize that the partitions in Definition
3.1
make sense for any Poisson manifold, integrable or not. However, these partitions may be ill-behaved and fail to satisfy the requirements to fit into a stratification. For a Poisson manifold of proper type the situation is much nicer as they do form stratifications which enjoy remarkable Poisson-theoretic properties. We will show that:

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold of
\mathcal{C}
-type. Then the strata of both the canonical and the infinitesimal stratifications are regular Poisson submanifolds of
(M,\pi)
of
\mathcal{C}
-type. Furthermore, the infinitesimal strata are core Poisson submanifolds of
(M,\pi)
.

The notion of (regular) core Poisson submanifold, mentioned in the theorem, arises in connection with the transverse geometry of the ambient Poisson manifold. More precisely, core Poisson submanifolds retain all the information about monodromy of the ambient Poisson manifold (see Definition
3.17
).

A more detailed version of the previous theorem states that, from a proper integration of
(M,\pi)
, one obtains proper integrations of each member of either canonical stratification. Since the strata are regular Poisson manifolds, the results in the second paper in this series
[
12
, Chapter 3]
show that the leaf space of each stratum
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
and
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
is an integral affine orbifold. Moreover, the strata are saturated submanifolds of the ambient Poisson manifold so if
\mathfrak{S}\subset\Sigma
, one obtains inclusions of leaf spaces:

\mathfrak{S}/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}\subset\Sigma/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}\subset M/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}.

It is natural to wonder if the integral affine orbifold structures on
\mathfrak{S}/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
and
\Sigma/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
are induced by an integral affine orbifold structure on
M/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
. However, the ambient Poisson manifold is not regular, so
[
12
]
does not apply to it. We will be able to address this issue once we have introduced in Section
5
the desingularization of
(M,\pi)
. Hence, we postpone to that section the discussion of leaf spaces of PMCTs.

3.1.
Stratifications and orbit types

Before we discuss in detail the canonical stratifications of PMCTs, we will start by providing a short overview of the basics on stratifications and we will recall the orbit type stratification of a proper Lie groupoid.

3.1.1.
General stratifications

We consider stratifications of a space
M
in the following sense.

Definition 3.3
.

A
stratification

\mathcal{S}
of a topological space
M
is a locally finite partition into locally closed, connected, subspaces – called
strata
– which are manifolds and satisfy the following frontier condition: the closure of each strata
S\in\mathcal{S}
satisfies

\overline{S}=S\cup\bigcup_{i}S_{i},

where
S_{i}
are strata satisfying
\dim S_{i}<\dim S
. When
M
is a manifold and each stratum is an embedded submanifold we call
\mathcal{S}
a
smooth stratification
.

Remark 3.4

(connected strata)
.

The condition that the strata be connected is more important than it may seem at first sight. This condition makes the notion of stratification into a local one, which agrees with Mather’s germ-viewpoint on stratifications but without reference to germs (see, e.g.,
[
36
]
). In practice, one usually starts with a locally finite partition
\mathcal{P}
of
M
by manifolds and one passes to the partition
\mathcal{P}^{c}
consisting of the connected components of the members of
\mathcal{P}
. In many examples (e.g., for the partition by orbit types), the frontier condition holds only after this passage. That implies, in particular, that a stratification may be induced by several different (but interesting) partitions. To handle such situations, one has the following simple lemma (see, e.g.,
[
14
]
).

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
be two partitions of a space
M
by manifolds with the subspace topology (whose connected components may have different dimensions). Let
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{c}
be the partition obtained by taking the connected components of the members of
\mathcal{P}_{i}
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{1}^{c}=\mathcal{P}_{2}^{c}
if and only if, for each
x\in M
, there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
x
in
M
such that

P_{1}\cap U=P_{2}\cap U,

where
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}
are the members containing
x
.

The strata of a stratification
\mathcal{S}
of
M
are partially ordered by

S\preccurlyeq T\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad S\subset\overline{T}.

The strata that are maximal w.r.t.
\preccurlyeq
are precisely the open strata. The union of
all the maximal strata form a dense open subset of
M
, called the
\mathcal{S}
-regular part
of
M
and denoted

M^{\mathcal{S}-{\mathrm{reg}}}\subset M.

A stratum
S
is called
subregular
if it is maximal among the non-regular strata.

The
\mathcal{S}
-regular part is the first member of a filtration

C_{0}\subset C_{1}\subset\ldots\subset C_{n}=M,

where
C_{k}
consists of the disjoint union of all strata of codimension at most
k
. If
\mathcal{S}
is a smooth stratification, this codimension is relative to the ambient manifold
M
. For a general stratification the codimension of a stratum is relative to the dimension of the maximal stratum whose closure contains it. The stratification
\mathcal{S}
is recovered from the filtration by considering the connected components of the codimension
k
-locus:

(3.5)

\Sigma_{k}:=C_{k}\setminus C_{k-1}.

The axioms on
\mathcal{S}
translate into properties of the filtration – e.g., that
C_{0}
is dense or, as a consequence of the frontier axiom, that each
\Sigma_{k}
is a disjoint union of manifolds closed in
C_{k}
, so all the members of the filtration are open in
M
. One can also consider a similar filtration by dimension, but for us codimension arises more naturally. The previous discussion also implies the following result.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
be two stratifications of
M
and assume that for every
x\in M
the strata of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
through
x
have the same codimension. Then
\mathcal{S}_{1}=\mathcal{S}_{2}
.

The following result should be standard, but since we could not find a proof in the literature we include one.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a smooth stratification of a manifold
M
.
If
\mathcal{S}
has no strata of codimension
k\in\{1,\ldots,p\}

then the maps induced in homotopy by the inclusion,

i_{*}:\pi_{j}(M^{\mathcal{S}-{\mathrm{reg}}})\to\pi_{j}(M),

are isomorphisms in all degrees
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,p-1\}
.

Proof.

If
S\subset M
is a submanifold of codimension
k
, then the inclusion
M\setminus S\hookrightarrow M
induces an isomorphism

\pi_{j}(M\setminus S)\simeq\pi_{j}(M),\quad(0\leq j<k-1).

This is a folklore result which can be proved representing elements of homotopy groups by smooth maps and using transversality theory.

Now apply this result to the codimension
k
submanifold
\Sigma_{k}
of
C_{k}
. Since
C_{k-1}=C_{k}\setminus\Sigma_{k}
, we conclude that if
\mathcal{S}
has no strata of codimension
k\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
, then

\pi_{j}(M)=\pi_{j}(C_{n})=\cdots=\pi_{j}(C_{p})=\pi_{j}(M^{\mathcal{S}-{\mathrm{reg}}})\quad(0\leq j<p).

∎

3.1.2.
The stratifications induced by a proper action

An important class of examples of stratifications comes from proper actions.

At the global level, an action of a Lie group
G
on a manifold
M
determines the following equivalence relations on
M
:

•

Isotropy types
:
x\sim y
if the isotropy groups
G_{x}
and
G_{y}
are isomorphic.

•

Orbit types
:
x\underset{\textrm{orb}}{\sim}y
if the isotropy groups
G_{x}
and
G_{y}
are conjugate.

•

Local orbit types:

x\underset{\textrm{loc}}{\sim}y
if
G_{y}=\textrm{Ad}_{g}G_{y}
and there is an isomorphism of the normal isotropy representations
G_{x}\curvearrowright\nu_{x}
and
G_{y}\curvearrowright\nu_{y}
compatible with
\textrm{Ad}_{g}
.

•

Morita types
:
x\underset{\textrm{Mor}}{\sim}y
if the normal isotropy representations
G_{x}\curvearrowright\nu_{x}
and
G_{y}\curvearrowright\nu_{y}
are isomorphic (this is equivalent to the existence of a Morita equivalence between neighborhoods of the orbits through
x
and
y
, preserving these orbits).

There are some obvious implications

(3.6)

\textstyle{\underset{\textrm{orb}}{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\underset{\textrm{loc}}{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underset{\textrm{Mor}}{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

For a proper action, all these equivalence relations determine partitions of
M
by submanifolds. Upon passing to connected components, these partitions induce the same smooth stratification of
M
. We call it the
canonical stratification
induced by the proper action, denoted
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
. We also have infinitesimal versions of the equivalence relations above, such as:

•

Infinitesimal isotropy types
:
x\underset{{\mathrm{inf}}}{\sim}y
if
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{y}
are isomorphic.

•

Infinitesimal Morita types
:
x\overset{\textrm{Mor}}{\underset{{\mathrm{inf}}}{\sim}}y
if
(\mathfrak{g}_{x},\nu_{x})
and
(\mathfrak{g}_{y},\nu_{y})
are isomorphic as pairs consisting of a Lie algebra and a representation.

Again one finds that, upon passing to connected components, the partitions defined by them induce the same smooth stratification. We call it the
canonical infinitesimal stratification
induced by the proper action, denoted
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}_{G}(M)
. We refer the reader to
[
14
, Section 4]
for details.

The strata of both
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}_{G}(M)
are saturated by orbits of the action, and therefore give rise to two partitions of the orbit space
M/G
, denoted
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}_{G}(M/G)
. The first partition is a stratification of
M/G
in the sense of Definition
3.3
. The second one would also be a stratification if in the definition we would allow the members of the partition to be orbifolds. Furthermore, the two stratifications can be interpreted as “smooth stratifications of
M/G
”. Indeed, although
M/G
may fail to be a manifold, there are various known frameworks to make sense of “smooth structures” on
M/G
, of smooth subspaces and, ultimately, of smooth stratifications of
M/G
(see Remark
5.3
).

3.1.3.
The stratifications induced by a proper Lie groupoid

Most (but not all) of the previous equivalence relations for proper
G
-actions extend to general proper Lie groupoids
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
. They give rise to the groupoid analogues of the canonical stratifications above.

First of all, one can define right away the isotropy type equivalence relations
\sim
and
\underset{\mathrm{inf}}{\sim}
using the isotropy Lie group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
and the associated Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
, at any point
x\in M
. Then, since there are also isotropy representations
\nu_{x}\in\textrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G}_{x})
one can also introduce the Morita type equivalence relation
\underset{\textrm{Mor}}{\sim}
and its infinitesimal version. Passing to connected components, and using that the slice theorem holds in this generality, one ends up with two smooth stratifications of
M
, induced by
\sim
and
\underset{\mathrm{inf}}{\sim}
, and denoted

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{G}}(M)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}_{\mathcal{G}}(M).

We call them the
canonical stratification
and
the
canonical infinitesimal stratification
induced by
\mathcal{G}
, respectively. These are discussed in greater detail in
[
14
]
.

The regular part of the stratifications
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{G}}(M)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}_{\mathcal{G}}(M)
are called the
principal locus
and the
regular locus
of
M
, respectively:

M^{{\mathrm{princ}}}:=M^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{G}}(M)-{\mathrm{reg}}},\quad M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}:=M^{\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}_{\mathcal{G}}(M)-{\mathrm{reg}}}.

Recalling that a point is regular w.r.t. to a stratification if and only if the corresponding stratum is open, and using the slice theorem, one finds:

\displaystyle M^{{\mathrm{princ}}}

\displaystyle=\{x\in M:\textrm{the action of $\mathcal{G}_{x}$ on $\nu_{x}$ is trivial}\},

\displaystyle M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}

\displaystyle=\{x\in M:\textrm{the action of $\mathfrak{g}_{x}$ on $\nu_{x}$ is trivial}\}.

We will also call the
subregular locus
of
M
the subregular part of the infinitesimal stratification:

M^{\mathrm{subreg}}:=M^{\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}_{\mathcal{G}}(M)-{\mathrm{subreg}}}.

Similarly, at the level of the orbit space
B=M/\mathcal{G}
, one obtains partitions

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{G}}(B)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}_{\mathcal{G}}(B).

Again, these are “smooth stratifications” in the more general sense mentioned before for orbit spaces of proper actions.

Remark 3.8
.

The 1-1 correspondence between subspaces of
B=M/\mathcal{G}
and invariant subspaces of
M
gives rise to a 1-1 correspondence
between stratifications of
B
and
\mathcal{G}
-invariant stratifications of
M
(provided the orbit spaces of the
\mathcal{G}
-invariant strata are manifolds). The fact that the canonical projection
p:M\to B
is an open map implies that the frontier condition is preserved.

Remark 3.9

(Morita equivalences II)
.

It follows from Remark
2.2
that for Morita equivalent proper groupoids
Q
-related points have isomorphic isotropic groups. Hence, both canonical stratifications for proper groupoids are Morita invariant.

3.1.4.
Stratification of foliations of proper type

One class of groupoids that plays an important role for us is that of
foliation groupoids
, i.e., Lie groupoids
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows M
that integrate a foliation
(M,\mathcal{F})
(not necessarily s-connected). Equivalently, these are Lie groupoids
with all isotropy groups discrete. In particular this implies that the infinitesimal stratification on the base of such a Lie groupoid is trivial.

We recall from
[
12
, Chapter 2]
that a foliation
\mathcal{F}
is said to be
\mathcal{C}
-type if it admits a s-connected integration that is of
\mathcal{C}
-type.

Proposition 3.10
.

Two proper s-connected foliation groupoids integrating a foliation
(M,\mathcal{F})
induce the same canonical stratification.

Proof.

Recall that any foliation groupoid
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows M
integrating
\mathcal{F}
covers the holonomy groupoid
\Hol(M,\mathcal{F})
, and the properness of
\mathcal{B}
forces the properness of
\Hol(M,\mathcal{F})
. Therefore it suffices to compare the stratifications induced by
\mathcal{B}
and
\Hol(M,\mathcal{F})
.

In short, the argument is based on a combination of Lemma
3.5
and the Morita equivalence underlying the normal form for
\mathcal{B}
from
[
12
, Theorem 2.5.5]
. More precisely, to check the condition from Lemma
3.5
at a point
x\in M
for the partitions associated to
\mathcal{B}
and
\Hol(M,\mathcal{F})
, we make use of
[
12
, Theorem 2.5.5]
applied at
x
and we consider the resulting Morita equivalence

\textstyle{\mathcal{B}|_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{\Gamma\ltimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U}
\textstyle{V}

where
\Gamma=\mathcal{B}_{x}
,
V=\nu_{x}(S)
and
x
is
Q
-related to
0
. The holonomy group
\Hol_{x}
is the image of the representation map
\Gamma\to\GL(V)
and the Morita equivalence above induces one between the holonomy groupoid and the action groupoid
\Hol_{x}\ltimes V
. By Remark
3.9
, to check the equality from Lemma
3.5
at
x
for the partitions associated to
\mathcal{B}
and
\Hol(M,\mathcal{F})
, we have to check the similar equality on the right hand side at
0
for the partitions associated to
\Gamma\ltimes V
and
\Hol_{x}\ltimes V
. This boils down to the fact that a vector
v\in V
is
\mathcal{B}_{x}
-invariant if and only if it is
\Hol_{x}
-invariant.
∎

Definition 3.11
.

Given a foliation of proper type
\mathcal{F}
of
M
, the
holonomy stratification induced by
\mathcal{F}
, denoted
\mathcal{S}_{\Hol}(M,\mathcal{F})
, is the canonical stratification induced by any s-connected proper foliation groupoid integrating
\mathcal{F}
.

Since each strata is saturated,
\mathcal{S}_{\Hol}(M,\mathcal{F})
induces a partition of the leaf space
M/\mathcal{F}
, denoted

\mathcal{S}_{\Hol}(M/\mathcal{F})
. This turns out to be a stratification in the sense of definition
3.3
. The smoothness of the strata is obtained as a corollary of the previous proof.
We call a leaf
principal
if it is contained in the principal part of the holonomy stratification.

Corollary 3.12
.

For any stratum
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\Hol}(M,\mathcal{F})
one has

\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})=\nu_{M}(\mathcal{F})^{\Hol}

and
(\Sigma,\mathcal{F}|_{\Sigma})
is a simple foliation. In particular a leaf is principal if and only if it has trivial holonomy.

Proof.

The equality follows from the previous proof. The second part follows from the fact that proper foliations with trivial holonomy must be simple.
∎

3.2.
The canonical infinitesimal stratification of PMCTs

Finally, we move to the setting of PMCTs. We will show that the canonical and the canonical infinitesimal stratifications
induced by the corresponding proper symplectic groupoids do not depend on the integrations themselves but only
on the actual Poisson bivector, and that their strata will inherit Poisson-geometric structures. In this subsection we discuss the canonical infinitesimal stratification and in the next one the canonical stratification.

Besides the equivalence relation
x\underset{{\mathrm{inf}}}{\sim}y
, defined by (
3.3
), we also consider
the equivalence relation

x\underset{\textrm{cod}}{\sim}y\quad\textrm{iff}\quad{\codim}_{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}\,\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})={\codim}_{\mathfrak{g}_{y}}\,\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{y}),

that partitions
M
into the subsets

(3.7)

\Sigma_{k}^{{\mathrm{inf}}}(M,\pi)=\{x\in M\,|\,\mathrm{codim}_{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}\,\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})=k\},\quad k=0,1,\dots.

In the case of PMCTs the isotropy Lie algebras are of compact type, and for these we have

\codim_{\mathfrak{g}}\,\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathrm{dim}\,\mathfrak{g}^{{\mathrm{ss}}},

where
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{ss}}
is the semi-simple part of
\mathfrak{g}
. Indeed, this follows from the usual decomposition

(3.8)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{{\mathrm{ss}}},\quad\mathfrak{g}^{{\mathrm{ss}}}:=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}].

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold of proper type. Then the partition
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
is a stratification of
M
and its codimension
k
strata are precisely the connected components of
\Sigma_{k}^{{\mathrm{inf}}}(M,\pi)
. This stratification satisfies:

(3.9)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{0}^{{\mathrm{inf}}}(M,\pi)

\displaystyle=M^{{\mathrm{reg}}};

(3.10)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}^{{\mathrm{inf}}}(M,\pi)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{2}^{{\mathrm{inf}}}(M,\pi)=\emptyset,

where
M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
consists of points where the rank of
\pi
is locally constant.
In particular,
the set
M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
is connected, so all regular leaves have the same dimension, and
the inclusion
M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}\subset M
induces an isomorphism of fundamental groups.

Remark 3.14
.

Equation (
3.9
) says that the regular locus of the Poisson bivector coincides with the regular locus of the infinitesimal stratification, so we can unambiguous call this locus the
regular locus
of
(M,\pi)
. We define the
subregular locus
of
(M,\pi)
as the subregular locus of the infinitesimal stratification

M^{{\mathrm{subreg}}}:=M^{\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)-{\mathrm{subreg}}}.

According to the theorem, this is the subset of
M^{\mathrm{sing}}:=M\backslash M^{\mathrm{reg}}
where the rank
\pi|_{M^{\mathrm{sing}}}
is maximal.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a proper symplectic groupoid integrating
(M,\pi)
. The fact that

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
is a stratification follows from the general discussion on proper Lie groupoids; the discussion below provides further insight.

To prove that
\underset{\textrm{cod}}{\sim}
and
\underset{{\mathrm{inf}}}{\sim}
induce the same stratifications, we proceed like in the proof of Proposition
3.10
. We use Lemma
3.5
and the normal form of Theorem
2.1
, which yields a Morita equivalence (see Remark
2.2
)

\textstyle{\mathcal{G}|_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{G\ltimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U}
\textstyle{V}

with
V\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
invariant under the coadjoint action, and with
x_{0}

Q
-related to
0\in V
. By Remark
3.9
and the fact that
\underset{\textrm{cod}}{\sim}
only depends on isotropy Lie algebras, to prove the condition from Lemma
3.5
at
x_{0}
it suffices to check that

(3.11)

V\cap{\Sigma_{0}}=V\cap\Sigma_{k}^{{\mathrm{inf}}}(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}),

where
\Sigma_{0}
is the stratum of the canonical stratification of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
through the origin and
k
equals the codimension of
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})
in
\mathfrak{g}
.

Notice that
V\cap{\Sigma_{0}}=V^{\mathfrak{g}}
, the fixed-point set of the restriction of the coadjoint action to
V
. On the other hand, we have that
\Sigma_{k}^{{\mathrm{inf}}}(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}})\cap V
consists of the points
\xi\in V
such that
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})
has the same dimension as
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})
. Since
\mathfrak{g}
is compact, this happens if and only if
\xi\in\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}
, which is precisely
V^{\mathfrak{g}}
.

For later reference, we note that the previous argument gives the following description of the stratum
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
through
x_{0}
in the Hamiltonian local normal form

(3.12)

U\cap\Sigma=\mu^{-1}(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{*})/G.

The equality (
3.9
) is a restatement of the fact that a point
x\in M
is regular for
\pi
if and only if
its isotropy Lie algebra is abelian. The equalities (
3.10
) are consequences of the fact that
there are no semisimple Lie algebras of compact type of dimension
1
and
2
.
Lemma
3.7
implies that the inclusion
M^{\mathrm{reg}}=\Sigma_{0}\subset M
induces
an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
, proving also the remaining claims about
M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.15
.

For any stratum
\Sigma
of the infinitesimal stratification of a Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)
and any
x\in\Sigma
one has

(3.13)

(T_{x}\Sigma)^{0}=((\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{x})^{\mathfrak{g}_{x}-{\mathrm{inv}}})^{0}=\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{ss}}_{x}.

In particular,

(3.14)

T_{x}\Sigma/T_{x}S_{x}=\mathfrak{z}_{x}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}.

Moreover, the following are equivalent:

(a)

\Sigma
is a subregular stratum;

(b)

the codimension of
\Sigma
is
3
;

(c)

\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\mathrm{ss}}\cong\mathfrak{su}(2)
for a/any
x\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

The first part of the result follows from (
3.12
). The equivalences (a)-(c)
hold when
(M,\pi)
is the dual of a Lie algebra of compact type. Hence, as in the proof of the theorem, they hold for any Poisson manifold of proper type.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

Theorem
3.13
and Corollary
3.15
admit generalizations to the infinitesimal stratification of any proper groupoid
\mathcal{G}
. Namely, one can show that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(\mathcal{G})
coincides with the stratification induced by the codimension of the fixed point set of the actions of the isotropy Lie algebras on the normal spaces. The identity (
3.9
) remains valid, but not the identities (
3.10
). Also, the identity (
3.13
) now becomes

(T_{x}\Sigma)^{0}=(\nu_{x}(\mathcal{O}_{x})^{\mathfrak{g}_{x}})^{0}.

We now move to the Poisson geometric properties of the canonical infinitesimal stratification. Notice that, by the definition of
\underset{{\mathrm{inf}}}{\sim}
, the strata are saturated with respect to the symplectic foliation, hence are Poisson submanifolds. As we shall see shortly, they belong to the following very special class of Poisson submanifolds.

Definition 3.17
.

A
core Poisson submanifold
of
(M,\pi)
is a Poisson submanifold
\Sigma
with symplectic foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}
such that:

(i)

\Sigma
is saturated and
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}
is regular;

(ii)

for each
x\in\Sigma
, the canonical map

(3.15)

\mathfrak{g}_{x}=\nu_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\pi})\to\nu_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}),\quad\xi\mapsto\xi|_{T\Sigma},

restricts to an isomorphism

(3.16)

r:\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\nu_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}).

Core Poisson submanifolds allow one to reduce the study of certain invariants of Poisson manifolds to the regular case, where its geometry is more transparent and they become easier to compute.

Theorem 3.18
.

If
(M,\pi)
is a Poisson manifold of (strong)
\mathcal{C}
-type, then so are all the strata of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
. Moreover, every strata is a core Poisson submanifold of
(M,\pi)
.

Before proceeding with the proof, we first recall the following construction of Hamiltonian reduction of symplectic groupoids along Poisson submanifolds.
Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a symplectic groupoid. Its restriction
\mathcal{G}_{N}\rightrightarrows N
to any saturated submanifold
N\subset M
is a coisotropic subgroupoid of
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
and one applies the classical symplectic reduction. Namely, the kernel

\mathfrak{K}:=\Ker(\Omega|_{\mathcal{G}_{N}})\subset T\mathcal{G}_{N}

can be interpreted as a foliation on
\mathcal{G}_{N}
and, if the leaf space

(3.17)

\mathcal{G}_{\textrm{red}}:=\mathcal{G}_{N}/\mathfrak{K}

is a smooth manifold, then
\Omega|_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}
descends to symplectic structure
\Omega_{\textrm{red}}
on
\mathcal{G}_{\textrm{red}}
. In our context, it follows that

\left(\mathcal{G}_{\textrm{red}},\Omega_{\textrm{red}}\right)\rightrightarrows N

is a symplectic groupoid (cf.
[
8
]
). The various groupoids appearing in this reduction are represented by the following diagram:

\textstyle{(\mathcal{G}_{N},\Omega_{N})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{G},\Omega)}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{red}},\Omega_{\mathrm{red}})}

We say
that the saturated submanifold
N
is
suited for smooth reduction of
\mathcal{G}
if the leaf space (
3.17
) is, indeed, a smooth Hausdorff manifold. The resulting symplectic groupoid
\left(\mathcal{G}_{\textrm{red}},\Omega_{\textrm{red}}\right)
will be called the
reduction of
\mathcal{G}
along
N
.
It is clear that, in this case, if
\mathcal{G}
is of
\mathcal{C}
-type, then so is its reduction along
N
.

The foliation
\mathfrak{K}
can be further described by taking advantage of the groupoid context. First of all,
\mathfrak{K}
can be reconstructed from its restriction
\mathfrak{K}|_{N}
to
N
via right (or left) translations. Secondly,
\mathfrak{K}|_{N}
is a smooth sub-bundle of the bundle of isotropy Lie algebras of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
which, via the isomorphism
\textrm{Lie}(\mathcal{G})\simeq T^{*}M
given by
\Omega
, is identified with the Lie algebra bundle

\mathfrak{K}|_{N}\simeq(TN)^{0}\subset T^{*}_{N}M.

Thirdly, each fiber
\mathfrak{K}_{x}
integrates to a connected subgroup
\mathcal{K}_{x}\subset\mathcal{G}_{x}
and one obtains a bundle of groups
\mathcal{K}\to N
that acts on
\mathcal{G}_{N}
.
Finally, the leaf space (
3.17
) can
now be described as the quotient of this action. This leads to the following result.

Lemma 3.19
.

Given a symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
, a saturated submanifold
N\subset M
is suited for smooth reduction of
\mathcal{G}
if and only if the bundle of connected groups
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{G}
integrating
\mathfrak{K}|_{N}=(TN)^{0}
is closed in
\mathcal{G}_{N}
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is of (strong)
\mathcal{C}
-type, then
N
is suited for smooth reduction if and only if each connected subgroup
\mathcal{K}_{x}
is closed in
\mathcal{G}_{x}
, in which case
\mathcal{G}_{\textrm{red}}
is also of (strong)
\mathcal{C}
-type.

Proof.

The first part of the lemma follows from the previous discussion. Assume then that
\mathcal{G}
is of
\mathcal{C}
-type. If all
\mathcal{K}_{x}
are closed, they are also compact since
\mathcal{G}
is proper. It follows that the bundle
\mathcal{K}
is closed in
\mathcal{G}_{N}
.
The reduced groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\textrm{red}}
is proper since it is the restriction of
\mathcal{G}
to the saturated submanifold
N
, followed by the quotient by a bundle of compact Lie groups
\mathcal{K}
. Similarly, the s-fiber of
\mathcal{G}_{\textrm{red}}
at
x
is the quotient of the s-fiber
\mathcal{G}
at
x
by the compact connected subgroup
\mathcal{K}_{x}
. It follows that if
\mathcal{G}
is of (strong)
\mathcal{C}
-type so is
\mathcal{G}_{\textrm{red}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.18
.

Fix a stratum
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
. This is a regular Poisson submanifold since, by the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
, points in
\Sigma
have isomorphic isotropy.
Notice also that, by Corollary
3.15
, the kernel of the canonical map

\mathfrak{g}_{x}=\nu_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\pi})\to\nu_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}),\quad\xi\mapsto\xi|_{T\Sigma},

is precisely
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{ss}}_{x}
. Therefore, the restriction of this map to the center
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})
gives an isomorphism onto
\nu_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma})
, i.e.,
\Sigma
is a core Poisson submanifold.

To prove that
\Sigma
is of (strong)
\mathcal{C}
-type we show that
\Sigma
is suited for smooth reduction of
\mathcal{G}
by appealing to Lemma
3.19
. We are left with proving that for each
x\in\Sigma
the connected Lie group
\mathcal{K}_{x}
integrating
(T\Sigma)^{0}\subset T^{*}_{\Sigma}M
is closed in
\mathcal{G}_{x}
. By (
3.13
)
(T\Sigma)^{0}=\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\textrm{ss}}
and thus its integration
is a closed subgroup of
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}
(see, e.g., pp. 165 in
[
22
]
).

∎

The following result shows that core Poisson submanifolds retain all the information about two important invariants, that we recall after its statement.

Theorem 3.20
.

For any core Poisson submanifold
\Sigma
of a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
:

(i)

the holonomy representations of
(M,\pi)
and
(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma})
at any
x\in\Sigma
are isomorphic, i.e. one has a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\GL(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{r_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(S,x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\Hol}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\Hol}_{\Sigma}}
\textstyle{\GL(\nu_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}))}

where
r
is the isomorphism (
3.16
);

(ii)

the monodromy map of
(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma})
at any
x\in\Sigma
factors through the monodromy map of
(M,\pi)
:

\textstyle{Z(G(\mathfrak{g}_{x}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\pi_{2}(S,x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\partial^{\Sigma}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})}

where we use the isomorphism
\nu_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma})\simeq\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})
given by (
3.16
), and
\Phi
is the restriction to the center of the group morphism
G(\mathfrak{g}_{x})\to\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})
integrating the map (
3.15
) in the core condition.

For a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
its
linear holonomy representation
at
x\in M
is a homomorphism

(3.18)

\rho^{\Hol}:\pi_{1}(S,x)\to\GL(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})),

where
S
is the symplectic leaf containing
x
. It is the parallel transport map for the canonical flat connection on the bundle of centers of the isotropy
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S})\to S
defined by

(3.19)

\nabla:\mathfrak{X}(S)\times\Gamma(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S}))\to\Gamma(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S})),\quad\nabla_{\pi^{\sharp}(\alpha)}\xi=[\alpha,\xi].

In the regular case this is just the Bott connection and (
3.18
) is the dual of the usual linear holomomy representation of a foliation. When
(M,\pi)
is integrable, the linear holonomy also has the following interpretation.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be any symplectic integration of
(M,\pi)
, fix
x\in M
and let
S
be the symplectic leaf containing
x
. Then the linear holonomy representation factors through the adjoint representation of
\mathcal{G}_{x}/\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}
on
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})

\textstyle{\pi_{1}(S,x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\Hol}_{M}}
\textstyle{\GL(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}))}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{x}/\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Ad}

where the vertical arrow arises from the homotopy exact sequence of
\mathbf{t}:\mathbf{s}^{-1}(x)\to S
.

The proof is deferred to the end of the section.

Next we briefly recall the other invariant present in the statement of Theorem
3.20
. For any Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
, its
monodromy map
at
x
is a group homomorphism

(3.20)

\partial_{x}:\pi_{2}(S,x)\to Z(G(\mathfrak{g}_{x})).

In the regular case, this map has a nice geometric interpretation as the variation of symplectic areas of spheres in directions transverse to
S
(see
[
10
]
or
[
13
, Chapter 14]
).
In the non-regular case, assuming that
(M,\pi)
is integrable with the source 1-connected integration denoted
\Sigma=\Sigma(M,\pi)
, then (
3.20
) can be defined as the boundary map in the long homotopy sequence associated to
\mathbf{t}:\mathbf{s}^{-1}(x)\to S
, combined with the canonical inclusion
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{x})
into
Z(G(\mathfrak{g}_{x}))
(see
[
10
]
or
[
13
, Chapter 14]
). We also recall that one defines the
monodromy group
of
(M,\pi)
at
x
as the image of the monodromy map

\mathcal{N}_{x}:=\textrm{Im}(\partial_{x})\subset Z(G(\mathfrak{g}_{x})).

The integrability of a Poisson manifold is controlled by the groups
\mathcal{N}_{x}\cap Z(G(\mathfrak{g}_{x}))^{0}
.

In general, passing to a Poisson submanifold, one loses information about the holonomy and/or the monodromy of the ambient Poisson manifold. Theorem
3.20
asserts that core Poisson submanifolds have the remarkable property of keeping the relevant part of this information and allows to reduce the computation of these groups to the regular case. More precisely, they encode the entire linear holonomy and, since
\Phi
restricts to an isomorphism on
Z(G(\mathfrak{g}_{x}))^{0}
