, they also detect the obstructions to integrability.

Proof of Theorem
3.20
.

The representation (
3.21
) is natural with respect to a surjective morphism
\phi:A\to B
of transitive Lie algebroids over
S
. Similarly, at the groupoid level with the adjoint representation. Upon restriction to the centers, the naturality translates to the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{1}(S,x)\times\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}^{A}_{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\Hol}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{({\mathrm{id}},\phi)}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}^{A}_{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(S,x)\times\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}^{B}_{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\Hol}_{B}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}^{B}_{x})}

A similar naturality argument applies to the monodromy maps of transitive algebroids, and one obtains another commutative diagram

\textstyle{Z(G(\mathfrak{g}^{A}_{x}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\pi_{2}(S,x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{B}}
\textstyle{Z(G(\mathfrak{g}^{B}_{x}))}

where
\Phi:G(\mathfrak{g}^{A}_{x})\to G(\mathfrak{g}^{B}_{x})
is the morphism integrating
\phi
.

The result follows from the previous commutative diagrams applied to the algebroid morphism

T^{*}_{S}M\to T^{*}_{S}\Sigma,

where
S
is a symplectic leaf inside
\Sigma
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.21
.

We start by remarking that the linear holonomy representation (
3.18
) of
(M,\pi)
at
x
depends only on the restricted algebroid
T^{*}_{S}M
, and in fact makes sense for a general transitive Lie algebroid
(A_{S},[\cdot,\cdot]_{A_{S}},\rho_{S})\to S
. For such an algebroid, one has a flat
A_{S}
-connection on the isotropy Lie algebra bundle
\mathfrak{g}_{S}:=\mathrm{Ker}(\rho_{S})
given by

(3.21)

\nabla:\Gamma(A_{S})\times\Gamma(\mathfrak{g}_{S})\to\Gamma(\mathfrak{g}_{S}),\quad\nabla_{\alpha}(\beta)=[\alpha,\beta]_{A_{S}}.

For any source connected Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{S}
with algebroid
A_{S}
, this integrates to the adjoint representation of
\mathcal{G}_{S}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{S}
. Upon restriction to the centers
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S})
, the
A_{S}
-connection factors via the anchor map
\rho_{S}:A_{S}\to TS
to a flat connection

\nabla:\mathfrak{X}(S)\times\Gamma(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S}))\to\Gamma(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S})).

Using this remark, let
\mathcal{G}_{S}
be the restriction of
\mathcal{G}
to
S
and let
\mathcal{I}_{S}
be the bundle of groups consisting of the unit connected components of the isotropy groups of
\mathcal{G}_{S}
. Then
\mathcal{G}_{S}
and
\mathcal{I}_{S}
have Lie algebroids
A_{S}=T^{*}_{S}M
and
\mathfrak{g}_{S}=\Ker(\rho_{S}=\pi^{\sharp}|_{S}:A_{S}\to TS
). The proof is a combination of several ingredients:

1)

The representation
\Ad
from the statement is simply the one induced by the adjoint representation of the Lie group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
on its Lie algebra. The key remark is that the adjoint action makes sense as an action of the entire
\mathcal{G}_{S}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{S}
and, when acting just on
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S})
, it descends to an action

\Ad:\mathcal{G}_{S}/\mathcal{I}_{S}\to GL(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S})).

2)

The infinitesimal counterpart of
\Ad
is the the action of
A_{S}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{S}
induced by the Lie bracket, i.e., the flat
A_{S}
-connection (
3.21
).
As remarked above, upon restriction to the center, it descends to an action of
A_{S}/\mathfrak{g}_{S}
. Under the isomorphism
A_{S}/\mathfrak{g}_{S}\simeq TS
this becomes the flat connection on

\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S})
whose parallel transport defines the representation
\rho^{\Hol}_{M}
. At the global level, one can consider the entire parallel transport as a representation

\rho^{\Hol}_{M}:\Pi(S)\to GL(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S})).

3)

Notice that

\mathcal{G}_{S}/\mathcal{I}_{S}
is a
s
-connected Lie groupoid integrating
A_{S}/\mathfrak{g}_{S}
while
\Pi(S)
is a source 1-connected integration of
TS
. Therefore, we have a groupoid morphism

(3.22)

\Psi:\Pi(S)\to\mathcal{G}_{S}/\mathcal{I}_{S},

integrating the isomorphism
TS\simeq A_{S}/\mathfrak{g}_{S}
. Restricting to the isotropy group at a point
x\in M
provides the desired vertical group homomorphism in the statement. Explicitly, a class
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(S,x)
is sent to the class of
u(1)\in\mathcal{G}_{x}/\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
, where
u:I\to\mathbf{s}^{-1}(x)
is any lift of
\gamma
along the target map starting at the unit. This is precisely the boundary map from the statement.

We now see that the representations
\Ad\circ\Psi
and
\rho^{\Hol}_{M}
have the same linearization and, therefore, they must coincide.
The argument can summarized by the following diagrams

\textstyle{TS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{S}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{gl}(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S}))}
\textstyle{A_{S}/\mathfrak{g}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ad}

\textstyle{\Pi(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\Hol}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\GL(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{S}))}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{S}/\mathcal{I}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Ad}

where the left diagram is the infinitesimal counterpart of the right diagram. At
x
, one obtains the diagram from the statement.
∎

3.3.
The canonical stratification of PMCTs

We now discuss the canonical stratification
\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
, originally introduced in Definition
3.1
.
We will give two analogues of the result we proved for proper foliations, Proposition
3.10
.
The first one is a simple consequence for regular PMCTs of the definition of
\underset{\textrm{hol}}{\sim}
.

Proposition 3.22
.

For any regular
Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)
its canonical stratification
\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
coincides with the canonical stratification of its foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
.

Proof.

We note that we even have an equality at the level of partitions. The linear holonomy of the algebroid
T^{*}M
that is used in the definition of
\underset{\textrm{hol}}{\sim}
is nothing but the (dual of) the linear holonomy of the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
.
∎

The second Poisson geometric analogue of Proposition
3.10
holds for arbitrary PMCTs, also justifying our terminology for
\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
.

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold of proper type. Then for any proper integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
the canonical stratification
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{G}}(M)
coincides with the partition
\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
induced by the equivalence relation
\underset{{\mathrm{hol}}}{\sim}
defined by (
3.4
).

Proof.

Proposition
3.21
implies that the equivalence relation
\underset{\textrm{hol}}{\sim}
agrees with the following one:

x\sim^{\prime}_{\mathcal{G}}y\quad\text{iff}\quad\begin{cases}\mathfrak{g}_{x}\simeq\mathfrak{g}_{y}\\
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\simeq\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{y})^{\mathcal{G}_{y}}.\end{cases}

So we have to prove that
\sim_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\sim^{\prime}_{\mathcal{G}}
produce the same partition of
M
. We do that by an argument similar to the one in the proof of Theorem (
3.13
), making use of
Lemma
3.5
and the normal form of Theorem
2.1
. We are left with proving the analogue of (
3.11
):

V\cap\mathfrak{S}_{0}=V\cap\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}_{0},

Writing out the two sides, we are left with proving that, for
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
:

\xi\in V^{G}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\xi\in V^{\mathfrak{g}}\ \textrm{and}\ \mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})^{G_{\xi}}\simeq\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{G}.

To show this, fix first
\xi
satisfying the conditions in the right-hand side. The first condition guarantees that
\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}=\mathfrak{g}
which, in turn, implies that the second isomorphism is actually an equality. On the other hand, if we choose a
G
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
, then the dual
v_{\xi}\in\mathfrak{g}
of
\xi
is always in

\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})^{G_{\xi}}
and hence it has to be
G
-invariant. Therefore
\xi
is
G
-invariant as well. The opposite implication is clear.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

The previous theorem implies that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{G}}(M)
does not depend on the choice of proper integration of
(M,\pi)
and can be defined in terms of infinitesimal data. This, together with an appropriate modification of the theorem, holds true for proper integrations of arbitrary algebroids.
The more general version of
\underset{\textrm{hol}}{\sim}
is:
x\underset{\textrm{hol}}{\sim}y
if
\mathfrak{g}_{x}\simeq\mathfrak{g}_{y}
and the invariant parts of
\nu^{*}_{x}(\mathcal{O}_{x})
and
\nu^{*}_{y}(\mathcal{O}_{y})
are isomorphic.

The
principal part
of
(M,\pi)
is defined as the regular part of this stratification

M^{{\mathrm{princ}}}:=M^{\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)-{\mathrm{reg}}}.

Also, we will denote by
\mathfrak{S}_{k}(M,\pi)
the union of the codimension
k
strata of
\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
. Similar to Theorem
3.13
for the infinitesimals stratification, we have the following.

Theorem 3.25
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold of proper type. Then the codimension of the strata of
\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
through
x\in M
is precisely the codimension of

\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{\Hol}
in
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
, i.e.,

\mathfrak{S}_{k}(M,\pi)=\left\{x\in M:\codim_{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{\Hol})=k\right\}.

In particular,
M^{{\mathrm{princ}}}
consists of those points
x\in M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
with the property that the linear holonomy representation
\rho^{\Hol}_{M}:\pi_{1}(S_{x})\to\GL(\nu_{x})
is trivial.

Notice however that, unlike the case of the infinitesimal stratification, the canonical stratification of a Poisson manifold of proper type may have codimension one strata. See Example
3.27
.

Proof.

Notice that, by Proposition
3.21
,
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{\Hol}=\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
so the codimension partition is invariant under Morita equivalences. Therefore we can apply again the same type of argument used in the proofs of Theorem (
3.13
) and Proposition
3.23
.
We will be left with proving that:

\xi\in(\mathfrak{g}^{*})^{G}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad{\codim}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}}\left(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})^{G_{\xi}}\right)={\codim}_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\right).

Notice that the codimension of
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})^{G_{\xi}}
in
\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}
can can only increase when moving away from
0\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Hence, if
\xi
satisfies the conditions on the right hand side, then
\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}=\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})^{G_{\xi}}=\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
, from which it follows that
\xi\in(\mathfrak{g}^{*})^{G}
. The opposite implication is clear.
∎

Similar to Corollary
3.15
we have:

Corollary 3.26
.

For any stratum
\mathfrak{S}
of the canonical stratification of a Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)

and any
x\in\Sigma
one has:

(3.23)

(T_{x}\mathfrak{S})^{0}=((\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*})^{\mathcal{G}_{x}})^{0}=\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{ss}}_{x}\oplus\mathrm{Coinv}_{\pi_{1}(S_{x})}(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})).

In particular,

(3.24)

T_{x}\mathfrak{S}/T_{x}S_{x}=(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{*})^{\mathcal{G}_{x}/\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}}.

Example 3.27
.

Similarly to what happens for proper Lie group actions, the canonical stratification of PMCTs may have codimension one strata. For example, let
(S,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold with a connected double cover
q:\widetilde{S}\to S
. Also, let
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
act on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
by inversion, so we have the principal
\mathbb{S}^{1}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-bundle

P:=\widetilde{S}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathbb{S}^{1}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to S.

The gauge construction for
P
and
(S,\omega)
produces a Poisson manifold of proper type (see
[
11
, Section 5.8]
)

M_{\mathrm{lin}}=:\widetilde{S}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathbb{R},

with Poisson structure
\pi_{\mathrm{lin}}
induced by the leafwise symplectic form on
\widetilde{S}\times\mathbb{R}
.
The coadjoint action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
is by reflection and it follows that
\mathcal{S}(M_{\mathrm{lin}},\pi_{\mathrm{lin}})
has the codimension one strata

\Sigma_{1}=\widetilde{S}\times\{0\}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\equiv S.

Next, we discuss the interaction between the canonical and the infinitesimal strata.

Theorem 3.28
.

For any Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)
the canonical stratification
\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
is a refinement of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
. Its restriction to a stratum
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
coincides with the canonical stratification of
(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma})
, i.e.,

\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)|_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{S}(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma}).

Furthermore, the principal part of
(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma})
consists of those points
x\in\Sigma
for which the representation
\rho^{\Hol}_{M}:\pi_{1}(S_{x})\to\GL(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}))
is trivial.

Proof.

If
\Sigma\subset M
is a stratum of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
, since
(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma})
is a core Poisson submanifold, by Theorem
3.20
its linear holonomy coincides with the linear holonomy of
(M,\pi)
. This implies the statement in the theorem.
The last part also follows from the fact that
\Sigma
is a core Poisson submanifold and from the description in (
3.14
)
of
\nu_{\Sigma}(S_{x})
.
∎

Next, we have the analogue of Theorem
3.18
:

Theorem 3.29
.

If
(M,\pi)
is a Poisson manifold of proper type each strata of
\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
is a saturated, regular, Poisson submanifold of
(M,\pi)
, of the same (strong)
\mathcal{C}
-type as
(M,\pi)
.

Proof.

A stratum
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
is saturated by symplectic leaves and therefore it is a Poisson submanifold of
(M,\pi)
. It is regular because it is a Poisson submanifold of a regular Poisson manifold
(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma})
, for some
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be an integration of
(M,\pi)
of
\mathcal{C}
-type and fix
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathcal{S}(M,\pi)
. We have
\mathfrak{S}\subset\Sigma
for some
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi)
, and we already know that
\Sigma
is suited for smooth reduction of
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
. So we may assume without loss of generality that
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is an integration of
\mathcal{C}
-type of the regular Poisson manifold
(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma})
. We make use of Lemma
3.19
and of the notations therein. We need to show that
\mathfrak{S}
is suited for smooth reduction of
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
, i.e., that the integration
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{G}
of the bundle of abelian subalgebras

(T\mathfrak{S})^{0}\subset T^{*}\Sigma

is a closed subgroupoid.

The bundle of groups
\mathcal{K}
is contained in the connected component of the isotropy
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows\Sigma
which, since
(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma})
is regular, is a bundle of tori with fibers

\mathcal{T}_{x}=\nu^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma})_{x}/\Lambda_{x}.

Here
\Lambda
denotes the transverse integral affine structure associated to the regular Poisson manifold of proper type
(\Sigma,\pi_{\Sigma})

[
12
, Section 3.3]
, i.e., the kernel of the exponential map

\exp:\mathfrak{g}_{x}=\nu_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{G}_{x}.

To show that
\mathcal{K}
is closed it is enough to show that its fibers are subtori of the fibers of
\mathcal{T}
, i.e., that each
(T_{x}\mathfrak{S})^{0}
is an integral affine subspace of
(\mathfrak{g}_{x},\Lambda_{x})
. It follows from (
3.23
) that we have a canonical
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-invariant splitting

\mathfrak{g}_{x}=\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\oplus(T_{x}\mathfrak{S})^{0}.

Since
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is abelian, the action of the connected component
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
is trivial and we obtain a
\mathcal{G}_{x}/\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
-invariant splitting

\mathfrak{g}_{x}=\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\mathcal{G}_{x}/\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}}\oplus(T_{x}\mathfrak{S})^{0}.

Observing that the action of
\mathcal{G}_{x}/\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is by integral affine transformations,
to conclude our proof it suffices to invoke the following lemma.

Lemma 3.30
.

Let
V
be an integral affine vector space and let
\Gamma
be a finite group acting by integral affine transformations on
V
. Then there is a canonical
\Gamma
-invariant splitting by integral affine subspaces

V=V^{\Gamma}\oplus((V^{*})^{\Gamma})^{0}.

Proof of the Lemma.

Let
T:V\to V
be the linear integral affine map

T(v):=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\gamma\cdot v.

This map preserves the fixed point
V^{\Gamma}
, has image
V^{\Gamma}
and kernel
((V^{*})^{\Gamma})^{0}
, so the lemma follows.
∎

∎

3.4.
Canonical stratifications of DMCTs

Twisted Dirac manifolds of
\mathcal{C}
-type also have canonical stratifications, with the same features as in the Poisson case. Namely, Theorems
3.13
and
3.25
, as well as Corollaries
3.15
and
3.26
, hold as stated for (possibly twisted) DMCTs. This follows because the Hamiltonian normal form (cf. Theorem
2.5
and Remark
2.9
) still gives, around each leaf, a Morita equivalence with a
G
-invariant neighborhood of 0 in the dual of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

The definition of core submanifold
\Sigma
of a twisted Dirac manifold
(M,L)
(cf. Definition
3.17
) is the same, since the conormal bundle still coincides with the isotropy of the Dirac structure. Also, Theorems
3.18
and
3.29
still hold, because:

(i)

The notion of saturated submanifold
i:N\hookrightarrow M
suited for smooth reduction in the Dirac setting, as well as Lemma
3.19
, carry through. To that end, one sets
\mathfrak{K}:=\Ker(\Omega|_{\mathcal{G}_{N}})\cap\Ker(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s})\cap\Ker(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t})
. Under the isomorphism
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathcal{G})\simeq L
we still have
\mathfrak{K}|_{N}=(TN)^{0}
;

(ii)

Theorem
3.20
still holds. In its proof, one replaces the algebroid morphism between cotangent Lie algebroids by the analogous one for twisted Dirac structures, namely
L|_{S}\to(i^{*}L)|_{S}
, where
i^{*}L
denotes the pullback Dirac structure to
N
.

From these it follows also that the relationship between the canonical and (i) the canonical infinitesimal stratification (Theorem
3.28
) and (ii) the holonomy stratification of its characteristic foliation in the regular case (Proposition
3.23
), also hold in the twisted Dirac setting.

Finally, Proposition
3.21
also holds for twisted presymplectic groupoids, as can easily be seen from its proof.

Remark 3.31
.

The concept of submanifold suited for smooth reduction has an interesting interpretation in the case of presymplectic torus bundles
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})\rightrightarrows M
. By
[
12
, Proposition 3.2.8]
, such bundles encode transverse integral affine structures
(\mathcal{F},\Lambda)
on
M
. Using the same arguments as in
[
12
]
, it follows that a submanifold
N\subset M
is suited for smooth reduction of
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
if and only if it is a transverse integral affine submanifold of
(\mathcal{F},\Lambda)
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is a proper, regular, symplectic groupoid with associated short exact sequence

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1.}

Then one checks that given a saturated submanifold
N\subset M
, the symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is suited for reduction along
N
if and only if the presymplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
is suited for reduction along
N
.

4.
The Weyl Resolution

In this section we show that any Poisson manifold of proper type can be “desingularized”, in the sense that the non-regular locus can be eliminated. Such a resolution of singularities will force us to consider the more general setting of Dirac geometry. However, these Dirac resolutions will still be Poisson on a dense open subspace and of proper type. For simplicity of the presentation, we will assume that the Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
is connected. For a general Poisson manifold one may apply these results to each component.

The definition of the resolution can be given straight away as follows.

Definition 4.1
.

The Weyl resolution of a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
is defined as

\widehat{M}:=\{(x,\mathfrak{t})\,|\,x\in M,\,\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{x}\,\textrm{maximal\ torus}\},

together with the map

{\mathscr{R}}:\widehat{M}\to M,\quad\ {\mathscr{R}}(x,\mathfrak{t})=x.

If
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
is a symplectic groupoid integrating
(M,\pi)
then it acts naturally on the the Weyl resolution along the resolution map

(4.1)

g\cdot(x,\mathfrak{t})=(y,\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{t})),

where
g
is an arrow from
x
to
y
and
\Ad_{g}
is the differential of conjugation by
g
.

For a general Poisson manifold,
\widehat{M}
may even fail to be smooth and the Weyl resolution can be of little use. However, for PMCTs the situation is quite different and in this section we will discuss many nice properties of the resolution for these Poisson manifolds. For example, the pullback by
{\mathscr{R}}
of
\pi
, as a Dirac structure, is a Dirac structure

(4.2)

\widehat{L}_{\pi}:={\mathscr{R}}^{*}L_{\pi}.

We will denote the associated presymplectic foliation by

(4.3)

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}:={\mathrm{pr}}_{T\widehat{M}}(\widehat{L}_{\pi}).

Its leaves are the preimages by
{\mathscr{R}}
of the leaves of the symplectic foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
. We have the following result which summarizes the contents of this section:

Theorem 4.2
.

For any Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)
:

(i)

(\widehat{M},\widehat{L}_{\pi})
is a regular Dirac manifold of proper type, and its Poisson locus is precisely the preimage
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(M^{{\mathrm{reg}}})
of the regular part of
(M,\pi)
;

(ii)

{\mathscr{R}}
is a forward Dirac map which restricts to a Poisson diffeomorphism from
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(M^{{\mathrm{reg}}})
to
M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
;

(iii)

{\mathscr{R}}
descends to a homemorphism between the leaf spaces of
(\widehat{M},\widehat{L}_{\pi})
and
(M,\pi)
.

(iv)

For any proper integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows(M,\pi)
, the action groupoid and the pullback of
\Omega

\widehat{\mathcal{G}}:=\mathcal{G}\ltimes\widehat{M}\rightrightarrows\widehat{M},\quad\widehat{\Omega}:={\mathrm{pr}}_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\Omega,

is a proper presymplectic integration of
(\widehat{M},\widehat{L}_{\pi})
.

If
(M,\pi)
is a Poisson manifold of proper type, then Theorem
2.1
implies that the rank of the isotropy Lie algebras is locally the rank of the isotropy Lie algebras of the coadjoint action, which is constant. Hence, since we assume
M
to be connected, the common rank of the isotropy Lie algebras equals codimension of a regular leaf, which is usually called the
corank
of
(M,\pi)
. Denoting this corank by
r
, the Weyl resolution sits canonically inside the Grassmann bundle:

\widehat{M}\subset{\mathrm{Gr}}_{r}(T^{*}M).

In particular
\widehat{M}
comes equipped with a natural topology. In fact,
\widehat{M}
will turn out to be a closed submanifold of the Grassmannian.

Example 4.3

(The case
M=\mathfrak{g}^{*}
)
.

Let us consider the linear Poisson manifold
M=\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, where
\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie algebra of compact type. Denote

(4.4)

\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}):=\{\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{t}\ \textrm{is\ a\ maximal\ torus}\}\subset{\mathrm{Gr}}_{r}(\mathfrak{g}),

where
r
is the rank of
\mathfrak{g}
. The standard decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{t}]
allows one to write

\mathfrak{t}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}

Using this inclusion we find
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})=\{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}):\xi\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}\}
.
Hence, the Weyl resolution is

(4.5)

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}=\{(\xi,\mathfrak{t}):\mathfrak{t}\in\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}),\xi\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}\}.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group integrating
\mathfrak{g}
and fix a maximal torus
T\subset G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}
. Then all maximal tori in
\mathfrak{g}
are conjugate to
\mathfrak{t}
so we have an isomorphism

(4.6)

G/N(T)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}),\quad g\mapsto\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{t}),

where
N(T)
is the normalizer of
T
in
G
. Using (
4.5
) we obtain a bijection

(4.7)

\phi:(G\times\mathfrak{t}^{*})/N(T)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{\mathfrak{g}^{*}},\quad(gT,\xi)\mapsto(\Ad_{g}^{*}(\xi),\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{t})).

Note that we also have a natural identification

(G\times\mathfrak{t}^{*})/N(T)\simeq G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*},

where
W=N(T)/T
is the Weyl group of
G
relative to
T
. Viewing
\phi
as a map into
{\mathrm{Gr}}_{r}(T^{*}\mathfrak{g}^{*})=\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}_{r}(\mathfrak{g})
, one checks that the first component is proper and
\phi
is an immersion. It follows that
\phi
is a proper immersion or, equivalently, a closed embedding. We conclude that
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
is a submanifold of
{\mathrm{Gr}}_{r}(T^{*}\mathfrak{g}^{*})
.

Notice that in the model given by (
4.7
) the resolution map is simply:

(4.8)

{\mathscr{R}}:G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*},\quad(gT,\xi)\mapsto\Ad_{g}^{*}(\xi),

and this maps the
W
-classes with representatives in
G/T\times\{\xi\}
to the coadjoint orbit through
\xi
. In other words, the symplectic foliation of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is resolved into a regular foliation. If we pullback the symplectic form on the coadjoint orbit through
\xi
, we obtain a
G
-invariant closed form
\omega_{\xi}
on
G/T\times\{\xi\}
which at the point
[(T,\xi)]
is given by the familiar formula

\omega_{\xi}((u,0),(v,0))=\xi([u,v]).

These leafwise presymplectic forms descend to
W
-classes and together define the Dirac structure
\widehat{L}_{\pi}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
given by (
4.2
).

For later use, notice that we have not assumed
G
to be connected. The construction of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
does not depend on the choice of Lie group integrating
\mathfrak{g}
. We have a short exact sequence

(4.9)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G/G^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

where
W^{0}=(N(T)\cap G^{0})/T
, so the we can replace
G
by
G^{0}
in the construction above. This agrees with the fact that Definition
4.1
only uses infinitesimal data.

4.1.
Smoothness of the resolution

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold of proper type of corank
r
. Then
\widehat{M}
is a closed embedded submanifold of
{\mathrm{Gr}}_{r}(T^{*}M)
and
{\mathscr{R}}:\widehat{M}\to M
is a proper, smooth surjection with connected fibers. Moreover, for any integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
of
(M,\pi)
the action (
4.1
) is smooth and its orbit foliation is regular of codimension
\operatorname{corank}\pi
.

Proof.

One can cover
M
by saturated open sets where the Hamiltonian local model holds and it is enough to prove the various statements on each such domain. So we assume that
(M,\pi)=(Q/G,\pi_{\mathrm{red}})
, where
\mu:(Q,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is as in Theorem
2.1
. We denote by

\mathfrak{p}:Q\to M

the canonical projection, we pull back along
\mathfrak{p}
the map
\widehat{M}\to{\mathrm{Gr}}_{r}(T^{*}M)
covering the identity of
M
, and we consider the following diagram:

\textstyle{Q\mathchoice{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\mu^{*}}

where for
(q,x,\mathfrak{t})\in Q\mathchoice{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}\widehat{M}
the remaining maps behave as follows:

\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}^{*}}

Here

(4.10)

\phi_{q}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mu(q)}\to\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{p}(q)}^{M}

is the differential at unit of the map
\psi_{q}
from Remark
2.2
.
In the diagram above, the top horizontal arrow is a bijection, inducing a smooth structure on
Q\mathchoice{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}\widehat{M}
. The solid arrows represent proper embeddings, and therefore it follows in sequence that the dash arrows are proper embeddings.

Now consider the diagram

\textstyle{Q\mathchoice{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}{\times^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.3764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.2879pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.77707pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{M}}}}\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
