3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\mathcal{G}(\Sigma),\Omega(\Sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 226.62515pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 226.62515pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}({\Sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 278.70853pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 278.70853pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

(b)

and, if
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathcal{S}(M)
with
\mathfrak{S}\subset\Sigma
, then
(\mathcal{G}(\Sigma),\Omega(\Sigma))
is suited for smooth reduction along
\mathfrak{S}
, resulting in

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left(\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S}),\Omega(\mathfrak{S})|_{\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S})}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 134.70844pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 134.70844pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left(\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{S}),\Omega(\mathfrak{S})\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 218.66684pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 218.66684pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}({\mathfrak{S}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 269.08356pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 269.08356pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
\mathcal{B}({\mathfrak{S}})=\mathcal{B}({\Sigma})|_{\mathfrak{S}}
. Also, this coincides with the reduction of
\mathcal{G}
along
\mathfrak{S}
.

Therefore, by Remark
3.31
, the transverse integral affine foliation
(\Sigma,\mathcal{F}_{\pi}|_{\Sigma})
contains
\mathfrak{S}
as a transverse integral affine submanifold.

To prove item (i) of Proposition
5.11
we have to compare
\mathcal{B}({\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}})
and
\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{S})
, and, similarly,
\mathcal{B}({\widehat{\Sigma}})
and
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
. This follows from two general remarks concerning the orbifold atlas
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})
on the orbit space
M/\mathcal{G}
of a proper regular Lie groupoid with associated exact sequence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.34726pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.34726pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.19449pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 120.19449pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 172.90286pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 172.90286pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Namely:

1)

For any two such proper regular groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightrightarrows M
, if there is a submersive groupoid morphism
\mathcal{G}_{1}\to\mathcal{G}_{2}
covering the identity, then the orbifold structure
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
is an isotropy reduction of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G}_{1})
;

2)

Secondly, if a proper regular groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
acts transitively along a submersion
\widehat{M}\to M
with connected fibers, then the orbit space of
\mathcal{G}\ltimes\widehat{M}\rightrightarrows\widehat{M}
can be identified with
M/\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})
is an isotropy reduction of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G}\ltimes\widehat{M})
.

Finally,to complete the proof, item (iii) of Proposition
5.11
follows from Proposition
3.22
and item (ii).
∎

Remark 5.13
.

Item (iii) in Proposition
5.11
is a special case of a general fact for proper Lie groupoids. Namely,
let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
be a proper Lie groupoid with leaf space
B
. Then each member of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{G}}(B)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}_{\mathcal{G}}(B)
inherits a natural orbifold structure from
\mathcal{G}
: for each such member
N\subset M
, the restriction
\mathcal{G}|_{N}
is a regular proper groupoid and its effectivization defines an orbifold atlas for the leaf space of
N
(see, e.g.,
[
14
,
32
]
). Now, given
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M)
, one finds that the restriction
\mathcal{S}(B)|_{B_{\Sigma}}
is the orbifold stratification of
B_{\Sigma}
. Moreover, the members of
\mathcal{S}(B)|_{B_{\Sigma}}
– namely
B_{\mathfrak{S}}
for
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathcal{S}(M)
with
\mathfrak{S}\subset\Sigma
– are embedded suborbifolds of smooth type.

5.5.
Coverings and the main diagram

In this section we collect from
[
12
]
various facts about coverings of the resolution and of the leaf space of a PMCT, which arise from the integral affine geometry. This will be heavily used in the remainder of this paper and can be summarized in the following result.

Proposition 5.14

(The main diagram)
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold with a proper integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows(M,\pi)
and fix a base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}=(x_{0},\mathfrak{t}_{0})\in\widehat{M}
. The resolution map extends to a commutative diagram

(5.5)

\textstyle{{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B=\widehat{M}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}}

with the following properties:

(i)

In the top row the spaces are covering spaces of
\widehat{M}
. In particular, they inherit pullback Dirac structures from
\widehat{L}_{\pi}
.

(ii)

In the bottom row
B^{\mathrm{lin}}
is an
integral affine smooth manifold, while
B^{\mathrm{aff}}
and
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
are covering spaces of
B^{\mathrm{lin}}
, with
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
simply connected. So they are integral affine smooth manifolds as well.

(iii)

The fibers of the vertical rows are the presymplectic leaves of the pullback Dirac structures.

Remark 5.15
.

(a)

The spaces in the diagram depend on the choice of proper integration.

(b)

We will see in Section
6.1
that the bottom row of the diagram consists of orbifold covering spaces of
B
, viewed as a classical orbifold. In particular,
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is the orbifold universal covering space of
B
, so
B
is a good orbifold.

In the sequel we will explain the spaces in the diagram and how this proposition follows from the results of
[
12
, Appendix B]
. We will make use of the tautological bundle

\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}\to\widehat{M},\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}_{(x,\mathfrak{t})}:=\mathfrak{t}.

Notice that this provides a concrete description of the normal bundle to the foliation
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
since we have a canonical identification

\nu(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\cong\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}.

We recall that this bundle carries an integral affine structure
\Lambda\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}
induced from
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\widehat{\Omega})
, the dual of the lattice
\Ker(\exp:\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{G}})
. We will denote by

\GL_{\Lambda}(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*})\rightrightarrows\widehat{M},

the transitive groupoid whose arrows are the invertible integral affine transformations between the fibers of
\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}
.

5.5.1.
The space
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{lin}}

The bundle
\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}
carries a canonical flat connection, uniquely determined by the condition that the local sections of
\Lambda
are flat. The corresponding parallel transport gives rise to the
linear holonomy action
of
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}
denoted

(5.6)

h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}:\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\to\GL_{\Lambda}(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}\,).

It is proved in
[
12
, Appendix B]
that its image is an embedded subgroupoid

\Pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}(\widehat{M})\subset\GL_{\Lambda}(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}\,).

Since
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
and
\Pi^{{\mathrm{lin}}}_{1}(\widehat{M})
are transitive, these groupoids are encoded in their isotropy groups at the base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
, acting on
the s-fiber
\mathbf{s}^{-1}({\widehat{x}}_{0})
. For
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
we obtain the fundamental group at
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
and the universal cover of
\widehat{M}

\pi_{1}(\widehat{M}):=\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}}_{0}),\quad\widetilde{\widehat{M}}:=\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})({\widehat{x}}_{0},-).

For
\Pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}(\widehat{M})
, notice that (
5.6
) induces at
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
a group homomorphism

(5.7)

h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}_{{\widehat{x}}_{0}}:\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\to\GL_{\Lambda_{0}}(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0}).

The kernel and image of this homomorphism will be denoted

K^{{\mathrm{lin}}}=\Ker(h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}_{{\widehat{x}}_{0}})\subset\pi_{1}(\widehat{M}),\quad\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}={\mathrm{Im}}(h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}_{{\widehat{x}}_{0}})\subset\GL_{\Lambda_{0}}(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0}).

The group
\Gamma^{\mathrm{lin}}
is precisely the isotropy group of
\Pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}(\widehat{M})
at
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
, while its
s
-fiber at
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
can be canonically identified with

(5.8)

\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}=\widetilde{\widehat{M}}/K^{{\mathrm{lin}}},

i.e., the covering space of
\widehat{M}
with
\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
as group of deck transformations. We call
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
the
linear holonomy cover
of
\widehat{M}
.

5.5.2.
The space
B^{\mathrm{lin}}

The presymplectic foliation
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
of the Dirac structure
\widehat{L}_{\pi}
is a proper foliation
\widehat{M}
. Hence, its holonomy and linear holonomy groupoids coincide. Moreover, the Bott connection of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
coincides with the restriction of the flat connection on
\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}
, and one has a groupoid embedding (see
[
12
, Appendix B]
)

\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\hookrightarrow\GL_{\Lambda}(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}\,),

whose image is contained in
\Pi^{{\mathrm{lin}}}_{1}(\widehat{M})
.
The holonomy and the monodromy groupoids of the foliation
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
then fit into a diagram

\textstyle{\Mon(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{hol}}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}}
\textstyle{\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}}
\textstyle{\Pi^{{\mathrm{lin}}}_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\GL_{\Lambda}(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}\,)}

where
i_{*}:\Mon(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\to\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
is the map induced by inclusion. Fixing the base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
, one obtains a similar diagram for the isotropy groups

\textstyle{\pi_{1}(S_{{\widehat{x}}_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{hol}}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}}
\textstyle{\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})_{{\widehat{x}}_{0}}\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\GL_{\Lambda_{0}}(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0})}

Pulling back the Dirac structure
\widehat{L}_{\pi}
along the covering (
5.8
) yields a presymplectic foliation
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
so one has a covering of foliated spaces

(\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{{\mathrm{lin}}})\to(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}).

The remarkable property of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{lin}}
proved in
[
12
, Appendix B]
is that it is simple and its leaf space

B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}:=\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}

is a smooth integral affine manifold. Moreover, the action of
\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
on
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
descends to a proper action on

B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
by integral affine transformations and
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
yields a Morita equivalence of Lie groupoids

(5.9)

\textstyle{\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\rtimes\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}}
\textstyle{B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}}

One obtains an isomorphism of classical integral affine orbifolds

B\cong\widehat{M}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}\cong B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}/\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}.

At this point we have obtained the 2 right squares in the main diagram (
5.5
).

5.5.3.
The spaces
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{aff}}
and
B^{\mathrm{aff}}

The affine part of the diagram arises from the developing map associated to the transverse integral affine structure on
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
, denoted

(5.10)

\dev:\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\to\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}.

This is the groupoid cocycle that integrates the projection on the normal bundle to
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
,
T\widehat{M}\to\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}
, interpreted as an algebroid 1-cocycle. Here we are using the representation given by the linear holonomy action (
5.6
).

The developing map together with the linear holonomy action give rise to the
affine holonomy action
where an element
[\gamma]\in\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
acts by the affine transformation

h^{\mathrm{aff}}([\gamma]):\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}_{\gamma(0)}\to\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}_{\gamma(1)},\quad v\mapsto h^{\mathrm{lin}}([\gamma])(v)+\dev([\gamma]).

As shown in
[
12
, Appendix B]
, the affine holonomy action gives an embedding of Lie groupoids

(5.11)

h^{{\mathrm{aff}}}:\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{Aff}}_{\Lambda}(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}\,).

Its image
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\subset{\mathrm{Aff}}_{\Lambda}(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}\,)
fits into a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\GL_{\Lambda}(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}\,)}
\textstyle{\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\overset{i_{*}}{\longrightarrow}\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}
\scriptstyle{h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{Aff}}_{\Lambda}(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}\,)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Fixing the base point, ones obtains a corresponding diagram of isotropy groups, as in Section
5.5.1
, giving rise to the groups
\Gamma^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
and
K^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
as in the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{K^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\GL_{\Lambda_{0}}(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0})}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}}
\scriptstyle{h^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}
\textstyle{K^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{Aff}}_{\Lambda_{0}}(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Also, we can identify the s-fiber of
\Pi(\widehat{M})^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
with the covering space of
\widehat{M}
with
\Gamma^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
as group of deck transformations, namely

(5.12)

\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}=\widetilde{\widehat{M}}/K^{{\mathrm{aff}}},

We call
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
the
affine holonomy cover
of
\widehat{M}
. Pulling back the Dirac structure
\widehat{L}_{\pi}
along the covering map yields a presymplectic foliation
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
so one has sequence of foliated coverings

(\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{{\mathrm{aff}}})\to(\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{{\mathrm{lin}}})\to(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}).

The foliation
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{aff}}
is simple and its leaf space

B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}:=\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}

is a smooth integral affine manifold. Moreover, the action of
\Gamma^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
on
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
descends to a proper action on
B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
by integral affine transformations and
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
yields another Morita equivalence of Lie groupoids

(5.13)

\textstyle{\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\rtimes\Gamma^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}}
\textstyle{B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}

where the left action is by translations via
h^{\mathrm{aff}}\circ i_{*}:\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\to\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{aff}}(\widehat{M})
. This gives one further isomorphism of classical integral affine orbifolds

B\cong\widehat{M}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}\cong B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}/\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\cong B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}/\Gamma^{{\mathrm{aff}}}.

Altogether, we obtain the 3 right squares in the main diagram (
5.5
).

5.5.4.
The spaces
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{orb}}
and
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}

Finally, there is the left-hand square of the main diagram. For that, we let
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
be the universal covering space of
B^{\mathrm{aff}}
(which is also the universal covering space of
B^{\mathrm{lin}}
). Then we can pullback the submersion
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{aff}}\to B^{\mathrm{aff}}
along the covering projection, obtaining
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{orb}}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
. Noticing that the square involving
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
and
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
is also a pull-back diagram, we have

\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{orb}}=\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\times_{B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}=\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\times_{B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}}\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}.

With this definition the left-hand square of the main diagram is commutative, and the induced map
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{orb}}\to\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{aff}}
is a cover.

Furthermore, the principal actions of
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
in either
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
or
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
, give a principal action of
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
on
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{orb}}
whose quotient is
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
, so we have a Morita equivalence

(5.14)

\textstyle{\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{?\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}}

The groupoid on the right-hand side, apriori, is the gauge groupoid. We will see later that it can be identified with an action groupoid
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ltimes\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\rightrightarrows\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
, where
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
is the orbifold fundamental group of
B
(viewed as a classical orbifold).

5.5.5.
The developing map

For later use, we observe that developing map (
5.10
) factors through the groupoid morphism
h^{\mathrm{aff}}:\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\to\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{aff}}(\widehat{M})
. We will denote by the same letter the resulting groupoid cocycle

\dev:\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{aff}}(\widehat{M})\to\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}.

The restriction of this cocycle to the source fiber of the base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
, gives the
developing map based at
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
denoted

\dev_{0}:\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0}.

This map is constant along the leaves of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}^{\mathrm{aff}}
, and the resulting map

\dev_{0}:B^{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0},

is an integral affine local diffeomorphism.

Definition 5.16
.

A Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
of proper type is called
complete
if its developing map
\dev_{0}:B^{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0}
is a global diffeomorphism.

This definition is equivalent to say that the canonical flat connection on
B^{\mathrm{lin}}
(or any of its covering spaces) is complete. In the
\mathbf{s}
-proper case, it is equivalent to say that the canonical flat connection on the normal bundle to the foliation on
\widehat{M}
(or any of its covering spaces) is complete.

5.6.
The main diagram for the Hamiltonian local model

As in the local model for a Poisson manifold of proper type, we let
\mu:(Q,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a
G
-Hamiltonian space for a free and proper
G
-action, where
\mu
has connected fibers, we let
V=\mu(Q)
and we consider the quotient

M:=Q/G,

equipped with the quotient Poisson structure
\pi
. This Poisson manifold has proper integration
\mathcal{G}=(Q\times_{V}Q)/G\rightrightarrows M
and fixing a maximal torus
T\subset G
we claim that the main diagram in this case becomes

(5.15)

\textstyle{{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}=\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu^{-1}(\mathfrak{t}^{*})/Z_{G}(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu^{-1}(\mathfrak{t}^{*})/N(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{Q/G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{t}^{*}\cap V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{t}^{*}\cap V)/W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\cap V)/G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}=B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B=\widehat{M}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}}

Here
W=N(T)/T
and
Z_{G}(T)\subset N(T)
are the elements in the normalizer with trivial coadjoint action on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
and we also claim that

\Gamma^{\mathrm{aff}}=\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\cong N(T)/Z_{G}(T).

To show that these claims hold we fix as base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}=(x_{0},\mathfrak{t})
where
x_{0}\in M
is any point that is
Q
-related to
0\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. We also use the identification
\widehat{M}=\mu^{-1}(\mathfrak{t}^{*})/N(T)
(see (
4.14
)). We will first compute the representation (
5.6
)

h^{\mathrm{lin}}:=h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}_{{\widehat{x}}_{0}}:\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\to\GL(\mathfrak{t}^{*}).

The flat bundle
\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}
whose holonomy was used to define this representation is easier to describe after pulling it along the cover

\overline{M}:=\mu^{-1}(\mathfrak{t}^{*})/T\to\mu^{-1}(\mathfrak{t}^{*})/N(T).

Indeed, the foliation
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
now becomes the one associated to the submersion
\overline{M}\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
induced by
\mu
, so the normal bundle becomes the trivial bundle with fiber
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
and the connection becomes the flat one.
Conversely,
\widehat{M}
is the quotient of the action of
W
on
\overline{M}
and this group also acts on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
via the standard representation

(5.16)

W\to\GL(\mathfrak{t}^{*}).

It follows that
\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}=(\overline{M}\times\mathfrak{t}^{*})/W
and that
h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
is the composition of (
5.16
) with the boundary map

(5.17)

\partial:\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\to W

associated to the homotopy sequence for
\overline{M}\to\widehat{M}
. Since the image of (
5.16
) is precisely
\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
and its kernel is
Z_{G}(T)/T
, we deduce that

\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\cong N(T)/Z_{G}(T).

This also implies that the holonomy cover is
precisely the quotient of
\overline{M}
modulo the kernel

Z_{G}(T)/T
, i.e.,

\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}=\mu^{-1}(\mathfrak{t}^{*})/Z_{G}(T).

One obtains also
B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}=\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. Since this space is 1-connected, it follows that
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=B^{\mathrm{aff}}=B^{\mathrm{lin}}
and then that
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{orb}}=\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{aff}}=\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{lin}}
, which proves all the claims.

For the sequel, we will need to restrict to an invariant open subset
U\subset M
. This will allow, for example, the reduction of fact-checking for a general Poisson manifold to the Hamiltonian local model. The main diagram is functorial for such restrictions.

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold with a choice of proper integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
. Let
U\subset M
be an invariant open subset and consider the restriction
(\mathcal{G}|_{U},\Omega|_{U})\rightrightarrows U
. Fixing a base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}=(x_{0},\mathfrak{t})
with
x_{0}\in U
, the corresponding main diagrams (
5.5
) are related by

(5.18)

\textstyle{\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{orb}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{U}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{U}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{aff}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{lin}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{aff}}_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{lin}}_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{aff}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{lin}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}

where the diagonal maps are open embeddings, except possibly for the ones on the left face of the diagram. The map
\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{orb}}\to\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{orb}}
is an open embedding if
B^{\mathrm{aff}}_{U}\to B^{\mathrm{aff}}
is one.

Proof.

The construction of the resolution shows that we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\widehat{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{M}

where the left vertical arrow is an open embedding. The diagonal maps in (
5.18
), with the exception of the ones on the left face of the diagram, arise from the identification of
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
as the s-fiber of
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
of the image
\Pi^{{\mathrm{lin}}}_{1}(\widehat{M})\subset\GL(\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}^{*})
of the linear holonomy map (
5.6
). The bottom diagonal map on the left face of the diagram is the map induced on universal covering spaces from the embedding
B_{U}^{\mathrm{aff}}\to B^{\mathrm{aff}}
, while the first top diagonal map is the one induced between fiber products

\widehat{U}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{U}\times_{B_{U}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}\widehat{U}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\to{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\times_{B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}.

This map is an embedding if the map
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{U}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is an embedding.
∎

6.
The Weyl Group

In this section we analyse further the structure of the leaf space of a PMCT
(M,\pi)
. We saw before that its leaf space
M/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
, can be identified with the leaf space
\widehat{M}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
of its resolution, and we denoted it by
B
, so we have

\textstyle{\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}

The space
B
inherits an integral affine orbifold structure for each choice of proper integration. Any such choice induces the same classical orbifold structure on
B
, which coincides with the orbifold structure induced by the proper groupoid
\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
. In this section
we will work exclusively with this classical orbifold structure
. We will still need to fix an integration to specify the integral affine structure on
B
.

6.1.
The orbifold fundamental group

The orbifold fundamental group(oid) will play a crucial role for us, so let us recall its definition. It is convenient to adopt a more general point of view and define the fundamental groupoid of a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
. For details we refer to
[
6
,
34
]
. First, one defines a Haeflieger path on
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows X
to be a sequence

\eta=(\gamma_{0},g_{1},\gamma_{1},\dots,g_{k},\gamma_{k})

where
\gamma_{i}
are paths in
X
,
\gamma_{i}\in\mathcal{G}
, and

\mathbf{t}(g_{i})=\gamma_{i-1}(0),\quad\mathbf{s}(g_{i})=\gamma_{i}(1).

One defines the source and target of
\eta
by

\mathbf{s}(\eta)=\gamma_{k}(0),\quad\mathbf{t}(\eta)=\gamma_{0}(1).

There is an obvious concatenation operation on the set
P(\mathcal{G})
of all Haeflieger paths. Also, given two Haeflieger paths
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in P(\mathcal{G})
with the same end-points one defines a homotopy between them to be the equivalence relation
\sim
generated by the following operations:

(i)

multiplication:
If
\gamma_{i}
is a constant path, then

(\gamma_{0},g_{1},\gamma_{1},\dots,g_{i},\gamma_{i},g_{i+1},\dots,g_{k},\gamma_{k})\sim(\gamma_{0},g_{1},\gamma_{1},\dots,g_{i}g_{i+1},\dots,g_{k},\gamma_{k});

(ii)

concatenation:
If
g_{i}=1_{\gamma_{i}(1)}
, then

(\gamma_{0},g_{1},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{i-1},g_{i},\gamma_{i},\dots,g_{k},\gamma_{k})\sim(\gamma_{0},g_{1},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{i-1}\gamma_{i},\dots,g_{k},\gamma_{k});

(iii)

deformation:
If there is a continuous family of Haeflieger paths
\eta_{\varepsilon}
with fix end-point
\mathbf{s}(\eta_{\varepsilon})=x_{0}
and
\mathbf{t}(\eta_{\varepsilon})=x_{1}
, then
\eta_{0}\sim\eta_{1}
.

The fundamental groupoid of
\mathcal{G}
has arrows the equivalence classes of Haeflieger paths and will be denoted

\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G})\rightrightarrows X.

Multiplication is induced by concatenation. It is proved in
[
34
]
that this is a Lie groupoid.
The fundamental group of
\mathcal{G}
based at
x
is, by definition, the resulting isotropy group at
x
,

\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},x):=\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G})_{x}.

Since
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G})
is transitive, the fundamental groups of
\mathcal{G}
based at different points are isomorphic.

Notice that we have natural Lie groupoid morphisms

(6.1)

\mathcal{G}\to\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}),\qquad\Pi_{1}(X)\to\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}).

The second one is an instance of the fact that a groupoid morphism
\phi:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
induces a morphism between the fundamental groupoids
\phi_{*}:\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H})\to\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G})
. The later is actually an isomorphism whenever
\phi
is a weak equivalence, so Morita equivalent groupoids have isomorphic fundamental groups and groupoids.

Let
B
be an orbifold, with a choice of an orbifold atlas
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows X
. We will fix a base point
x_{0}\in X
and we will call

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B,b_{0}):=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B},x_{0})

the
orbifold fundamental group
of
B
based at
b_{0}=p(x_{0})
. We have the map induced by the inclusion of the units

(6.2)

\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B,b_{0}).

which can also be thought as being induced by the orbit space projection
p:X\to B
.

We recall also that any (effective) orbifold
B
has a universal covering space

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}

which is itself an (effective) orbifold. If
B
is presented by an orbifold atlas
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows X
and we fix
x_{0}\in X
, then the model for
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
, based at
x_{0}
, is the orbifold atlas given by the action groupoid

(6.3)

\mathcal{B}\ltimes\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{B})(x_{0},-)\rightrightarrows\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{B})(x_{0},-),

together with the (right) action of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B,b_{0})=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B},x_{0})
.

We will be interested in two more specific situations that we now describe.

6.1.1.
Orbifolds presented by a holonomy groupoid

Assume
B
has orbifold atlas the holonomy groupoid of a proper foliation

\mathcal{B}=\Hol(X,\mathcal{F})\rightrightarrows X.

Note that in this case every arrow in
\mathcal{B}
is represented by a path, from which it follows that (
6.2
) is a surjective morphism. This allows us to reinterpret
\pi^{\mathrm{orb}}_{1}(B,x_{0})
as the quotient of
\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})
modulo the equivalence relation, denoted
\sim_{\mathcal{F}}
and called
\mathcal{F}
-homotopy
, induced by the kernel of (
6.2
):

(6.4)

K_{x_{0}}=\big\{[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})\,|\,\gamma\sim_{\mathcal{F}}x_{0}\big\}.

Explicitly, a loop is
\mathcal{F}
-homotopic to the constant path
x_{0}
if it is homotopic to a concatenation of loops
\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{k}
, each of the form

\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}\alpha_{i}^{-1},

where
\beta_{i}
is a leafwise loop with trivial holonomy, based at some
y_{i}
, and
\alpha_{i}
is an arbitrary path connecting
y_{i}
to
x_{0}
(note that the
\beta_{i}
’s will be in general contained in different leaves).

In this case, the construction (
6.3
) of the universal covering space yields a connected space

X^{\mathrm{orb}}:=\Hol(X,\mathcal{F})(x_{0},-)\longrightarrow X

This is precisely the covering space of
X
with group of deck of transformations
\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)
, i.e., the quotient of the universal covering space of
X
modulo the kernel of (
6.2
).
The construction of the orbifold universal cover yields an orbifold atlas consisting of the holonomy groupoid of the pull-back foliation
\mathcal{F}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
along
X^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to X
. In particular, the underlying topological space is
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=X^{{\mathrm{orb}}}/\mathcal{F}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
with atlas

\Hol(X^{{\mathrm{orb}}},\mathcal{F}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})\rightrightarrows X^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}X^{{\mathrm{orb}}}/\mathcal{F}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}.

For the next lemma recall (Corollary
3.12
) that principal leaves of a proper foliation are the leaves with trivial holonomy.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{F})
be a proper foliation and on the leaf space
p:X\to B
consider the orbifold defined by the atlas
\Hol(X,\mathcal{F})\rightrightarrows X
. If all principal leaves are 1-connected then one has an isomorphism

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B,p(x_{0}))\simeq\pi_{1}(X,x_{0}).

Proof.

Let
K_{x_{0}}
be the kernel of the morphism (
6.2
). An element
[\gamma]\in K_{x_{0}}
is a concatenation of loops
of the form

l=\sigma\beta\sigma^{-1},

where
\beta
is a loop with trivial holonomy in the leaf
L
containing the base point
x_{0}
and
\sigma
connects
x_{0}
with the starting point of
\beta
. Let
\nu(L)\subset X
be a tubular neighborhood of
L
. Since
\beta
has trivial holonomy, pulling back
(\nu(L),\mathcal{F})
along
\beta
yields a foliation which is trivial near the zero section, so we can homotope
\beta
to any close enough leaf. Since the principal locus is dense, we can assume that
\beta
is in a principal leaf. By assumption, principal leaves are 1-connected so
\beta
is contractible in its leaf. Therefore we conclude that
l
is contractible and thus that
K_{x_{0}}
is the trivial group.
∎

6.1.2.
Good orbifolds

Assume now that
B
has orbifold atlas the action groupoid

\mathcal{B}=B^{\prime}\rtimes\Gamma\rightrightarrows B^{\prime}

associated with a proper and effective action of a discrete group
\Gamma
on a manifold
B^{\prime}
. The orbifold fundamental group has the explicit model
\pi_{1}(B^{\prime}\ltimes\Gamma,b^{\prime}_{0})
defined as the collection of pairs
(g,\sigma)
with
g\in\Gamma
and where
\sigma
is a path homotopy class in
B^{\prime}
from
b^{\prime}_{0}
to
b^{\prime}_{0}g
, with the composition

(6.5)

(g_{1},\sigma_{1})(g_{2},\sigma_{2})=(g_{1}g_{2},g_{2}^{*}(\sigma_{1})\sigma_{2}).

where
g_{2}^{*}(\sigma_{1})
is the
g_{2}
-translate of
\sigma_{1}
.
It follows right away that it fits into a short exact sequence

(6.6)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(B^{\prime},b^{\prime}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B,b_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

In this case, the action groupoid (
6.3
) presenting the orbifold universal cover has base space a smooth manifold, namely the universal covering space of
B^{\prime}
based at
b^{\prime}_{0}
:

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=\widetilde{B^{\prime}},

which we think of as consisting of path homotopy classes of paths in
B^{\prime}
starting at
b^{\prime}_{0}
.
The resulting (right) action of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B,b_{0})
on this space can be described explicitly as:

(6.7)

\tau\cdot(g,\sigma)=g^{*}(\tau)\sigma

This action is proper and effective, and this can also be checked directly using (
6.6
). However, the action is free if and only if the original action of
\Gamma
on
B^{\prime}
is free.

6.1.3.
Orbifold fundamental group of the leaf space of PMCTs

We can now apply the previous discussion to our setting of PMCTs. Recall we are viewing the leaf space
B=\widehat{M}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}=M/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
as a classical orbifold. The next result clarifies the missing piece in diagram (
5.14
).

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold with a proper integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows(M,\pi)
and fix a base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}=(x_{0},\mathfrak{t}_{0})\in\widehat{M}
. In the main diagram (
5.5
)
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is the orbifold universal covering of the leaf space
B
. In particular,
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
acts properly and effectively on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
and there is a Morita equivalence

(6.8)

\textstyle{\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\rtimes\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}}
\textstyle{\,\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}}

Note also that the kernel
K=K_{x_{0}}
of the homomorphism
6.2
coincides with the normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}}_{0})
associated with the covering space
{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to\widehat{M}
.

6.2.
The Weyl group

Given a Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)
and chosing
{\widehat{x}}=(x,\mathfrak{t}_{x})\in\widehat{M}
, the kernel of the map

{\mathscr{R}}_{*}:\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})\to\pi_{1}(M,x)

will be denoted
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}}
. We recall that we view the leaf space
B
of
(M,\pi)
as a classical orbifold presented by
\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
.

Definition 6.3
.

The Weyl group
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
of
(M,\pi)
at
{\widehat{x}}
is the image of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}}
under the map

(6.9)

\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B,b).

By passing from
\widehat{M}
to
B
we are able to make use of the action of
\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B,b)
on the orbifold universal covering space
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
. In particular, we obtain a proper and effective action of
\mathcal{W}_{\widehat{x}}
on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
. Our ultimate aim is to show that
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
is a Coxeter group acting as a reflection group on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
, in the sense of
[
17
]
.

Note that the Weyl group
\mathcal{W}_{\widehat{x}}
is a normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B,b)
because (
6.9
) is surjective. The following commutative diagram explains the relationship between the various groups that appear in the definition:

(6.10)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(M,x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B,b)}

The top row splits canonically: we have an isomorphism
{\mathscr{R}}:\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{reg}}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
and
M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}\hookrightarrow M

induces an isomorphism in
\pi_{1}
. Hence, we have

\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})\cong\pi_{1}(M,x)\ltimes\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}}.

We will see later that the orbifold fundamental group of
B
is also a semi-direct product with kernel
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
.

Remark 6.4

(Base-points)
.

All the covering spaces appearing in the main diagram are constructed using paths based at some
{\widehat{x}}=(x,\mathfrak{t}_{x})
. Note that concatenation by a path
\sigma
gives isomorphisms between the spaces in the main diagram based at
\sigma(0)={\widehat{x}}
and at
\sigma(1)={\widehat{y}}
. Conjugation by
\sigma
gives isomorphisms
\phi
between the (orbifold) fundamental groups at
{\widehat{x}}
and at
{\widehat{y}}
, that carry the groups
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}}
to
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{y}}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{y}}}
. The isomorphisms between the spaces are
\phi
-equivariant. In other words, the path
\sigma
provides an equivariant isomorphism between the main diagram based at
{\widehat{x}}
and at
{\widehat{y}}
.

With that in mind, from now on we fix a base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}=(x_{0},\mathfrak{t}_{0})
with
x_{0}\in M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
. We endow all the spaces with the resulting base points, denoted

b_{0}=p({\widehat{x}}_{0})\in B,\ x_{0}\in M,\ \widetilde{b}_{0}\in\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}},\textrm{etc}.

and we denote

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}=\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}_{0}},\quad\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}_{0}}.

Furthermore, when we use fundamental groups, we omit base-points from the notation unless necessary.

Example 6.5
.

For
M=\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the Lie algebra of a compact connected Lie group
G
, we claim that

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\cong\mathcal{W}\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\cong W,

where
W=N(T)/T
is the classical Weyl group of
G
relative to the maximal torus
T\subset G
.

To prove this claim, recall from Example
4.3
that the resolution map for
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is

{\mathscr{R}}:G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*},\quad(gT,\xi)\mapsto\Ad_{g}(\xi).

Since the image is contractible, by (
6.10
) we have

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\cong\pi_{1}(G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*}),\quad\mathcal{W}\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B),

where
\pi_{1}(G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*})\cong W
.
Since the principal leaves are diffeomorphic to
G/T
, they are simply connected and Lemma
6.1
implies that
\pi_{1}(G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*})\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
, and so the claim follows.

Observe that the Weyl group
\mathcal{W}
in this example appears as the fundamental group of the fiber of the resolution
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(0)=\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})
:

(6.11)

\pi_{1}\left(\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})\right)\cong N(T)/T=W.

This fact will be generalized appropriately in Proposition

6.8
.

For a more concrete example let

M=\mathfrak{su}(2)^{*}
, which we can
identify with
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The coadjoint action of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is given, via the covering morphism
\mathrm{SU}(2)\to\mathrm{SO}(3)
, by rotations. We let
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{su}(2)
be the span of the infinitesimal generator of the rotations around an axis and set
W
to be the Weyl group of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
relative to
T
. We have the diffeomorphism

(6.12)

\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)^{*}}\cong\left(\mathrm{SU}(2)/T\times\mathfrak{t}\right)/W\cong(S^{2}\times\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{Z}_{2},

where
S^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the unit sphere and the non-trivial element of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
act as

(6.13)

(\theta,z)\mapsto(-\theta,-z).

Under the isomorphism (
6.12
) the resolution map becomes

{\mathscr{R}}:(S^{2}\times\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad[\theta,z]\mapsto z\theta,

its fiber over the origin is

(S^{2}\times\{0\})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\mathbb{RP}^{2}.

The Weyl group in this case is
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\cong\mathcal{W}\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\cong\pi_{1}(\mathbb{RP}^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Example 6.6
.

Consider now the Cartan-Dirac structure
L_{G}
on a compact simply connected Lie group
G
determined by a fixed
\Ad
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
. Recall from Example
4.20
that, fixing a maximal torus
T\subset G
, the resolution can be identified with

\widehat{G}=G/T\times_{W}T.

We claim that this twisted Dirac structure has Weyl group

\mathcal{W}\cong\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\cong\Lambda_{G}\rtimes W,

where
W=N(T)/T
acts on
\Lambda_{G}=\Ker(\exp:\mathfrak{t}\to T)
via the standard representation
W\to\GL(\mathfrak{t})
.
Since, by assumption, the base of the resolution map

{\mathscr{R}}:G/T\times_{W}T\to G

is simply connected, by (
6.10
) we have

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\cong\pi_{1}(G/T\times_{W}T),\quad\mathcal{W}\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B).

Since the principal leaves are diffeomorphic to
G/T
, they are simply connected and Lemma
6.1
implies that
\pi_{1}(G/T\times_{W}T)\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
.

We are left to prove that

\pi_{1}(G/T\times_{W}T)\simeq\Lambda_{G}\rtimes W.

This follows by observing that we have an isomorphism

(G/T\times\mathfrak{t})/(\Lambda_{G}\rtimes W)\cong G/T\times_{W}T,\quad[(gT,\xi)]\mapsto[(gT,\exp(\xi))],

where on the left side,
\Lambda_{G}
acts trivially on
G/T
and by translations on
\mathfrak{t}
.

For a concrete example, the case
G=\mathrm{SU}(2)
. To obtain an explicit expression of the resolution map we let
T
be the diagonal matrices in
\mathrm{SU}(2)
and we regard
\mathrm{SU}(2)
as the unit sphere in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
with coordinates
z_{1},z_{2}
. Then
T
is identified with the unit circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
with coordinate
u
. The resolution map becomes

{\mathscr{R}}:\left(\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\right)/N(\mathbb{S}^{1})\to\mathrm{SU}(2),\quad[z_{1},z_{2},u]\mapsto(uz_{1}\bar{z}_{1}+\bar{u}z_{2}\bar{z}_{2},(\bar{u}-u)z_{1}z_{2}).

Its fiber at
\pm I
is

\left(\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\{\pm 1\}\right)/N(\mathbb{S}^{1})\cong\mathbb{S}^{2}/{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cong\mathbb{RP}^{2}.

Note that the Weyl group in this case is

\mathcal{W}\cong\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\cong\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2},

where
-1\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts on
\mathbb{Z}
by multiplication.

Observe that we can also compute the fundamental group of

\left(\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\right)/N(\mathbb{S}^{1})\cong\mathbb{S}^{2}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathbb{S}^{1},

using the Seifert-Van Kampen theorem, and this recovers the well-known isomorphism

\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}*\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Example 6.7
.

Let us consider the twisted Dirac structure
(M,L)
obtained by removing from the Cartan-Dirac structure on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
the point
-I
. Then it follows from the previous example that the resolution is

{\mathscr{R}}:\widehat{M}=\mathbb{S}^{2}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathbb{S}^{1}\backslash\{-1\})\to M=\mathrm{SU}(2)\backslash\{-I\}.

Since the base of the resolution and the principal fiber – still
\mathbb{S}^{2}
– are simply connected, we have as in the previous examples, that

\mathcal{W}\cong\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\cong\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Note that this example does not fit into classical Lie theory, so one does not have a classical notion of Weyl group.

6.3.
Abstract reflections

Our next aim is to show that
\mathcal{W}
is generated by certain “reflections”, which will be introduced below abstractly as certain elements of order two. In the next section we will show that they can be realized as actual geometric reflections on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
.

The most obvious way to exhibit elements of the Weyl group is by focusing on the fundamental groups of the fibers of
{\mathscr{R}}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Fix a point
{\widehat{x}}=(x,\mathfrak{t}_{x})\in\widehat{M}
and let
i:{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)\hookrightarrow\widehat{M}
be the inclusion. Then there is a canonical diffeomorphism

{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)\cong G/N(T),

where
G=\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}
and
T\subset G
is the torus corresponding to
\mathfrak{t}_{x}
. The composition

\textstyle{\pi_{1}({\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x),{\widehat{x}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B,b)}

gives a canonical injective homomorphism

W\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}\,,

where
W=N(T)/T
is the Weyl group.

Proof.

For simplicity of notations let
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}_{x}
,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{x}
,
G=\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}
, and
T=T_{x}
. By definition,
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)=\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})
and the canonical isomorphism in the statement is

G/N(T)\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}),\quad gN(T)\mapsto\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{t}).

We claim that the map
i_{*}

is injective. For that we use the holonomy representation associated to the flat tautological bundle
\mathscr{T}
over
\widehat{M}
,

h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}:\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})\to\GL(\mathfrak{t}).

More precisely, we will show that
h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\circ i_{*}
is injective. This composition is, of course, the holonomy map of the flat bundle
\mathscr{T}|_{{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)}
. In turn,

\mathscr{T}|_{{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)}
is identified with the tautological bundle over
G/N(T)
, call it
E
. This flat bundle can be trivialized by pulling it back to the universal cover

G/T\to G/N(T).

We see that, conversely,
E\to G/N(T)
can be described, as a flat bundle, as the quotient of the trivial bundle
G/T\times\mathfrak{t}
modulo the diagonal action of
W
, where the action on the second factor is via the standard representation
W\to\GL(\mathfrak{t})
.
This is similar to the computation of the linear holonomy in Section
5.6
.
The holonomy of
E\to G/N(T)
is the the standard representation of
W
.
Hence
i_{*}
is injective.

On the other hand, recall that
h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
factors through
p_{*}
:

\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{{\mathrm{lin}}}}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{{\mathrm{lin}}}\subset\GL(\mathfrak{t})}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B,b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Therefore, the previous argument implies also that
p_{*}\circ i_{*}
is injective.
∎

Example 6.9
.

When
M=Q/\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is the Hamiltonian local model, if one uses points
Q
-related to
0\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, then the previous proposition gives a copy of the Weyl group
W^{0}:=N(T)\cap G^{0}/T
inside the Weyl group of
M
. Later, in Proposition
6.16
(see also Remark
6.18
), we will show that Weyl groups are Morita invariant, so one finds
\mathcal{W}\cong W^{0}
.

When
{\widehat{x}}\in\widehat{M}
is a subregular point one has
\mathfrak{g}_{x}\cong\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{z}_{x}
with
\mathfrak{z}_{x}
abelian,
the fiber over
x
is

{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)=\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{su}(2))\cong\mathbb{RP}^{2},

so has fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The last proposition then shows that at any such subregular point one obtains a non-trivial involution

\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}\in\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}.

This will be called the
subregular reflection
at
{\widehat{x}}
.

Definition 6.10
.

An
abstract reflection
in
\mathcal{W}
is any element obtained from a subregular reflection
\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}\in\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
by transporting it along a path from
{\widehat{x}}
to
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
.

Note that each subregular reflection
\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}
does not give rise to a unique abstract reflection in
\mathcal{W}
. Rather, it produces a canonical conjugacy class of involutions which only depends on the subregular strata
\Sigma
through
{\widehat{x}}
.

Similarly, one shall make use of abstract reflections in
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
, induced by the order two elements
\widehat{\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}}
in the image of
i_{*}:\pi_{1}({\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x),{\widehat{x}})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})
.

Example 6.11
.

In the case
M=\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, fixing a maximal torus
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, we saw in Example
6.5
that the Weyl group
\mathcal{W}
is isomorphic to the classical Weyl group
W
of
G
relative to
\mathfrak{t}
. The abstract reflections are exactly the standard reflections associated to the root hyperplanes in
\mathfrak{t}
. Indeed, if
\xi
is a subregular point on a root hyperplane
\mathcal{H}
, then
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(\xi)
embedds canonically in
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(0)\cong G/N(T)
as the set of maximal tori of
\mathfrak{g}
contained in
\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}
. The generator of the fundamental group of this embedded
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
is the standard reflection on
\mathcal{H}
. The straight path connecting
\widehat{\xi}:=(\xi,\mathfrak{t})
with
(0,\mathfrak{t})
conjugates
\tau_{\widehat{\xi}}
to the standard reflection on
\mathcal{H}
.

The fact that the standard reflections generate the classical Weyl group generalizes as follows.

Proposition 6.12
.

For any Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)
the Weyl group
\mathcal{W}
is generated by the abstract reflections.

The proposition follows from the following lemma.

Lemma 6.13
.

Any element in
\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
can be written as a product of abstract reflections in
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
and an element in the image of the splitting in (
6.10
).

Proof of the Lemma.

Let
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
.
We can assume that
\gamma
is a smooth loop which, as in the proof of Lemma
3.7
, intersects at most the codimension one strata of
\mathcal{S}^{\textrm{rk}}(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}})={\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi))
and any such intersection is transverse. By Corollary
4.17
these are subregular points.

Next, we claim that we can eliminate successively the finitely many intersection points one by one:
for the first subregular intersection point we construct an abstract reflection
\widehat{\tau}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
such that
[\gamma]=\widehat{\tau}\cdot[\gamma^{\prime}]
where
\gamma^{\prime}
has one less subregular intersection point than
\gamma
.

Let
t_{1}\in[0,1]
be the fist value such that
\gamma(t_{1})={\widehat{x}}
is a subregular point on the stratum
{\widehat{\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\textrm{rk}}(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}})
. We can assume that the reflection
\widehat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}
is represented by an embedded loop
\kappa

and we decompose
\gamma=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
according to the subdivision
[0,1]=[0,t_{1}]\cup[t_{1},1]
. Then we have the factorization

[\gamma]=[\sigma_{1}\kappa\kappa\sigma_{2}]=[\sigma_{1}\kappa\sigma_{1}^{-1}][\sigma_{1}\kappa\sigma_{2}],

where, by construction,
[\sigma_{1}\kappa\sigma_{1}^{-1}]
is an abstract reflection. We claim that
\sigma_{1}\kappa\sigma_{2}
is homotopic to a loop which intersects
the codimension one strata
{\widehat{\Sigma}}
in one point less than
\gamma
.

Since
\widehat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}=[\kappa]
is nontrivial, the restriction of the normal bundle of
{\widehat{\Sigma}}
in
\widehat{M}
to
\kappa
is the non-trivial line bundle over
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
. It further follows that there is an embedded Moebius band

\Phi:\mathbb{R}\times(-1,1)/(\theta,s)\sim(\theta+2\pi,-s)\to\widehat{M},

\kappa(t)=\Phi([2\pi t,0]),\,t\in[0,1],\quad{\widehat{x}}=\Phi([0,0]),

so that
{\widehat{\Sigma}}\cap\Phi=\kappa
.
We can arrange that
\gamma\cap\Phi

be the normal fiber over
{\widehat{x}}
and that points in the Moebius band other than the zero section are regular points.
Then the normal fibers can be used to perform a homotopy supported in the Moebius band which moves the curve

\sigma_{1}\kappa\sigma_{2}
away form the zero section, i.e., away from
{\widehat{\Sigma}}
(see Figure
1
).

Figure 1.
Homotopy supported on Moebius band

∎

To close this section we will show that
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
is a
\mathcal{W}
-semi-direct product. In other words, that
there is an analogue for
\mathcal{W}
of the split exact sequence in the diagram
6.10
.

For that, note that for any Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)
, the regular part
(M^{\mathrm{reg}},\pi)
is also of proper type. The restriction of a proper integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
to
M^{\mathrm{reg}}
endows the leaf space
B^{\mathrm{reg}}
with an integral affine orbifold structure. For this structure, the inclusion of the regular part
M^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset M

descends to an embedding of classical integral affine
orbifolds (see Section
5.2
)

B^{\mathrm{reg}}\hookrightarrow B.

We also define the regular part of any of the orbifold covering spaces of
B
, denoted

(B^{\mathrm{lin}})^{\mathrm{reg}},\,(B^{\mathrm{aff}})^{\mathrm{reg}},\,(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathrm{reg}},

as the pre-image of
B^{\mathrm{reg}}
by the corresponding covering maps.

Proposition 6.14
.

The map in homotopy induced by the inclusion
B^{{\mathrm{reg}}}\subset B
gives a splitting of the short exact sequence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 69.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 69.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 208.12994pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 208.12994pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Furthermore, the image of this splitting consists of the
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
-stabilizer of the connected component of
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
containing the base-point.

Proof.

We complete the diagram
6.10
with the orbifold fundamental group of the regular part
M^{\mathrm{reg}}
. The orbifold embedding
B^{\mathrm{reg}}\hookrightarrow B
induces a map between the orbifold fundamental groups that makes the outer square of the following diagram commutative

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(M^{\mathrm{reg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{\widehat{i}}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathscr{r}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}

We claim that there exists dotted arrow
\mathscr{r}_{*}
making the inner square commutative. By diagram chasing, using that vertical arrows are surjective, this implies the exactness of the sequence

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

Next, again by a diagram chasing, it follows that the existence of
\mathscr{r}_{*}
is equivalent to the fact that
{\mathscr{R}}_{*}(K)\subset\Ker(p_{*})
, where
K
is the kernel of the middle vertical arrow (described in (
6.4
)).
This will follow by showing that

(6.14)

{\widehat{i}}\circ{\mathscr{R}}_{*}(K)\subset K.

To prove this, we show that any
[\gamma]\in K
can be factorized as

(6.15)

[\gamma]=[\gamma_{1}]\cdots[\gamma_{k}],\quad\textrm{ with }\quad[\gamma_{i}]=w_{i}[\gamma^{\prime}_{i}]w_{i}^{-1},

where
\gamma_{i}^{\prime}\subset\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{reg}}
,
[\gamma_{i}^{\prime}]\in K
, and
w_{i}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
. Using this factorization, we find that if we let
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\gamma^{\prime}_{k}
, then

{\widehat{i}}\circ{\mathscr{R}}_{*}[\gamma]={\widehat{i}}\circ{\mathscr{R}}_{*}[\gamma^{\prime}]=[\gamma^{\prime}]\in K,

where the last equality holds because of the definition of
\widehat{i}
and
\gamma^{\prime}\subset\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Hence, (
6.14
) follows.

It remains to prove the factorization (
6.15
). By (
6.4
), we note that any
[\gamma]\in K
is a product loops
of type

(6.16)

\mathscr{l}=\sigma\beta\sigma^{-1},

with
\beta
a null-holonomic leafwise loop and
\beta
is a path between the base point of
\beta
and
x_{0}
. We will show that for such
\mathscr{l}
we have

(6.17)

\mathscr{l}=w\mathscr{l}^{\prime}w^{-1}

with
w\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
and
\mathscr{l}^{\prime}\in K
sitting inside
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
, this proving (
6.15
).
To prove (
6.17
), starting with
6.16
, we make the following modifications:

(i)

Since
\beta
is null-holonomic, as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, we may write
\mathscr{l}=\alpha\beta^{\prime}{\alpha^{-1}}

with
\beta^{\prime}\subset\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{reg}}
a leafwise null-holonomic path.

(ii)

We observe that the factorization result of Lemma
6.13
applies not just to loops, but to paths with end-points in
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. By applying it to
\alpha
we obtain that our
\mathscr{l}
can be written as claimed in (
6.17
).

The last part of the statement can be seen as a particular case of a property of classical orbifolds. In order to state it, we use the notion of classical embedded orbifold (see Section
5.2
).

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
B
be a good orbifold, assume that
B_{0}\subset B
is a classical embedded suborbifold with
b_{0}\in B_{0}
.
Then the image of the map

i_{*}:\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B_{0})\to\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)

induced by the inclusion is the
\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)
-stabilizer of the connected component
C
of
p^{-1}(B_{0})
containing the base point
\widetilde{b}_{0}
,
where
p^{-1}(B_{0})
is the pre-image of
B_{0}
by the canonical projection
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to B

Proof.

We use the notations and the discussion from Section
6.1.2
.
Since
B
is a good orbifold and
B_{0}
is a classical embedded suborbifoldwe can write

(B,b_{0})=(B^{\prime},b_{0}^{\prime})/\Gamma,\quad(B_{0},b_{0})=(B^{\prime}_{0},b_{0}^{\prime})/\Gamma,

with
B^{\prime}_{0}\subset B^{\prime}
a
\Gamma
-invariant embedded submanifold and the
\Gamma
-action on
B^{\prime}_{0}
remains effective.
Given an element

a=(g,\sigma)\in\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)=\pi_{1}(B^{\prime}\ltimes\Gamma)

(see (
6.5
) and the discussion preceding it) the condition
C\cdot a=C
is equivalent to the existence of a path in
p^{-1}(B_{0})
connecting
\widetilde{b}_{0}
with
\widetilde{b}_{0}a
. Recalling that
\widetilde{b}_{0}=1_{b_{0}^{\prime}}
is represented by the constant path at
b_{0}^{\prime}
, (
6.7
) gives

\widetilde{b_{0}}\cdot a=g^{*}(1_{b_{0}^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma=1_{b_{0}^{\prime}g}\cdot\sigma=\sigma,

where
\cdot
stands for the concatenation.
Hence there exists a path in
p^{-1}(B_{0})
between

\widetilde{b}_{0}
and
\sigma
. That means precisely that
\sigma
is represented by a path in
B^{\prime}_{0}
, i.e., that
a
is in the image of the splitting.
∎

∎

6.4.
Morita invariance of the Weyl group

Next we prove Morita invariance of the Weyl groups.

Proposition 6.16
.

Let
(M_{i},\pi_{i})
be two PMCTs with integrating proper symplectic groupoids
(\mathcal{G}_{i},\Omega_{i})\rightrightarrows(M_{i},\pi_{i})
, and let
(Q,\Omega)
be a Morita equivalence between them.
Then the homeomorphism induced by
Q
between the leaf spaces
B_{i}=M_{i}/\mathcal{F}_{\pi_{i}}
,

\phi:B_{1}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}B_{2},

is an isomorphism between the orbifold integral affine structures induced by
(\mathcal{G}_{i},\Omega_{i})
. Furthermore, the induced map in the orbifold fundamental groups,

\varphi:\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B_{1})\to\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B_{2})

restricts to an isomorphism between the corresponding Weyl groups and makes the induced isomorphism
\phi_{*}:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{1}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{2}
a
\varphi
-equivariant map. Under this isomorphism abstract reflections correspond to each other.

Remark 6.17
.

The statement of the proposition can be refined by specifying the base points
{\widehat{x}}_{i}=(x_{i},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\in\widehat{M}_{i}
appropriately. The points
x_{i}\in M_{i}
should be
Q
-related via a point
q\in Q
. Such a
q
gives rise to an identification between the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{x_{i}}
(cf. Remark
2.2
) and we require that the
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
’s correspond to each other. With these choices in place, we obtain concrete models for the orbifold fundamental groups, for the
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}_{i}}
sitting inside them, as well as for
\phi_{*}
mapping
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}_{1}}
isomorphically to
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}_{2}}
.

Proof.

Observe that for a Morita equivalence
\textstyle{M_{1}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}_{2}}
\textstyle{M_{2}}
as in the statement the pull-back of the graphs of the Poisson structures,

L_{1}:=\mathfrak{p}_{1}^{*}L_{\pi_{1}}
and
L_{2}:=\mathfrak{p}_{2}^{*}L_{\pi_{2}}
, are related to each other via a gauge transformation w.r.t.
\Omega
(see
[
13
, page 154]
). Moreover,
(Q,L_{1})
and
(Q,L_{2})
are DMCTs with integrating proper presymplectic groupoids the pullback groupoids
\mathfrak{p}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{i}=Q\times_{M_{i}}\mathcal{G}_{i}\times_{M_{i}}Q\rightrightarrows Q
endowed with presymplectic forms that differ by
\mathbf{t}^{*}\Omega-\mathbf{s}^{*}\Omega
. Therefore, the leaf spaces of
(Q,L_{i})
, their integral affine orbifold structures and their Weyl groups are identical .

We are left with proving the following claim. Given a submersion with connected fibers
\mathfrak{p}:Q\to M
into a Poisson manifold of proper type
(M,\pi)
and a proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
, consider the Dirac manifold
(Q,L=\mathfrak{p}^{*}L_{\pi})
together with the proper presymplectic integration
\mathfrak{p}^{*}\mathcal{G}=Q\times_{M}\mathcal{G}\times_{M}Q\rightrightarrows Q
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism between the leaf space of
(M,\pi)
and
(Q,L)
which preserves their integral affine structures and identifies their Weyl groups.

To prove this claim we pass to the level of the resolutions. Their construction shows that
\mathfrak{p}:Q\to M
induces a submersion with connected fibers

\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}:\widehat{Q}\to\widehat{M}.

This submersion pulls back the foliation of
\widehat{M}
to the foliation of
\widehat{Q}
. Hence their leaf spaces are canonical isomorphic classical orbifolds. Also, observe that the natural groupoid morphisms
\mathfrak{p}^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
yields a groupoid morphism at the level of the action groupoids over the resolutions:

\mathfrak{p}^{*}\mathcal{G}\ltimes\widehat{Q}\to\mathcal{G}\ltimes\widehat{M}.

This restricts to an isomorphism between isotropy groups so the transverse integral affine structures of
\widehat{Q}
and
\widehat{M}
also correspond to each other, and we have an isomorphism of the integral affine structures on the leaf spaces.

For the claim on the Weyl groups, we consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\widehat{q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{Q},\widehat{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(Q,q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}_{*}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\pi^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{1}(B,b)}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(M,x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

where
\widehat{q}\in\widehat{Q}
determines all the other points
q\in Q
,
{\widehat{x}}\in\widehat{M}
,
x\in M
,
b\in B
. We have to prove that

(6.18)

i(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\widehat{q}})=j(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}}),

coincide. The inclusion

i(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\widehat{q}})\subset j(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}})
is clear. For the reverse, we start with
u\in j(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}})
. By Proposition
6.12
, we may assume that
u
arises from a reflection
corresponding to a point
{\widehat{y}}
in a subregular strata:
u=j(\sigma\tau_{{\widehat{y}}}\sigma^{-1})
, with
\tau_{{\widehat{y}}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{y}}}
as above, and where
\sigma
is a path in
\widehat{M}
between
{\widehat{x}}
and
{\widehat{y}}
. We also choose a point
\widehat{r}\in\widehat{Q}
above
{\widehat{y}}
and a path
\widetilde{\sigma}
in
\widehat{Q}
between
\widehat{q}
and
\widehat{r}
. Since the groups that we are comparing are normal in
\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)
we may assume that
\widetilde{\sigma}
covers
\sigma
. Observe now that:

(i)

\widehat{r}
is necessarily subregular for
(Q,L)
, so it has an associated element
\tau_{\widehat{r}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\widehat{r}}
.

(ii)

The map
\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}
sends
\tau_{\widehat{r}}
to
\tau_{{\widehat{y}}}
. This is due to the fact that
p:\widehat{Q}\to\widehat{M}
restricts to a diffeomorphism between the fibers of the resolution maps above
q
and
x
.

It follows that
j(\tau_{{\widehat{y}}})=i(\tau_{\widehat{r}})

and then

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=j(\sigma\tau_{{\widehat{y}}}\sigma^{-1})

\displaystyle=j(\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}(\widetilde{\sigma})\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}(\tau_{\widehat{r}})\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}(\widetilde{\sigma})^{-1})

\displaystyle=j\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{*}(\widetilde{\sigma}\tau_{\widehat{r}}\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1})

\displaystyle=i(\widetilde{\sigma}\tau_{\widehat{r}}\widetilde{\sigma}).

This shows that (
6.18
)
