holds and completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 6.18

(s-connecteness)
.

Our symplectic groupoids are assumed to be source connected. However, in the Hamiltonian local model we will encounter non-source connected groupoids whenever the isotropy
G=\mathcal{G}_{x}
is disconnected. For that, it is important to note that Morita invariance of the Weyl groups remains valid for Morita equivalent symplectic groupoids that are not necessarily s-connected, provided the Morita bi-bundle is connected.

For this, note that for a proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows(M,\pi)
which is not necessarily s-connected, the relevant orbit space is
B=M/\mathcal{G}
, and that may be different from the leaf space
M/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
. To define the Weyl group
\mathcal{W}\subset\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
one still uses the resolution
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\widehat{\Omega})\to(\widehat{M},\widehat{L}_{\pi})
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}=\widehat{M}\rtimes\mathcal{G}
may fail to be s-connected. The classical orbifold structure on
B=M/\mathcal{G}\cong\widehat{M}/\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
is presented by the effectivization
\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})
of the proper foliation groupoid
\mathcal{B}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})
of the associated short exact sequence

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

The effective groupoid
\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})\rightrightarrows\widehat{M}
integrates the foliation
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
– although it is possibly not s-connected – so its source connected component is
\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
. Hence,
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
is defined as the fundamental group of
\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})
and the Weyl group is defined as before by the diagram

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{{\widehat{x}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(M,x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B,b)}

We remark that now the vertical arrow
p_{*}
is not surjective in general.

The proof of Proposition
6.16
can be adapted to show that
a connected symplectic Morita equivalence between (not necessarily s-connected) symplectic groupoids
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})\rightrightarrows(M_{1},\pi_{1})
and
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})\rightrightarrows(M_{2},\pi_{2})
induces a presymplectic Morita equivalence between the groupoids
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{1},\widehat{\Omega}_{1})\rightrightarrows(\widehat{M}_{1},\widehat{L}_{\pi_{1}})
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{2})\rightrightarrows(\widehat{M}_{2},\widehat{L}_{\pi_{2}})
. The latter induces a Morita equivalence between the effectivizations
\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{1})
and
\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
and hence an isomorphism between orbifold fundamental groups. This isomorphism restricts to an isomorphism of Weyl groups.

Example 6.19
.

We can now provide a Morita-equivalence interpretation of Example
6.9
, and find the orbifold fundamental group and Weyl group of the Hamiltonian local model
M=Q/G
. The space
Q
provides a symplectic Morita equivalence between the symplectic groupoids
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
and
(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rtimes G,\omega_{\mathrm{can}})\rightrightarrows\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, where the latter is not s-connected whenever
G
is disconnected. Fixing a maximal torus
T\subset G
, one finds that

\mathcal{B}(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\rtimes G)\cong\mathfrak{t}^{*}\rtimes N(T)

and so we find for the effectivization the action groupoid

\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\rtimes G)\cong\mathfrak{t}^{*}\rtimes N(T)/Z_{G}(T).

Then the previous remark gives

(a)

a computation of the orbifold fundamental group of
B
and the identification of the Weyl group:

\pi^{\mathrm{orb}}_{1}(B)\cong N(T)/Z_{G}(T),\quad\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)\cong W^{0}.

where
W^{0}:=(N(T)\cap G^{0})/T
.
Notice that one also obtains
\Gamma^{\mathrm{lin}}=\pi^{\mathrm{orb}}_{1}(B)
.

(b)

an identification of the orbifold universal cover
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
with
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
, as
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}
-equivariant integral affine manifolds.

In particular, we obtain an identification of the action of
\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
with the classical action of
W^{0}
on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
.

Corollary 6.20
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold of proper type and choose any
{\widehat{x}}=(x,\mathfrak{t}_{x})\in\widehat{M}
.
The image of any standard hyperplane reflection under the morphism
W\to\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
(see Proposition
6.8
) is an abstract reflection.

Proof.

The inclusion of any saturated open subset
(U,\pi|_{U})\to(M,\pi)
maps abstract reflections of
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}(U,\pi|_{U})
to abstract reflections of
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}(M,\pi)
. Therefore it is enough to prove the result for the Hamiltonian local model
M=Q/G
.

By Example

6.11

the standard reflections in
W
are exactly the abstract reflections. By Proposition
6.16
, the symplectic Morita equivalence between
(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\pi_{\mathrm{lin}})
and
(M,\pi)
provided by
Q
, maps isomorphically
W
into
\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
, identifying abstract reflections.
∎

7.
The Weyl Group as a Geometric Reflection Group

Here, as in the previous section, the leaf space
B=\widehat{M}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}=M/\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
is endowed with its classical orbifold structure.

7.1.
Geometric reflections

Our next aim is to show that the abstract reflections can be realised as geometric ones. By a
geometric reflection
on a manifold
N
we mean any smooth involution
r:N\rightarrow N
with the property that the fixed-point set

N^{r}:=\{x\in N:r(x)=x\}

is a codimension one submanifold that separates
N
, i.e.,
N\setminus N^{\tau}
has two connected components.

Definition 7.1
.

A
reflection group
on a manifold
N
is a discrete group acting properly and effectively on
N
and generated by geometric reflections.

When
N
is an integral affine manifold and the group acts by integral affine transformations, we call it an
integral affine reflection group
on
N
.

Given a reflection group
\Gamma
acting on an orientable manifold
N
, we define the
parity character
to be the group homomorphism

\delta:\Gamma\to\mathbb{Z}_{2},

which associates to
\gamma\in\Gamma
the element
1
(respectively,
-1
) if
\gamma
preserves (resp. reverses) orientation. We note that
\delta(\gamma)
coincides with the parity of the decomposition of
\gamma
into a product of reflections.

We will now show that.

Theorem 7.2
.

The Weyl group
\mathcal{W}
of a Poisson manifold of proper type is an integral affine reflection group on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
.

First we recall the following folklore result regarding the separation property.

Lemma 7.3
.

If
N
is a simply connected manifold, then any closed connected codimension one submanifold
Y
is co-orientable and separates
N
.

Proof.

The fact that the normal bundle of
Y
must be trivial is proved in
[
38
]
. By the excision property, the reduced homology group
\widetilde{H}_{0}(N,N\setminus Y)
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{H}_{0}(\nu(Y),\nu(Y)\setminus 0)\cong\mathbb{Z}
, so the result follows.
∎

We have already observed that the action of
\mathcal{W}
on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is proper and effective. Theorem
7.2
now follows from the following proposition and the fact that the Weyl group is generated by abstract reflections – see Proposition
6.12
.

Proposition 7.4
.

The action of any abstract reflection
\tau\in\mathcal{W}
on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is a geometric reflection on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
.

Proof.

Given
\tau\in\mathcal{W}
an abstract reflection and
\widetilde{b}\in\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
a fixed point of
\tau
, we claim that:

(C)

there exists a contractible neighborhood
V
of
\widetilde{b}\in\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
such that

V\cap(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\tau}

is a codimension one submanifold.

Assuming this claim, the result follows. To see this we first notice that
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\tau}\subset\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is a closed embedded submanifold, with connected components possibly of different dimensions. Let
\mathcal{H}
be the component that contains
\widetilde{b}
, which is assumed to be of codimension one. Since
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is simply connected we can apply the previous lemma, so
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\setminus\mathcal{H}
has two connected components and
\mathcal{H}
is the common boundary. Furthermore,
\mathcal{H}
would be orientable. Then since the action of
\tau
on
V
interchanges the two connected components of
V\setminus(V\cap\mathcal{H})
, we conclude that
\tau
interchanges the connected components of
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\setminus\mathcal{H}
. Therefore there cannot be any other fixed point away from
\mathcal{H}
.

We are left with proving (C). For that note that
\tau
comes from a fiber
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)
inside a subregular stratum, i.e.
\tau=\sigma\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}\sigma^{-1}
where
\sigma
joins the base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
with
{\widehat{x}}
and
\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}
is a non-contractible loop in
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)
. We divide the proof into several steps, that reduce the problem to the local Hamiltonian model.

Step 1:
We may assume that
{\widehat{x}}_{0}={\widehat{x}}
and
\tau=\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}
.

This follows from Remark
6.4
and the fact that conjugation by
\sigma
takes
\tau
to
\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}
.

Step 2:
It suffices to prove that the claim holds for
(U,\pi|_{U})
where
U
is a saturated neighborhood of
{\widehat{x}}
invariant by
\tau
.

This is a consequence of the functoriality of the main diagram with respect to restriction to saturated opens (see Remark
5.17
). The inclusion
U\hookrightarrow M
gives group homomorphisms
\phi
between the (orbifold) fundamental groups corresponding to
(U,\pi|_{U})
and
(M,\pi)
.
The maps relating the main diagrams are
\phi
-equivariant. Notice that
\phi
maps the groups
\mathcal{W}(U,\pi|_{U})
to
\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
and sends
\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}\in\mathcal{W}(U,\pi|_{U})
to
\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}\in\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
.

Step 3:
The claim holds when
M=Q/G
is the Hamiltonian local model with
{\widehat{x}}=(x,\mathfrak{t}_{x})

Q
-related to
(0,\mathfrak{t})
.

Since
{\widehat{x}}
a subregular point we have
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{ss}}=\mathfrak{su}(2)
. The discussion in Example
6.19
shows that the Weyl group of
Q/G
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and acts on

\mathfrak{t}^{*}=\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}\oplus{(\mathfrak{t}^{\mathrm{ss}})}^{*}
fixing the first summand. Therefore, the claim holds.
∎

7.2.
The Weyl group as a Coxeter group

The fact that the Weyl group of
(M,\pi)
is an integral affine reflection group on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
has several interesting consequences. To describe them we use several notions from the theory of reflection groups and Coxeter systems (see, e.g.,
[
18
]
). Namely, we will be using the following:

•

set of geometric reflections

\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathcal{W}
.

•

hyperplane

\mathcal{H}_{r}
of
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}},\mathcal{W})
: the fixed point set of a reflection
r\in\mathfrak{R}
.
Any
\mathcal{H}_{r}
is an integral affine hyperplane that separates
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
;

•

\mathcal{W}
-regular point
: any point
u\in\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
which is not fixed by any
w\in\mathcal{W}
. We denote by
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}-{\mathrm{reg}}}
the set of regular points.

•

chamber
of
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}},\mathcal{W})
: the closure of a connected component of the subset of regular points.

•

simple reflection
associated to the chamber
\Delta
: a reflection
r\in\mathfrak{R}
with the property that there is a point
u\in\Delta\cap\mathcal{H}_{r}
such that
r
is the only reflection that fixes
u
. We denote by
\mathfrak{R}_{\Delta}
the collection of all such reflections.

We recall also that a Coxeter system is a group
\mathcal{W}
with a set of generators
\mathfrak{R}_{0}=\{r_{i}:i\in I\}
satisfying relations

(r_{i}r_{j})^{m_{ij}}=1,\quad(i,j\in I),

where
m_{ii}=1
and
m_{ij}=m_{ji}\geq 2
is either an integer or
\infty
for
i\neq j
. The condition
m_{ij}=\infty
means that no relation
(r_{i}r_{j})^{m}=1
for any integer
m\geq 2
is imposed.

Using the general results in
[
17
]
concerning reflection groups one deduces the following result.

Theorem 7.5
.

For a Poisson manifold of proper type, one has:

(i)

(\mathcal{W},\mathfrak{R}_{\Delta})
is a Coxeter system.

(ii)

\mathcal{W}
acts freely and transitively on the set of chambers.

(iii)

\Delta
is a fundamental domain
in the sense that the canonical projection restricts to a homeomorphism
\Delta\cong\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}/\mathcal{W}
. In particular,

\Delta\cdot\mathcal{W}=\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}.

(iv)

the
\mathcal{W}
-regular part is the complement of the hyperplanes:

(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}-{\mathrm{reg}}}=\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\setminus\bigcup_{r\in\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{H}_{r}.

(v)

the isotropy group
\mathcal{W}(u)
at any
u\in\Delta
is a finite group generated by the reflections from
\mathfrak{R}_{\Delta}
that fix
u
. Moreover, if
U
is a
\mathcal{W}(u)
-linear neighborhood of
u
, one has:

(1)

U\cap\Delta
is a chamber for the action of
\mathcal{W}(v)
on
U
;

(2)

\mathfrak{R}_{U\cap\Delta}
coincides with the reflections in
\mathfrak{R}_{\Delta}
which fix
u
.

(vi)

for
r\in\mathfrak{R}_{\Delta}
,
\Delta
is contained in the closure of one of the two components of
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{r}
. Denoting by
H_{r}^{+}(\Delta)
that component, one has

\Delta=\bigcap_{r\in\mathfrak{R}_{\Delta}}H_{r}^{+}(\Delta).

In particular, we have:

Corollary 7.6
.

The Weyl chamber
\Delta
is both a manifold with corners as well as a (classical) orbifold, with the orbifold atlas given by
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\rtimes\mathcal{W}\rightrightarrows\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
.

Next, the action of the entire
\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)
descends to an action of the group

\pi:=\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)/\mathcal{W}

on the chamber
\Delta
, with orbit space

\Delta/\pi\cong B.

Since the action of
\pi
is still proper and effective, one may think of
\Delta\rtimes\pi\rightrightarrows\Delta
as an atlas making
B
into an “orbifold with corners”.

At this point we have two notion of regular points in
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
: the points in
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathrm{reg}}
coming from from the infinitesimal stratification (see the comments preceding Proposition
6.14
), and
the
\mathcal{W}
-regular points described in item (iv) of the last theorem. We now show that the two notions coincide.

Proposition 7.7
.

The following equality holds:

(7.1)

(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathrm{reg}}=(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}-{\mathrm{reg}}}.

Moreover,

(i)

the chambers of
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}},\mathcal{W})
are the connected components of
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathrm{reg}}
;

(ii)

\mathfrak{R}
is precisely the set of all abstract reflections of
\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

We first prove the inclusion
\subset
.
Since
\mathcal{W}
acts transitively on connected components, if an element
w\in\mathcal{W}
fixes a regular point, a conjugate
w^{\prime}
of it fixes a regular point in the connected component
C
of
{(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})}^{\mathrm{reg}}
containing the base point, hence
C\cdot w^{\prime}=C
. Then Proposition
6.14
implies that
w^{\prime}
must be the identity, and therefore the inclusion holds.

At this point we have:

(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}-{\mathrm{reg}}}=\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\setminus\bigcup_{r\in\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{H}_{r}\subset\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\setminus\bigcup_{s\in\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{abs}}}\mathcal{H}_{s}

where
\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{abs}}
is the collection of all abstract reflections. Next we prove that the last term is included in the first one. In other words, we will show that any point
\widetilde{b}\in(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathrm{sing}}
is fixed by some abstract reflection
w\in\mathcal{W}
.

As in the proof of Proposition
7.4
we may assume that
\widetilde{b}
is the base-point of
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
and, using the functoriality, that
M
is the Hamiltonian local model. So let

M=Q/G
with
{\widehat{x}}=(x,\mathfrak{t}_{x})

Q
-related to
(0,\mathfrak{t})
and let
W
denote the Weyl group of
G^{0}
relative to
\mathfrak{t}
. Any standard reflection
w\in W
on a root hyperplane fixes the origin
0\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. By Corollary
6.20
, its image under the inclusion
W\to\mathcal{W}
is the desired abstract reflection.
We conclude that (
7.1
) holds and that

(7.2)

\mathcal{H}_{r}\subset\bigcup_{s\in\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{abs}}}\mathcal{H}_{s}\ \quad\quad\forall\ r\in\mathfrak{R}.

Item (i) follows immediately from the (
7.1
). As for item (ii), let
r\in\mathfrak{R}
be any geometric reflection and consider a neighborhood of a point
\widetilde{b}\in\mathcal{H}_{r}
. Since the action of
\mathcal{W}
can be linearized around
\widetilde{b}
and all the hyperplanes through
\widetilde{b}
become linear hyperplanes, it follows from (
7.2
) that a neighborhood of
\widetilde{b}
in
\mathcal{H}_{r}
is inside a
neighborhood of
\widetilde{b}
in some
\mathcal{H}_{s}
with
s\in\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{abs}}
. In the linearised neighborhood,
r
and
s
will be linear reflections on the same hyperplane and, therefore,
rs^{-1}
will be either the identity or will have infinite order. Using properness and effectiveness of the action, the isotropy group
\mathcal{W}_{\widetilde{b}}
is finite and we must have
rs^{-1}=1
.
∎

The previous results justify the following definition.

Definition 7.8
.

Given a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
of proper type, a
Weyl chamber
of
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is the closure of a connected component of the regular locus of
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
.

It follows from Propositions
6.14
and
7.7
that the orbifold fundamental group of the embedded integral affine orbifold
B^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset B
can identified with the subgroup of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}},b_{0})
which stabilizes the Weyl chamber
\Delta
containing
\tilde{b}_{0}
. The next result identifies the orbifold universal covering space of
B^{\mathrm{reg}}

Proposition 7.9
.

An open Weyl chamber
{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta)\subset\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is an orbifold universal covering space of
B^{\mathrm{reg}}
. In particular
{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta)
is simply connected.

Proof.

We can assume that
\Delta
is the Weyl chamber containing the base point
\tilde{b}_{0}
. Let us represent
B^{\mathrm{reg}}
by the orbifold atlas

{(B^{\mathrm{reg}})}^{\mathrm{lin}}\rtimes\Gamma_{0}^{\mathrm{lin}}\rightrightarrows(B^{\mathrm{reg}})^{\mathrm{lin}}

where
\Gamma_{0}^{\mathrm{lin}}
is the linear holonomy group of
(\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{reg}},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
. Note that it sits as a subgroup of
\Gamma^{\mathrm{lin}}
.

The inclusion
i:Y:=(B^{\mathrm{reg}})^{\mathrm{lin}}\hookrightarrow B^{\mathrm{lin}}
(see Prop.
5.17
) induces a map
\widetilde{i}:\widetilde{Y}\to\widetilde{B^{\mathrm{lin}}}=\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}

between orbifold universal covering spaces which is
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}
-equivariant. We have the following diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{i}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{i}(\widetilde{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B^{\mathrm{lin}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{lin}}}

where the left column is a sequence of covering maps among (connected) manifolds. This has two consequences:

(i)

the open subset
\widetilde{i}(\widetilde{Y})\subset\widetilde{B^{\mathrm{lin}}}
is the connected component of
p^{-1}(Y)
which contains the base-point. By Proposition
7.7
this connected component is exactly the interior of the chamber
\Delta
;

(ii)

\tilde{i}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image if and only if the induced map on fundamental groups
i_{*}:\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(B^{\mathrm{lin}})
is injective. If this holds, the proof of the proposition would follow.

To prove the injectivity of
i_{*}
we use the short exact sequences
relating fundamental groups and orbifold fundamental groups (see
6.6
):

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(B^{\mathrm{lin}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\mathrm{lin}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}((B^{\mathrm{reg}})^{\mathrm{lin}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\mathrm{lin}}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

By Proposition
6.14
the middle vertical arrow is injective, so the injectivity of
i_{*}
follows.
∎

In general, the Weyl group
\mathcal{W}
of a PMCT (or a DMCT) is not a classical (affine) Weyl group. However, as another application of Theorem
7.5
, we show that its isotropy subgroups are classical Weyl groups. Namely, the isotropy of
\mathcal{W}
at a point
{\widehat{x}}=(x,\mathfrak{t}_{x})
coincides with the classical Weyl group of the isotropy Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
relative to
\mathfrak{t}_{x}
. This is made more precise in the following result which improves Proposition
6.8
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
\tilde{b}\in\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
, fix any
{\widehat{x}}=(x,\mathfrak{t}_{x})\in\widehat{M}
mapping to the image of
\tilde{b}
in
B
and let
W
be the classical Weyl group relative to
\mathfrak{t}_{x}
.

Then there exists open neighborhoods
V_{b}
of
b\in\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
and
V
of
0\in\mathfrak{t}_{x}^{*}
, a diffeomorphism

\varphi:V\to V_{\widetilde{b}}

and a monomorphism
\phi:W\to\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
,
with the following properties:

(i)

\varphi
is
\phi
-equivariant;

(ii)

\phi
yields an isomorphism between the regular parts
{(\mathfrak{t}_{x}^{*})}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and
V_{b}\cap{(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})}^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

In particular,
\phi
is an isomorphism from
W
to the stabilizer
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}(\widetilde{b})
.

Proof.

We first prove that the existence of
\varphi
and
\phi
as in the statement implies the equality

(7.3)

\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}(\widetilde{b})=\phi(W).

To see this, by item (v) in Theorem
7.5
, the stabilizer
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}(\tilde{b})
is generated by all reflections which fix
\widetilde{b}
. In turn, each reflection is characterized by its fixed point set. By Proposition
7.7
the regular and
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
-regular parts agree. Therefore, every reflection hyperplane containing
\widetilde{b}
is the
\varphi
-image of a classical reflection hyperplane in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{x}
. Hence,
\varphi
identifies the corresponding reflection of
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
with the image by
\phi
of a classical reflection of
W
. The equality (
7.3
) follows.

Moreover, since the action of
W
on
\mathfrak{t}_{x}^{*}
is effective, it follows that
\phi
is a monomorphism.

To complete the proof we show the existence of a homomorphism
\phi:W\to\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
and a diffeomorphism
\varphi:V\to V_{\widetilde{b}}
which is
\phi
-equivariant. Since the action of
W
on
\mathfrak{t}_{x}^{*}
is effective, it follows that
\phi
is a monomorphism. This is divided into 3 steps, that reduce the problem to the local model:

1)

We may assume that
{\widehat{x}}_{0}={\widehat{x}}
(and therefore
\widetilde{b}=\widetilde{b}_{0}
). This is a consequence of the fact that conjugation by a path in
\widehat{M}
joining the base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
with
{\widehat{x}}
produces a
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}
-equivariant diffeomorphism between based orbifold universal covering spaces.

2)

It suffices to contruct the equivariant diffeomorphism for
(U,\pi|_{U})
, where
U

is an invariant neighborhood of
{\widehat{x}}
for which
B_{U}^{\mathrm{lin}}
is simply connected. This is a consequence of the functoriality of the main diagram with respect to restriction to invariant opens (see Remark
5.17
). We obtain a group homomorphisms
\phi
between the (orbifold) fundamental groups corresponding to
(U,\pi|_{U})
and
(M,\pi)
taking
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}(U,\pi|_{U})
to
\mathcal{W}_{{\widehat{x}}}(M,\pi)
. Because
B_{U}^{\mathrm{lin}}
is simply connected, it follows that the map
\psi:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{U}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
in diagram (
5.18
) is an equivariant embedding. Observe that regular points are obtained by pulling back
B^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset B
, thus
\psi({(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{U})}^{\mathrm{reg}})=\psi(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{U})\cap{(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})}^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

3)

The claim holds when
M=Q/G
is the Hamiltonian local model with
{\widehat{x}}=(x,\mathfrak{t}_{x})

Q
-related to
(0,\mathfrak{t}_{x})
. This is shown in Example
6.19
.

∎

8.
Some Examples Derived from Lie Theory

In this section we first analyze the structures on the leaf spaces of two fundamental examples from Lie theory: the linear Poisson structure on a compact Lie algebra
and the Cartan-Dirac structure on a compact Lie group. We will see, for example, that our notions of Weyl group and Weyl chambers coincide with the classical ones. We will also relate other Poisson theoretic aspects to classical results in Lie theory, leaving verifications to the reader.
A few of these results have already appeared in the text, but we find convenient to list them together here in a more detailed manner.
Also, the regular loci of these two examples were discussed in
[
12
, Section 4.5 and 5.3.2]
.

In the remaining of the section we will consider PMCTs and DMCTs obtained from
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
G
by removing saturated closed subsets, and we will discuss how that affects the Weyl group and other related notions.

8.1.
Coadjoint orbits

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a compact Lie algebra.
If
G
is a compact connected Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, then
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)=(T^{*}G,\omega_{\mathrm{can}})
is a s-proper integration of

(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}})
. We will use root data to describe the integral affine orbifold structure of the leaf space
B=\mathfrak{g}^{*}/\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
and of the members of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(B)
, and the Weyl group of
(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}})
. For the relevant background on Lie theory we refer the reader to
[
22
, Chapter 3]
. We first enumerate the main results.

(i)

There is a canonical diffeomorphism

G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*}\cong\widehat{M},

where
T
is the isotropy Lie group of the fixed base point, a point in the regular part, and
\mathfrak{t}
its Lie algebra. Also,
W=N(T)/T
is the Weyl group.
Therefore

B=\mathfrak{t}^{*}/W,\quad\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}=B^{\mathrm{lin}}\cong\mathfrak{t}^{*}.

(ii)

A (classical) integral affine orbifold atlas for
B
is given by

(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{G}^{\vee})\rtimes W\rightrightarrows\mathfrak{t}^{*},

where
\Lambda_{G}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}^{*}
is the lattice dual to

\Ker(\exp:\mathfrak{t}\to T)
.

(iii)

The Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and the orbifold fundamental group of
B

agree, and coincide with the (classical) Weyl group:

\mathcal{W}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\cong W.

The orbifold fundamental group of
B^{\mathrm{reg}}
is trivial, this being reflected in the triviality of the short exact sequence

(8.1)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

(iv)

The canonical and the infinitesimal stratifications of
B
agree. Their strata are in bijection with the open faces of a fixed Weyl chamber
\Delta
. If
\Sigma_{I}
denotes the stratum determined by the face
\Delta_{I}
with underlying vector subspace
\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{I}\subset\mathfrak{t}^{*}
, then an orbifold atlas for
B_{\Sigma_{I}}
is

(\mathrm{int}(\Delta_{I}),\Lambda_{G,I}^{\vee})\rtimes W_{I}.

Here
W_{I}\subset W
denotes the subgroup generated by the reflections fixing
\Delta_{I}
pointwise and
\Lambda^{\vee}_{G,I}=\Lambda_{G}^{\vee}\cap\mathfrak{t}_{I}^{*}
. In particular, the non-regular strata are purely ineffective orbifolds.

In order to prove the previous assertions, we start by recalling the Weyl resolution of the PMCT
(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}})
from Example
4.3
:

\widehat{M}=\{(\xi,\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})\,|\,\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*},\,\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}\,\,\mathrm{maximal}\,\,\mathrm{torus}\},\quad{\mathscr{R}}=\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\widehat{M}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}.

We have identifications

G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*}\cong G\times_{N(T)}\mathfrak{t}^{*}\cong\widehat{M},\quad[(gT,\xi)]\mapsto(\Ad^{*}_{g}\xi,\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{t}).

The groupoid integrating the resolution is the action groupoid

(8.2)

\widehat{\mathcal{G}}=G\ltimes(G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*})\rightrightarrows G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*}

associated to left
G
-translations on
G/T
. The associated foliation groupoid is

(8.3)

\mathcal{B}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})=(G/T\times G/T\times\mathfrak{t}^{*})/W\rightrightarrows G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*},\quad\mathbf{s}=\mathrm{pr}_{12},\,\mathbf{t}=\mathrm{pr}_{13}.

Observe that the obvious map
\mathfrak{t}^{*}\hookrightarrow G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*}
gives a complete transversal to the orbit foliation of
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
. Upon restriction to this transversal, one obtains a presymplectic groupoid
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}|_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}},i^{*}\widehat{\Omega})
Morita equivalent to
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\widehat{\Omega})
. The isotropy bundle of
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}|_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}}
is the trivial bundle with fiber
N(T)
, while the associated foliation groupoid is the action groupoid

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G}|_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}})=\mathfrak{t}^{*}\rtimes W\rightrightarrows\mathfrak{t}^{*}.

This action groupoid is effective, since the action of
W
on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
is effective (recall that
G
is connected). It follows that the foliation groupoid (
8.3
) is effective, so it is the holonomy groupoid of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
.
The identity component of the isotropy bundle is the trivial bundle,
\mathcal{T}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}|_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}})=\mathfrak{t}^{*}\times T\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
, and the pullback of the presymplectic form to this bundle is the canonical duality pairing between
\mathfrak{t}

and
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
.
We conclude that the induced integral affine structure on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
has (constant) lattice
\Lambda_{G}^{\vee}
dual to
\Ker(\exp:\mathfrak{t}\to T)
.

In this way we obtain a (classical) integral affine orbifold atlas for the leaf space
B
(c.f.
[
12
, Section 4.6.1]
):

(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{G}^{\vee})\rtimes W.

Being simply connected, this is also the universal orbifold covering space for
B
, therefore

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}=B^{\mathrm{lin}}\cong\mathfrak{t}^{*},

and

\mathcal{W}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)=W,\quad\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})=1.

This recovers the results we found in Example
6.5
using the simple connectivity of principal leaves of the resolution.

The equality of the canonical stratifications can be drawn from classical Lie theory: the infinitesimal orbit types and the orbit types agree because isotropy groups are always connected, since
G
is connected.

Next we describe the infinitesimal stratification
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(B)
.
An explicit computation in terms of root spaces (see
[
22
, Page 153]
) implies that all points of each open face
{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I})
share the same isotropy Lie algebra
\mathfrak{p}_{I}
. It also implies that
the saturation of
{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I})\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a stratum
\Sigma_{I}
admitting the canonical parametrization

(8.4)

G/P_{I}\times{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I})\to\Sigma_{I},\quad(gP_{I},\xi)\mapsto\Ad_{g}^{*}\xi.

Similarly, we have a parametrization of the corresponding strata of the resolution, namely

(G/T)/W_{I}\times{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I})\to{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{I},\quad([gT],\xi)\mapsto(\Ad_{g}^{*}\xi,\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{t}),

where
W_{I}\subset W
is the subgroup that fixes pointwise the supporting vector space
\mathfrak{t}_{I}^{*}
of
\Delta_{I}
. We conclude that
B_{\Sigma_{I}}={\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I})
, as topological spaces.

By Theorem
5.8
the reduction of
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\widehat{\Omega})
along
{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{I}

makes
B_{\Sigma_{I}}
into an integral affine embedded suborbifold of
B
.
Namely, restricting the groupoid (
8.3
) to
{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{I}
, we obtain the foliation groupoid

(G/T\times G/T\times{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I}))/W_{I}\to(G/T)/W_{I}\times{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I}).

Using the complete transversal
{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I})\hookrightarrow{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{I}
, we obtain that this groupoid is Morita equivalent to the (purely ineffective) action groupoid

{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I})\rtimes W_{I}\rightrightarrows{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I}).

This gives the desired orbifold structure for
B_{\Sigma_{I}}
. For its integral affine structure, we observe that the isotropy bundle of the reduced symplectic groupoid trivializes upon restriction to the complete transversal,
with fiber
T/(T\cap[P_{I},P_{I}])
. From
the explicit description of
\mathfrak{p}_{I}
we obtain the decomposition

\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{p}_{I})\oplus\mathfrak{t}\cap[\mathfrak{p}_{I},\mathfrak{p}_{I}],\quad\mathfrak{t}\cap[\mathfrak{p}_{i},\mathfrak{p}_{I}]=(\mathfrak{t}_{I}^{*})^{0}.

The first identity gives the identification
Z(P_{I})\cong T/(T\cap[P_{I},P_{I}])
, and the second one shows that the lattice

\Ker(\exp:\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{p}_{I})\to Z(P_{I}))
is identified, via the presymplectic form at units, with

\Lambda_{G,I}^{\vee}=\Lambda_{G}^{\vee}\cap\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{I}
. We now conclude that we have the integral orbifold atlas for
B_{\Sigma_{I}}

({\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I}),\Lambda_{G,I}^{\vee})\rtimes W_{I}.

Comparing with the integral orbifold atlas for
B
obtained above, we see that
B_{\Sigma_{I}}
is an embedded suborbifold of
B
.

A different integral affine orbifold structure on
B_{\Sigma_{I}}
is obtained by using the reduction of
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
along
\Sigma_{I}
. Since the leaves of
\mathcal{G}
are simply connected, the associated foliation groupoid must be the holonomy groupoid of the simple foliation
G/P_{I}\times{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I})
. Therefore, the orbifold structure on
B_{\Sigma_{I}}={\mathrm{int}}(\Delta_{I})
arising from this reduction is the usual manifold structure. Note that the integral affine structure is still given by the lattice
\Delta_{G,I}^{\vee}
.

In conclusion, reduction of
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\widehat{\Omega})
along
\Sigma_{I}
produces a purely ineffective integral affine orbifold atlas for
B_{\Sigma_{I}}

whose isotropy reduction is the integral affine orbifold atlas obtained from reduction of
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
along
\Sigma_{I}
. This concurs with item (i) of Proposition

5.11
.

8.2.
Conjugacy classes

Consider a compact, connected, Lie group
G
, let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be an
\Ad
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
, and let
L_{G}
be the corresponding
Cartan-Dirac structure on
G
with twisting the Cartan 3-form. We will describe the Weyl resolution, the integral affine orbifold structure of the leaf space, and the Weyl group and orbifold fundamental group in terms of classical Lie theoretic data, leaving the verifications for the reader; we shall also sketch the description of the members of the canonical stratifications. Our viewpoint shall provide a geometric interpretation of some of these classical results. For the material in Lie theory that we will use we refer the reader to
[
22
, Chapter 3]
and
[
7
, Chapter 5]
.
We first enumerate the main results:

(i)

There is a canonical diffeomorphism

G\times_{N(T)}T\cong\widehat{M},

where
T\subset G
is the centralizer of the fixed base point, a point in the principal part – so
T
is a maximal torus of
G
.
Therefore

B=T/W,\quad B^{\mathrm{lin}}\cong T,\quad\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\cong\mathfrak{t},

where
W=N(T)/T
is the Weyl group.

(ii)

One has a (classical) integral affine orbifold atlas for
B
given by

(T,\Lambda_{G}^{*})\rtimes W\rightrightarrows T,

where

\Lambda_{G}^{*}=\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{t}\,|\,\langle u,\lambda\rangle\in\mathbb{Z},\,\forall u\in\Lambda_{G}\},

with
\Lambda_{G}=\Ker(\exp:\mathfrak{t}\to T)
.

(iii)

The Weyl group of
(G,L_{G})
coincides with the classical affine Weyl group of
G
, while the orbifold fundamental group of
B
coincides with the (classical) extended affine Weyl group of
G
:

\mathcal{W}=W^{\mathrm{aff}}=W\cdot\Lambda_{\mathrm{R}^{\vee}},\quad\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)=W^{\mathrm{aff}}_{G}=W\cdot\Lambda_{G},

where
\Lambda_{\mathrm{R}^{\vee}}
denotes the coroot lattice of the root system of
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{t})
. When
G
is simply connected, the two groups coincide and we recover the results in Example
6.6
. On the other hand, this also yields another integral affine orbifold atlas for
B
, namely

(\mathfrak{t},\Lambda_{G}^{*})\rtimes W^{\mathrm{aff}}_{G}\rightrightarrows\mathfrak{t}.

Moreover, the orbifold fundamental group of
B^{\mathrm{reg}}
coincides with
\pi_{1}(G)
and all these groups fit into the following commutative diagram

(8.5)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{\mathrm{R}^{\vee}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}

In the two vertical short exact sequences, the kernel and the cokernel correspond to the translational and linear parts of
\mathcal{W}
and
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
, viewed as groups of affine transformations of
\mathfrak{t}
.

(iv)

According to Proposition
6.14
, the inclusion of the regular part
G^{\mathrm{reg}}
in
G
splits the short exact sequence of the orbifold fundamental group, giving a short exact sequence for
\pi_{1}(G)
:

(8.6)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\,\Lambda_{G}\cap\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\ \pi_{1}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\quad Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\mathrm{lin}}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}

The translational part of
\pi_{1}(G)
defines a (compact) covering group
\overline{G}
which factors into a central and a 1-connected semisimple factor

(8.7)

\overline{G}:=G(\mathfrak{g})/(\Lambda_{G}\cap\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}))=H\times G(\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{ss}}).

The linear part of
\pi_{1}(G)
defines a finite abelian group
Z\subset W
which acts on the faces of the Weyl alcove
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})\oplus\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}
determined by the choice of base point. Note also that

\overline{G}/Z\to G

is an isomorphism.

(v)

The strata of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(B)
are in bijection with the orbits of action of
Z
on the faces of the Weyl alcove. If
\Sigma_{[I]}
denotes the stratum determined by the face
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
of the alcove with underlying vector subspace
\mathfrak{t}_{I}\subset\mathfrak{t}
, then an orbifold atlas for
B_{\Sigma_{[I]}}
is

(H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{\mathrm{ss}}),\Lambda_{G,I}^{*})\rtimes W_{I}Z_{I}

where
Z_{I}
is the
Z
-stabilizer of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
,
W_{I}\subset W
is the subgroup generated by the reflections fixing
\mathfrak{t}_{I}
pointwise and
\Lambda^{*}_{G,I}=\Lambda_{G}^{*}\cap\mathfrak{t}_{I}
.

In order to justify these assertions, we start by discussing the Weyl resolution of
(G,L_{G})
. From Example
4.20
we have

\widehat{M}=\{(h,T^{\prime})\,|\,h\in T^{\prime},\,T^{\prime}\subset G\,\,\mathrm{maximal}\,\,\mathrm{torus}\},\quad{\mathscr{R}}=\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\widehat{M}\to G,

and, upon fixing a principal point with centralizer the maximal torus
T
, an identification

G\times_{N(T)}T=G/T\times_{W}T\cong\widehat{M},\quad[(g,h)]\mapsto(ghg^{-1},gTg^{-1}).

The groupoid integrating the resolution is the action groupoid

(8.8)

\widehat{\mathcal{G}}=G\ltimes(G/T\times_{W}T)\rightrightarrows G/T\times_{W}T

associated to left
G
-translations on
G/T
. The associated foliation groupoid is

(8.9)

\mathcal{B}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})=(G/T\times G/T\times T)/W\rightrightarrows G/T\times_{W}T,\quad\mathbf{s}=\mathrm{pr}_{12},\,\mathbf{t}=\mathrm{pr}_{13}.

Observe that the obvious map
T\hookrightarrow G/T\times_{W}T
gives a complete transversal to the orbit foliation of
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
. Upon restriction to this transversal, one obtains a presymplectic groupoid
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}|_{T},i^{*}\widehat{\Omega})
Morita equivalent to
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\widehat{\Omega})
. The background 3-form pulls back to zero under the inclusion
T\hookrightarrow G/T\times_{W}T
, so
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}|_{T},i^{*}\widehat{\Omega})
is untwisted. The isotropy bundle of
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}|_{T}
is the trivial bundle with fiber
N(T)
, while the associated foliation groupoid is the action groupoid

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G}|_{T})=T\rtimes W\rightrightarrows T.

This action groupoid is effective, since the action of
W
on
T
is effective. It follows that the foliation groupoid (
8.9
) is effective, so it is the holonomy groupoid of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
.
The identity component
\mathcal{T}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}|_{T})
is the trivial bundle
T\times T\to T
, and the pull back of the presymplectic form to this bundle is the symplectic form

i^{*}\widehat{\Omega}_{(h_{1},h_{2})}((u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2}))=\langle u_{1},v_{2}\rangle-\langle v_{1},u_{2}\rangle.

Therefore the induced integral affine structure on
T
has lattice at
h\in T
the translation of

\Lambda_{G}^{*}=\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{t}\,|\,\langle u,\lambda\rangle\in\mathbb{Z},\,\forall u\in\Lambda_{G}\}\subset\mathfrak{t}

(c.f.
[
12
, Section 4.6.2]
).

In this way we obtain the (classical) integral affine orbifold atlas for
B

(T,\Lambda_{G}^{*})\rtimes W,

with universal orbifold covering space

(\mathfrak{t},\Lambda_{G}^{*})\rtimes\pi^{\mathrm{orb}}_{1}(B).

In particular, we have

B^{\mathrm{lin}}\cong T,\quad\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=B^{\mathrm{aff}}\cong\mathfrak{t}.

Moreover, we also obtain the decomposition of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
into translational and linear parts (c.f. (
6.6
))

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1.}

This extension of the Weyl group by the lattice of
G
coincides with
W^{\mathrm{aff}}_{G}
, the extended affine Weyl group of
G
.

Next, we also relate
\mathcal{W}
to well-known groups in Lie theory.
Recall that
\exp^{-1}(T\setminus(T\cap G^{\mathrm{reg}}))
is the union of the affine root hyperplanes of the root system
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{t})
(hyperplanes where the roots attain integer multiples of
2\pi i
). The Weyl group
\mathcal{W}
is generated by (orthogonal) reflections on the affine root hyperplanes, and therefore it coincides with the classical affine Weyl group
W^{\mathrm{aff}}
. Its decomposition according to the translational and linear parts of the affine action gives the first column in (
8.5
)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{\mathrm{R}^{\vee}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1.}

Proposition

6.14
implies that the image of the splitting

i:\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)=W^{\mathrm{aff}}_{G}

are the elements of
W^{\mathrm{aff}}_{G}
leaving the Weyl alcove
\mathfrak{a}
stable. Since
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})\oplus\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}
and the second factor is a compact polytope, we conclude that the translation part of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})

is
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})\cap\Lambda_{G}
. Since the principal leaves of
G^{\mathrm{reg}}
are 1-connected, we can apply Lemma
6.1
to conclude that we have isomorphisms

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})\cong\pi_{1}(G^{\mathrm{reg}})\cong\pi_{1}(G).

This yields the right column of diagram (
8.6
), as well as (by diagram chasing) the monomorphism

i^{\mathrm{lin}}:Z\to W.

This also shows that the group
\overline{G}
defined by (
8.7
) is a covering group of
G
.

There is a second Lie theoretical description of
i^{\mathrm{lin}}
. First, we assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is semisimple and
G
is the adjoint group of
\mathfrak{g}
. We denote by
T^{\prime}\subset G(\mathfrak{g})
the maximal torus above
T
. On the one hand, we have a free and transitive action of the Weyl group on the connected components of
T^{\prime}\cap G(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathrm{reg}}
. On the other hand, we have another action by left/right translation of
Z(G(\mathfrak{g}))
on the connected components of
T^{\prime}\cap G(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Upon selecting the connected component containing the fixed base point and comparing the two actions, we get

i_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{lin}}:Z(G(\mathfrak{g}))\to W.

For an arbitrary compact group
G
we have

i^{\mathrm{lin}}=i^{\mathrm{lin}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{ss}}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}|_{Z},

where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\overline{G}\to G(\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{ss}})
is the projection in the second factor in
(
8.7
). The fact that this coincides with the previous definition of
i^{\mathrm{lin}}
follows along the same lines as the discussion in
[
22
, Corollary 3.9.5]
.

Next we describe the infinitesimal stratification
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(B)
, i.e., the one given by infinitesimal orbit types.
The explicit computation of isotropy Lie algebras in terms of root spaces
[
22
, Page 161]
implies that they are constant on the points which are in the image of the interior of each face
\exp({\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I}))
, where the interior of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
is relative to its supporting affine subspace. It follows that if
G=\overline{G}
then the faces of
\mathfrak{a}
parametrize the members of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(B)
. In general one has to account for the action of
Z
on the faces of
\mathfrak{a}
(equivalently, of
\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}
). To describe this action observe first that the covering map

\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{t}^{\mathrm{ss}}\to H\times\mathfrak{t}^{\mathrm{ss}}
sends the Weyl alcove onto
H\times\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}
, inducing a bijection on faces (the faces of
H\times\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}

being the preimage of the faces of
\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}
by the second projection). Diagram (
8.5
) allows to construct an action of

Z
on
H\times\mathfrak{t}^{\mathrm{ss}}
by integral affine transformations that preserves
H\times\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}
: for any element of
Z
one chooses a representative of it on
\pi_{1}(G)
, applies
i
to the representative, and then projects the corresponding (integral affine map) to an integral affine map on
H\times\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}
(which acts trivially on the first component).
Then the strata of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(B)
are parametrized by the orbits of
Z
acting on the faces of
\mathfrak{a}
.

Let
[I]
denote one such orbit and let
\Sigma_{[I]}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(G)
be its associated stratum. To describe it we denote by
Z_{I}
the isotropy group of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
for the action of
Z
on the faces of
\mathfrak{a}
, by
\mathfrak{p}_{I}\subset\mathfrak{g}
the isotropy Lie algebra on points in
\exp({\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I}))
and by
P_{I}\subset G
its connected integration. To parameterize
\Sigma_{[I]}
we take first its preimage by
\kappa:\overline{G}\to G/Z
, obtaining a disconnected union of manifolds

G/P_{J}\times(H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{\mathrm{ss}})),\quad J\in[I].

It then follows that we can parameterize the strata by the diffeomorphism

(8.10)

G/P_{I}\times_{Z_{I}}(H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{\mathrm{ss}}))\to\Sigma_{[I]},\quad[(gP_{I},h,u)]\mapsto g\kappa\big(h\exp_{\overline{G}}(u)\big)g^{-1},

where the action of
Z_{I}
on
G/P_{I}
is via
i^{\mathrm{lin}}
.

Lifting (
8.10
) along the resolution map gives a parameterization of the corresponding strata of the resolution, namely

{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{[I]}\cong((G/T)/W_{I})\times_{Z_{I}}(H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{\mathrm{ss}})),

where
W_{I}\subset W
is the linear part of the subgroup of
W^{\mathrm{aff}}
generated by reflections on affine root hyperplanes containing
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
, and we use that
Z_{I}
normalizes
W_{I}
.

The orbifold integral affine structure on
B_{\Sigma_{[I]}}\in S^{\mathrm{inf}}(B)

comes from the reduction of (
8.8
) along
{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{[I]}
. Namely, from (
8.9
) we obtain first the foliation groupoid

(G/T\times G/T\times(H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{\mathrm{ss}}))/W_{I}Z_{I}\rightrightarrows((G/T)/W_{I})\times_{Z_{I}}(H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{\mathrm{ss}})).

This is Morita equivalent to the ineffective orbifold

(H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{\mathrm{ss}}))\rtimes W_{I}Z_{I},

which gives the desired orbifold atlas for
B_{\Sigma_{[I]}}
. Next, for its integral affine structure, we consider the embedding

H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}_{I})\to G,\quad(h,u)\mapsto[h\exp_{\overline{G}(u)}],

which is a full transversal to the presymplectic foliation. The presymplectic torus bundle over
{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{[I]}
pull backs to this transversal to a trivial bundle with fiber isomorphic to
Z(P_{I})
. Its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{p}_{I})
is the vector subspace underlying
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}_{I}
,
and one checks that contraction by the presymplectic form followed by duality takes
\Lambda_{G,I}=\Ker(\exp:\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{p}_{I})\to Z(P_{I}))
to

\Lambda_{G}^{*}\cap\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{p}_{I})
, which yields the desired integral affine structure on
B_{\Sigma_{[I]}}
.

We finish the discussion on conjugacy classes with three comments about strata:

•

The action of
Z
by permutations of the vertices of the alcove is explicitly determined for every simple Lie group (see
[
28
]
), which
describes equivalently the action on codimension one faces). Therefore one has an explicit description of the members of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(B)
and their integral affine orbifold structures for every given compact Lie group.

•

The partition of
B_{\Sigma_{[I]}}
by canonical strata is given by the orbit type stratification of the action of
Z_{I}
on
H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I})
.

•

The integral affine orbifold atlas on
B_{\Sigma_{[I]}}
obtained from the reduction of
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
along
\Sigma_{[I]}
is

(H\times{\mathrm{int}}(\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{\mathrm{ss}}),\Lambda_{G}^{*}\cap\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{p}_{I}))\rtimes Z_{I}.

Notice that this is an isotropy reduction (by
W_{I}
) of the action groupoid in item (v).
It is effective if and only if
Z_{I}
acts effectively on
\mathfrak{a}^{\mathrm{ss}}_{I}
. This is equivalent to
\Sigma_{[I]}
have 1-connected principal leaves, necessarily diffeomorphic to
G/P_{I}
. The holonomy and monodromy groupoids of such a (proper) foliation must coincide.

8.3.
Removal of closed saturated subsets

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold of proper type with fixed symplectic integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
. The restriction of this symplectic groupoid to any open saturated submanifold
M_{0}\subset M
is a symplectic integration of
(M_{0},\pi)
. Let
B_{0}
denote the leaf pace
\widehat{M}_{0}/\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
. The relation
between the Weyl groups of these two PMCTs and of the orbifold fundamental groups of their leaf spaces is described in the following commutative diagram of split short exact sequences:

(8.11)

\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(M_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(M^{\mathrm{reg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})}

Note that on has also a local diffeomorphism

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}},

which is equivariant relative to the group morphism
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
.

We assume from now on that
M^{\mathrm{reg}}=M^{\mathrm{reg}}_{0}
, so the top and bottom horizontal diagrams become morphisms between split extensions of the groups
\pi_{1}(M^{\mathrm{reg}})
and
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})
, respectively.

Example 8.1
.

Consider the Poisson manifold

M:=(\mathbb{R}\times\mathfrak{su}(2))^{*}\cong\mathbb{R}\times\mathfrak{su}^{*}(2),

with the linear Poisson structure. We can take
T^{*}(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathrm{SU}(2))\rightrightarrows M
as an integration of this Poisson manifold, so it is of s-proper type. Since the maximal rank of the Poisson structure is
2
, there are only regular and subregular strata. The origin is a subregular point and we let

M_{0}:=M\setminus\{0\}.

It follows from Section
8.1
, that the resolutions of these Poisson manifolds can be identified with

\widehat{M}=(\mathbb{S}^{2}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\widehat{M}_{0}=(\mathbb{S}^{2}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}),

where the non-trivial element of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
by the antipodal map and on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

(x,y)\mapsto(x,-y).

Here
\mathbb{R}^{2}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathfrak{t}^{*}
where
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{su}(2)
is a maximal torus. Henceforth, we identify
\mathbb{R}^{2}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
so the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
becomes
z\mapsto\overline{z}
.

The leaf spaces are now

\textstyle{B_{0}=(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ B=\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}_{2},}

with orbifold atlas the action groupoids

\mathbb{C}\ltimes(\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\quad\mathbb{C}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2},

with projections
p_{0}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
p:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
given by

p_{0}(z)=[e^{2\pi z}],\quad p(z)=[z],

and where
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts in both cases by complex conjugation and
n\in\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{C}
acts by translations
z\mapsto z+in
. Since
M
and
M_{0}
are 1-connected, it follows that the Weyl groups coincide with the orbifold fundamental groups and, using (
6.6
), we obtain:

\mathcal{W}_{0}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})=\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad\mathcal{W}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)=\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Furthermore the morphism
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
in the diagram is the projection

\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

At the level of the universal orbifold covering spaces, both diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}
, one now obtains the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{i}}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}

where the top horizontal arrow
\tilde{i}
is the map

z\mapsto e^{2\pi z}.

This map is equivariant relative to the actions of the orbifold fundamental groups.

There is only one reflection in
\mathcal{W}
, whose fixed hyperplane is the real axis
\{x=0\}
. Hence we obtain two Weyl chambers
\Delta_{\pm}
, namely the closed upper and lower half-planes in
\mathbb{C}
. On the other hand, in
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}
by Proposition
7.1
the hyperplanes are the connected components of the preimage
p_{0}^{-1}(B_{0}^{\mathrm{sing}})=p_{0}^{-1}(\{y=0\})
, namely

\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\big\{x+i\tfrac{n}{2}:x\in\mathbb{R}\big\},\quad(n\in\mathbb{Z}),

where the even
\mathcal{H}_{n}
are mapped into the half line
\big\{(x,0):x>0\big\}
and the odd ones into the negative half line
\big\{(x,0):x<0\big\}
. Hence the chambers in
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}
the strips

\Delta_{n}:=\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}:\tfrac{n}{2}\leq y\leq\tfrac{n+1}{2}\},\quad(n\in\mathbb{Z}).

Notice that the translation
(1,0)\in\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
sends
\Delta_{n}
to
\Delta_{n+2}
, while the reflection
r_{0}=(0,[1])
sends
\Delta_{n}
to
\Delta_{-n}
; this is consistent with the fact that the Weyl group acts freely and transitively on the set of Weyl chambers.
The set
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathcal{W}_{0}
consist of the reflections of the form
r_{n}(x,y)=\left(x,n-y\right)
,
(n\in\mathbb{Z})
. The reflections
\mathfrak{R}_{\Delta_{n}}=\{r_{n},r_{n+1}\}
give a Coxeter system with solo relations

r_{n}^{2}=r_{n+1}^{2}=1.

This describes
\mathcal{W}
as the free product
\mathbb{Z}_{2}*\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

The integral lattice on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is a product lattice
\Lambda=\lambda_{1}\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}x+\lambda_{2}\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{d}y
, for fixed positive numbers
\lambda_{i}>0
. Its pullback by the map
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}

is the integral lattice defining the integral affine structure on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}
. The Weyl groups clearly act by integral affine transformations on these spaces. Moreover, we have the equalities

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=B^{\mathrm{aff}}=B^{\mathrm{lin}}=\mathbb{C},\quad B^{\mathrm{aff}}_{0}=B^{\mathrm{lin}}_{0}=\mathbb{C}\backslash\{0\},

and
\tilde{i}:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
can be viewed as the developing map
\dev:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
(modulo rescaling so that the product lattice
\Lambda
the standard integer lattice).

Example 8.2
.

Consider the Cartan-Dirac structure on
M=\mathrm{SU}(2)
and let
M_{0}=\mathrm{SU}(2)\backslash\{-I\}
. Then it follows from Examples
6.6
and
6.7
that the orbit spaces are

\textstyle{B_{0}=(\mathbb{S}^{1}\backslash\{-1\})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ B=\mathbb{S}^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{2},}

while at the level of universal covering spaces one has an inclusion

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}=\left(-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\hookrightarrow\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=\mathbb{R},

with orbifold covering projection

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to B,\quad t\mapsto[e^{2\pi it}].

On the other hand, the Weyl groups and the orbifold fundamental groups coincide

\mathcal{W}_{0}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})=\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad\mathcal{W}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)=\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}

and the morphism
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
in the diagram is the inclusion

\mathbb{Z}_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad a\mapsto(0,a).

Note that
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts by
x\mapsto-x
and
\mathbb{Z}
acts by translations. Finally, the Weyl chambers in
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
are the intervals
\Delta_{n}:=[\tfrac{n}{2},\tfrac{n+1}{2}]
, (
n\in\mathbb{Z}
). Intersecting with
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}
one obtains the two Weyl chambers of
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}
, namely
\left(-\tfrac{1}{2},0\right]
and
\left[0,\tfrac{1}{2}\right)
. The set
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathcal{W}
consist of the reflections of the form
r_{n}(t)=n-t
,
(n\in\mathbb{Z})
. The reflections
\mathfrak{R}_{\Delta_{n}}=\{r_{n},r_{n+1}\}
give a Coxeter system with solo relations

r_{n}^{2}=r_{n+1}^{2}=1.

This describes
\mathcal{W}
as the free product
\mathbb{Z}_{2}*\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Note that the integral lattice in the universal covering spaces is determined by the choice of
\Ad
-invariant inner product in
\mathfrak{su}(2)
, which is unique up to scaling. As we vary the inner product we obtain all lattices
\Lambda=\lambda_{0}\mathbb{Z}\,\mathrm{d}t
,
(\lambda_{0}>0)
. The Weyl group clearly acts by integral affine transformations for any such choice.

In the previous two examples, we saw that by removing a part of the subregular strata may create or destroy topology on the leaf spaces, and may reduce or increase the size of the Weyl group. We will now see that when one removes parts of lower strata one can only increase the size of the Weyl group.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold of proper type and assume that
M_{0}\subset(M,\pi)
is an open saturated set such that

M_{0}^{\mathrm{reg}}=M^{\mathrm{reg}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad M_{0}^{\mathrm{subreg}}=M^{\mathrm{subreg}}.

Then the following holds:

(i)

The canonical maps
\mathcal{W}_{0}\to\mathcal{W}
,
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
are surjective and have a common kernel
\mathcal{K}
.

(ii)

\mathcal{K}
is isomorphic to the fundamental group of the pre-image of
B_{0}
by the canonical projection
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to B
.

(iii)

\mathcal{K}=0
whenever
M_{0}
contains all the points
x\in M
with the property that the semisimple part of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is 2-dimensional.

Proof.

The hypothesis imply that
\widehat{M}_{0}
contains the regular and subregular strata of
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi))
. By
Lemma
3.7

we deduce that the inclusion induces a surjection
\pi_{1}(\widehat{M}_{0})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
. Therefore the top diagonal arrows in Diagram (
8.11
) fit into an epimorphism of short exact sequences.
Its kernel is then another short exact sequence. Since its rightmost group is trivial, the first two kernels are identified. Because the horizontal arrows in (
8.11
) are epimorphisms, the same result holds for the short exact sequences at the level of leaf spaces. This shows that

\mathcal{W}_{0}
and
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})
are extensions of
\mathcal{W}
and
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
by the same group
\mathcal{K}
, so (i) holds.

To prove item (ii), we use that for any open subset of
B
, its linear holonomy cover is an open subset of
B^{\mathrm{lin}}
(see Proposition
5.17
). This reduces statement (ii) from one for (good) orbifolds to one for open subsets in manifolds and ordinary fundamental groups, as we did in proof of Proposition
7.9
. Therefore item (ii) follows.

To prove item (iii) we observe that the assumption on isotropy Lie algebras is equivalent to the codimension 2 strata of
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{inf}}(M,\pi))
being contained in
\widehat{M}_{0}
. Then the map
\pi_{1}(\widehat{M}_{0})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
is an isomorphism, and so all diagonal maps in (
8.11
) are trivial.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

If in the previous proposition
(M,\pi)
is source-proper and has 1-connected principal leaves, then also the canonical maps
\mathcal{W}_{0}\to\mathcal{W}
,
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
are surjective and have kernel
\mathcal{K}
. In this case,
\mathcal{K}
also coincides with the fundamental group of the pre-image of
\widehat{M}_{0}
by the universal covering projection of
\widehat{M}
. Indeed, if the principal leaves as 1-connected, then by Lemma
6.1
the vertical arrows in diagram (
8.11
) are isomorphisms. Therefore the kernel of

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
is identified with the kernel of
\pi_{1}(\widehat{M}_{0})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
. Since s-properness implies that
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{orb}}

is the universal covering space of
\widehat{M}
, then
\mathcal{K}
coincides with the fundamental group of the preimage
p^{-1}(M_{0})\subset\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{orb}}=\widetilde{\widehat{M}}
.

Example 8.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a semi-simple, rank 2, compact Lie algebra. We consider the linear Poisson manifold
M=\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. It is strong s-proper with source 1-connected integration
(T^{*}G,\omega_{\mathrm{can}})
, where
G
is the 1-connected Lie group integrating
\mathfrak{g}
. Besides the regular and subregular strata, we have one further stratum that consists of the origin. We let

M_{0}:=M\setminus\{0\}.

It follows from Section
8.1
, that the resolution of this Poisson manifold can be identified with

\widehat{M}=(G/T\times_{W}\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\widehat{M}_{0}=(G/T\times_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}).

where
\mathbb{R}^{2}
appears as the dual
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
of a maximal torus
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}
and
W
is the classical Weyl group. It is a known fact from Lie theory that the simple, rank 2, compact Lie algebras and their Weyl groups are:

\mathfrak{g}

\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(2)

\mathfrak{su}(3)

\mathfrak{so}(5)

\mathfrak{g}_{2}

W

D_{2}

D_{3}

D_{4}

D_{6}

Here the Weyl group
D_{2}
acts on
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
by reflection in the coordinate axes and in the other three cases
D_{k}
acts on
\mathbb{C}
as the symmetry group of the regular polygon whose vertices are the
k
-th roots of unity.
The leaf spaces are now

\textstyle{B_{0}=(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})/D_{k}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ B=\mathbb{C}/D_{k},}

with orbifold atlas the action groupoids

\mathbb{C}\ltimes(\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\quad\mathbb{C}\rtimes D_{k},

with projections, respectively,

p_{0}(z)=[e^{2\pi z}],\quad p(z)=[z].

In the first action groupoid,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts by complex conjugation and the
\mathbb{Z}
action is generated by
z\mapsto z+\tfrac{i}{k}
.

The situation is entirely similar to Example
8.1
and, as there, we find that the Weyl groups coincide with the orbifold fundamental groups and are given by:

\mathcal{W}_{0}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})=\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad\mathcal{W}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)=D_{k}

The morphism
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{0})\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
in diagram (
8.11
) is the projection

\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to D_{k}=\mathbb{Z}_{k}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad(n,a)\mapsto(n\,\mathrm{mod}\,k,a).

Its kernel
\mathcal{K}=\mathbb{Z}
is identified via the map
\tilde{i}:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{0}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
with the fundamental group of

p^{-1}(B_{0})=\mathbb{C}\backslash\{0\}\subset\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=\mathbb{C}.

One can check that, in contrast to Example
8.1
,
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is a non-split extension of
\mathcal{W}
in all 4 cases.

The Weyl chambers are the strips (see Figure
2
)

\Delta_{n}:=\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}:\tfrac{n}{2k}\leq y\leq\tfrac{n+1}{2k}\},\quad(n\in\mathbb{Z}).

The translation
(1,0)\in D_{k}
sends
\Delta_{n}
to
\Delta_{n+2k}
, whereas the reflection
(0,[1])\in D_{k}

sends
\Delta_{n}
to
\Delta_{-n}
.

Figure 2.
Orbifold cover and Weyl chambers

Observe finally that for all four Lie algebras the corresponding
B_{0}
are isomorphic orbifolds, since one has isomorphisms of orbifold atlases (Morita equivalences)

(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{Z}_{k}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

However, they are non-isomorphic (integral) affine orbifolds since, for instance, the linear holonomy is
D_{k}
.

9.
The Linear Variation and the Volume Polynomial

In this section we will assume that we have a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
of
\mathbf{s}
-proper type. We will apply the theory developed in
[
12
]
to its resolution
(\widehat{M},\widehat{L}_{\pi})
, in order to obtain statements about the variation of the cohomology classes of the symplectic leaves of
(M,\pi)
and the variation of the symplectic volumes.

9.1.
The linear variation of cohomology

As described in
[
12
, Section 5]
one can interpret the cohomology classes of the of leafwise presymplectic form

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
as a section of a vector bundle over
\widehat{M}
whose fibers are the second cohomology groups of the s-fibers of
\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
. We denote the latter vector bundle by

\mathscr{H}\to\widehat{M},\quad\mathscr{H}_{{\widehat{x}}}:=H^{2}(\Hol({\widehat{x}},-)).

The section provided by the presymplectic forms will be denoted by

\varpi:\widehat{M}\to\mathscr{H},\quad\varpi_{{\widehat{x}}}:=[\mathbf{t}^{*}\widehat{\omega}_{\widehat{S}_{\widehat{x}}}],

where
\widehat{\omega}_{\widehat{S}_{\widehat{x}}}
denotes the
\widehat{S}_{\widehat{x}}
denotes the presymplectic leaf containing
{\widehat{x}}
and
\mathbf{t}:\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\to\widehat{M}
is the target map.

The vector bundle
\mathscr{H}
is equipped with several structures:

(i)

An integral affine structure defined by the image of the integral cohomology
H^{2}(\Hol({\widehat{x}},-),\mathbb{Z})
. This gives a canonical flat connection on
\mathscr{H}
, the Gauss-Manin connection, and hence a representation of the groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
on
\widehat{M}
;

(ii)

A representation of the groupoid
\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
, where the action is via the map induced in cohomology by right translations.

The two groupoids in the previous items fit into a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Mon(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}))}

where we are making use of the fundamental group from section
6.1
and the canonical maps (
6.1
).

Proposition 9.1
.

There is a representation of
\Pi_{1}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}))
on
\mathscr{H}
compatible with the representations of
\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
and
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
. Furthermore, the section
\varpi
is
\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
-invariant.

Proof.

We observe that:

(C)

given an element
[\gamma]\in\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
its action on
\mathscr{H}
coincides with the action of
[\gamma]
via the Gauss-Manin connection.

Using this, one obtains:

(a)

the kernel
K
of the map
\pi_{1}(\widehat{M},{\widehat{x}}_{0})\to\pi_{1}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}),{\widehat{x}}_{0})
acts trivially on
\mathscr{H}
. By (
6.4
) an element in
K
is represented by a loop homotopic to a concatenation of paths of the form
\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}\alpha_{i}^{-1}
, where each
\beta_{i}
is a leafwise path with trivial holonomy. Hence, by (C), each
\beta_{i}
acts trivially by the Gauss-Manin connection, and so does
\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}\alpha_{i}^{-1}
.

(b)

By (a) the action of
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
descends to an action of
\Pi_{1}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}))
. By (C) this action is also compatible with the action of
\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
.

The
\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
-invariance of
\varpi
follows from its definition and the fact that the target is invariant under right translations.
∎

The previous proposition shows that we can view
\mathscr{H}
has a orbivector bundle over the classical orbifold
B
, which is equipped with an action of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
, and has a canonical section
\varpi
. Recall that
B
can be presented by various atlases of a discrete group acting on a smooth manifold:

B^{\mathrm{lin}}\rtimes\Gamma^{\mathrm{lin}}\rightrightarrows B^{\mathrm{lin}}
,
B^{\mathrm{aff}}\rtimes\Gamma^{\mathrm{aff}}\rightrightarrows B^{\mathrm{aff}}
and
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\rtimes\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\rightrightarrows\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
. For each of these one obtains an equivariant vector bundle and an equivariant section, representing
\mathscr{H}
and
\varpi
, which will be denoted by the same letter. In particular the fiber at the base point will be denoted by
\mathscr{H}_{0}
. Note that it coincides with the 2nd cohomology group of the generic symplectic leaf
S_{x_{0}}
. The value of
\varpi
at this leaf will be denoted
\varpi_{0}
.

We define the
transport map
(denoted
\mathrm{var}_{\varpi}
in
[
12
]
) using the representation of
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
on
\mathscr{H}
by:

\mathcal{T}_{\varpi}:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to\mathscr{H}_{0},\quad[\gamma]\mapsto\gamma^{*}\varpi_{\gamma(1)}

where we view
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
as the universal cover of
B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
, hence
\gamma
is a path in this space starting at the fixed base point. It follows from the next theorem that this maps descends to a similar map in
B^{\mathrm{aff}}
.

Recall that we have the (based) developing map

\dev:B^{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathfrak{t}_{0},

where we use the identification of the tangent space of
B^{\mathrm{aff}}
at the base point with
\mathfrak{t}_{0}
. Also, denoting by
\nabla
the Gauss-Manin connection on
\mathscr{H}\to B^{\mathrm{aff}}
, we have the
linear variation map
defined by

\mathrm{var}_{\varpi}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}:\mathfrak{t}_{0}\to\mathscr{H}_{0},\quad v\mapsto\nabla_{v}\varpi,

where
\mathscr{H}_{0}
is the fiber of
\mathscr{H}
at the fixed base point. We also consider the
affine variation map
, defined by

\mathrm{var}_{\varpi}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}:=\varpi_{0}+\mathrm{var}_{\varpi}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}.

The linear variation of the cohomology classes of the symplectic leaves of
(M,\pi)
, can finally be stated as follows.

Theorem 9.2
.

The transport map descends to
B^{\mathrm{aff}}
yielding the following commutative diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 11.76566pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.76566pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.98698pt\raise 5.89168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.89168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}_{\varpi}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.14069pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.30627pt\raise-22.47922pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\dev}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.76566pt\raise-26.45847pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 39.95317pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
