0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 74.14069pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{H}_{0}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.76486pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 35.76566pt\raise-31.76486pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{t}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.54683pt\raise-23.3125pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.74501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{var}_{\varpi}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.21098pt\raise-7.05554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 80.13376pt\raise-31.76486pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

Remark 9.3
.

The reader may wonder why the previous result expresses the linear variation of the cohomology classes of the symplectic leaves of
(M,\pi)
. For that, observe that the resolution map yields for each symplectic leaf
S_{x}
, a map in cohomology:

\textstyle{H^{2}(S_{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\widehat{S}_{{\widehat{x}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{t}^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})({\widehat{x}},-))\simeq\mathscr{H}_{0}}

where the last isomorphism is obtained by choosing a path connecting
{\widehat{x}}
to the base point
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
. So the theorem states that the variation (of the pullback) of the cohomology classes
[\omega_{S_{x}}]
becomes linear in any integral affine chart. This will also be clear in the proof below.

A more explicit description can be obtained by fixing a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\mathfrak{b}_{\Lambda}
for
\Lambda_{{\widehat{x}}_{0}}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{0}
. This induces an identification of
\mathcal{G}_{x_{0}}^{0}
with the standard torus
\mathbb{T}^{r}
via the exponential map.
On the other hand, denoting
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{0}
, one has the principal
G
-bundle

\mathbf{t}:\mathcal{G}(x_{0},-)\to S_{x_{0}}

and the associated Chern-Weyl homomorphism is defined on

S(\mathfrak{g}^{*})^{G}\cong S(\mathfrak{t}_{0}^{*})^{G/G^{0}}=S(\mathfrak{t}_{0}^{*})\cong\mathbb{R}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}].

Here the last identification is the one induced by the basis
\mathfrak{b}_{\Lambda}
, and the middle equality derives from the fact that
x_{0}
is in the principal part. Therefore our principal bundle has associated Chern classes, denoted

c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}\in H^{2}(S_{x_{0}}).

Finally, the developing map will have coordinates
\dev^{k}
w.r.t. the basis
\mathfrak{b}_{\Lambda}
.

All together, the linear variation formula boils down to a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{var}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{{\dev_{0}}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(S)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt(v^{i})\mapsto[\omega_{0}]+\sum_{i}v^{i}c_{i}}

More explicitly, for any path
\gamma
in
\widehat{M}
starting at
x_{0}
, one has

\gamma^{*}[\omega_{\gamma(1)}]-[\omega_{0}]=\dev^{1}(\gamma)c_{1}+\ldots+\dev^{r}(\gamma)c_{r}.

In the strong s-proper case,
[
12
, Corollary ]
shows that the classes
c_{1},\dots,c_{r}
form a primitive family, i.e., we have

\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{r})=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{r})\cap H^{2}(S_{x_{0}},\mathbb{Z}).

Proof.

The result will follow from
[
12
, Theorem 5.2.4]
applied to the resolution of
(M,\pi)
, viewed as a regular Dirac manifold of
\mathbf{s}
-proper type.

The aforementioned theorem provides the commutative diagram

(9.1)

\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}_{\varpi}}
\scriptstyle{\dev}
\textstyle{\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{\nu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{var}_{\varpi}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}

The bundle
\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
where the transport map takes values differs slightly from our cohomology bundle: it is constructed from the atlas
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})
defining the orbifold structure on
B
, rather than the classical one. More precisely, it is the bundle over
\widehat{M}
whose fiber is the second cohomology of the source fibers
\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)
of the given orbifold atlas. In this case
\nu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})=\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}
, seen as an affine bundle, and
\mathrm{var}_{\varpi}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
is defined as a above, but considering
\varpi
as section of
\Gamma(\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{B}})\to\widehat{M}
.

To obtain the commutative diagram of the statement we take the following steps:

(i)

Replace the
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B})
-representation
\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
by the
\pi_{1}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}))
-representation
\mathscr{H}
: first the covering maps
\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)\to\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})({\widehat{x}},-)
induce an injection

\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{B}}

with image the fixed-point sets for the action of the groups of deck transformations. This inclusion preserves the integral affine structure given by integral cohomology and therefore is
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
-equivariant. The aforementioned covers arise from the short exact sequence of foliation groupoids

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 67.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 104.36115pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 104.36115pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 183.35696pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 183.35696pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

We also have a commutative diagram of fundamental groupoids

\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}))}

where the diagonal arrows are surjective. As in the proof of Proposition
9.1
, the representations of
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{B})
and
\Pi_{1}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}))
on
\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathscr{H}
are completely determined by the action of
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
. It follows that the inclusion
\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
is equivariant.

Finally, notice that the injection
\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
preserves the sections
\varpi
.

(ii)

Drop down to
B^{\mathrm{aff}}
:
the developing map descends to
\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{aff}}(\widehat{M})
, and, hence, so does the whole diagram.
Restricting to the s-fiber above
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
one obtains

\textstyle{\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}_{\varpi}}
\scriptstyle{\dev}
\textstyle{\mathscr{H}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{t}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{var}_{\varpi}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}

The last diagram also descends to
B^{\mathrm{aff}}
giving the commutative diagram in the statement.

∎

A Poisson manifold of
\mathbf{s}
-proper type around a symplectic leaf is described by the linear local model (see Section
2.2
). The linear variation of cohomology for such a linear local model is described explicitly in the following example.

Example 9.4
.

We described in Example
4.12
the resolution of the linear local model. Fixing the data of a principal
G
-bundle
p:P\to(S,\omega_{S})
, a connection 1-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})
, and choice of maximal torus
T\subset G
,
the resolution
{\mathscr{R}}:\widehat{M}\to M
of the linear Poisson model is

{\mathscr{R}}:((P\times V)/N(T),\widehat{L}_{\pi^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}})\to((P\times U)/G,\pi^{\theta}_{{\mathrm{lin}}}),\quad[(p,\xi)]_{N(T)}\to[(p,\xi)]_{G},

where the Dirac and Poisson tensors are built out of reduction of the forms

(9.2)

p^{*}\omega_{S}-\mathrm{d}\langle\theta^{T},\cdot\rangle\in\Omega^{2}(P\times\mathfrak{t}^{*}),\quad p^{*}\omega_{S}-\mathrm{d}\langle\theta,\cdot\rangle\in\Omega^{2}(P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}).

Here
\theta^{T}:=\mathrm{pr}_{\mathfrak{t}}\circ\theta
and

V
is the intersection with
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
of a small saturated neighborhood
U
of
0\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(so that
\pi_{\mathrm{lin}}^{\theta}
is Poisson).

We can move in the big diagram one step up. For simplicity, we assume that the centralizer
Z_{G}(T)=T
. We obtain that

\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{aff}}=\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{lin}}:=(P\times U)/T=P/T\times U,

with Dirac structure still induced by the form (
9.2
). Notice that now this form is the pullback under the projection
P\times\mathfrak{t}^{*}\to P/T\times\mathfrak{t}^{*}
of the form

(9.3)

\underline{\omega}:=q^{*}\omega_{S}-\mathrm{d}\langle\theta^{T},\cdot\rangle\in\Omega^{2}(P/T\times\mathfrak{t}^{*}),

where
q:P\to P/T
is the quotient map. In other words, the Dirac structure on
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{lin}}
has presymplectic leaves
P/T\times{\xi}
with presymplectic forms the restriction of the globally defined closed 2-form (
9.3
).

Fixing a basis for
\mathfrak{t}
, the principal torus bundle
q:P\to P/T
has Chern classes
[c_{i}]\in H^{2}(P/T)
represented by the components of the curvature form of
\theta^{T}
, i.e.,

-\mathrm{d}\theta^{T}=q^{*}(c_{1},\dots,c_{r}).

The presence of the minus sign is due to our convention that Lie algebras and Lie algebroids are constructed using right-invariant vector fields.

The closed 2-form (
9.3
) becomes

\underline{\omega}:=q^{*}\omega_{S}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}\,\xi^{i},

for
\xi=(\xi^{1},\dots,\xi^{r})\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}
.
The linear variation of the cohomology class of the symplectic forms can be seen as a cohomological version of the last formula. To see this, recall that the symplectic leaves of

(M,\pi^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})
are parametrised by
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. More precisely, the leaf
(S_{\xi},\omega_{\xi})
corresponding to
\xi

is

S_{\xi}=(P\times\{\xi\})/G_{\xi}

with the symplectic form obtained by descending

\underline{\omega}|_{G/T\times\{\xi\}}\in\Omega^{2}(G/T\times\{\xi\})

along the bundle map
q_{\xi}:G/T\times\{\xi\}\to G/G_{\xi}\times\{\xi\}
.
Therefore the previous formula evaluated at a specific
\xi
becomes

q_{\xi}^{*}\omega_{S_{\xi}}=q^{*}\omega_{S}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}\,\xi^{i}\in\Omega^{2}(G/T).

Now, observe that the developing map is just the projection

\dev:\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{aff}}=P/T\times U\to\mathfrak{t}^{*},

so we find that

\mathrm{var}_{\varpi}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}(\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}\,\xi^{i}.

9.2.
The symplectic volume polynomial

We now study how the symplectic volumes of the leaves vary. For that we need to pass from second degree cohomology to top-degree cohomology.
The discussion in the previous section on the bundle
\mathscr{H}
applies word by word to the similar bundles obtained using instead the cohomology in any fixed degree. In particular, it applies to the top degree of the regular leaves, which is (see the discussion following Theorem
4.2
)

2k:=\dim(M)-r,\quad\text{where}\quad r=\operatorname{corank}\pi.

Therefore one obtains a line bundle

\mathscr{L}\to\widehat{M},\quad\mathscr{L}_{{\widehat{x}}}=H^{2k}(\Hol({\widehat{x}},-)),

which is a representation of
\Pi_{1}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}))
. In particular, the fiber at
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
becomes

\mathscr{L}_{0}:=H^{2k}(S_{x_{0}})\in\Rep(\pi^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{1}(B)).

Furthermore, the role of the section
\varpi
is replaced by

\sigma_{\vol}:=\frac{\varpi^{k}}{k!}\in\Gamma(\mathscr{L}).

This section is
\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
-invariant dues to the fact that the map

\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{L}
,
u\mapsto u^{k}/k!
,
is
\Pi_{1}(\Hol(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}))
-equivariant.
This allows us to interpret
\sigma_{\vol}
as a section of an orbi-vector bundle over
B
. To work over smooth manifolds one can, as before, pullback
\mathscr{L}
together with
\sigma_{\vol}
to
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
,
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
, etc, via the canonical projections onto
\widehat{M}
, or drop them to
B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
,
B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
, etc.
When doing so we will keep the same notations
\mathscr{L}
and
\sigma_{\vol}
.

Next we explain how to encode
\sigma_{\vol}
in terms of a polynomial. There will be two ways of achieving this and both are relevant to us:

(i)

We will show that
\mathscr{L}
trivializes over
B^{\mathrm{aff}}
. This gives a polynomial function

{\mathscr{V}}_{0}:B^{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathbb{R}.

(ii)

The tensor product
\mathscr{L}\otimes\mathscr{L}
trivializes already over
B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
. We obtain a function whose pullback to
B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
coincides with
{\mathscr{V}}^{2}_{0}
.

In the statement of the next result, by the singular part of

B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
we mean the complement of the pre-image of
B^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
by the covering projection
B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\to B
.

Theorem 9.5
.

There exists a function

{\mathscr{V}}_{0}:B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\to\mathbb{R}

with the following properties:

(i)

it is a polynomial function on the integral affine manifold
B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
;

(ii)

its zero-set is precisely the singular part of
B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
;

(iii)

at regular points
\tilde{b}\in B^{\mathrm{aff}}
,

{\mathscr{V}}_{0}(\widetilde{b})=\pm\,\iota_{0}(b)\vol(S_{b},\omega_{S_{b}})

where
\iota_{0}:B^{\mathrm{reg}}\to\mathbb{N}
is the function that for each
b\in B
gives the order of the holonomy group of
S_{b}
;

(iv)

for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
and
\widetilde{b}\in\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
one has

{\mathscr{V}}_{0}(\widetilde{b}\,w)=\delta(w)\,{\mathscr{V}}_{0}(\widetilde{b}),

where
\delta:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the parity character;

(v)

the square of
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}
descends to an orbifold smooth function

{\mathscr{V}}_{0}^{2}:B\to\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

Consider
\mathscr{L}
as a line bundle over
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
. It can be trivialised

\mathscr{L}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\times\mathscr{L}_{0},

using the Gauss-Manin connection: writing
\widetilde{b}=[\gamma]
, where
\gamma
a path in
B^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
from
{\widehat{x}}
to
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
, parallel transport with respect to this connection yields the desired trivialization

T_{\gamma}:\mathscr{L}_{{\widehat{x}}}\to\mathscr{L}_{0}.

Next, if we identify
\mathscr{L}_{0}
with
\mathbb{R}
using a primitive integral covector

\lambda_{0}\in\mathscr{L}_{0}^{*}
, we set

(9.4)

{\mathscr{V}}_{0}([\gamma]):=\lambda_{0}\left(T_{\gamma}(\sigma_{\vol}(\gamma(1))\right)

Therefore, under the isomorphism above,
\sigma_{\vol}
is identified with the smooth function
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Since
\sigma_{\vol}=\frac{\varpi^{k}}{k!}
, the linear variation from Theorem
9.2
implies that
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}
is a polynomial function. It follows (see also Section
11
) that there exist a polynomial
P
in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
such that

{\mathscr{V}}_{0}=P\circ\dev.

Since
\dev
descends to
B^{\mathrm{aff}}
, the function
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}
factors through the projection

\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dev}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}}
\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{aff}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dev}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{V}}_{0}}

so it can be viewed as a polynomial function
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}:B^{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathbb{R}
, proving (i).

We now prove item (iii). For
\widetilde{b}=[\gamma]
with
\gamma:[0,1]\to M^{{\mathrm{lin}}}
a path from
{\widehat{x}}_{0}
to some
{\widehat{x}}
in the regular part, to compute
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}([\gamma])

we have to apply the parallel transport

T_{\gamma}:H^{2k}(\Hol({{\widehat{x}}},-))\to H^{2k}(\Hol({{\widehat{x}}_{0}},-))

to the Liouville element

\frac{\omega_{{\widehat{x}}}^{k}}{k!}\in H^{2k}(\Hol({{\widehat{x}}},-))

and then, up to a sign, pair it with the fundamental class for
\Hol({{\widehat{x}}_{0}},-)
. Up to a sign, that is the same as pairing the Liouville element at
{\widehat{x}}
with the fundamental class of
\Hol({{\widehat{x}}},-)
, i.e., the symplectic volume of

(\Hol({{\widehat{x}}},-),\omega_{{\widehat{x}}}).

In turn, this manifold is a covering of the symplectic leaf
S_{{\widehat{x}}}
, with group of deck transformations the holonomy group of
S_{{\widehat{x}}}
. Therefore (iii) follows.

We now move to the proofs of items (iv) and (v). Note that the triviality of the integral affine line bundle
\mathscr{L}\to\widehat{M}
is controlled by the character

\delta_{\mathscr{L}}:\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}

determined by

T_{\gamma}=\delta_{\mathscr{L}}([\gamma])\cdot{\mathrm{id}},

where
T_{[\gamma]}
denotes parallel transport w.r.t. the Gauss-Manin connection.
Furthermore, this descends to

\delta_{\mathscr{L}}:\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

On the other hand, it follows from (
9.4
) that we have the formula

(9.5)

{\mathscr{V}}_{0}(\widetilde{b}\,u)=\delta_{\mathscr{L}}(u)\,{\mathscr{V}}_{0}(\widetilde{b}),

for all
\tilde{b}\in\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
,
u\in\pi_{1}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}(B)
. This implies that (v) holds.

To prove (iv) note that
\delta_{\mathscr{L}}
is trivial over the image of the splitting given in Proposition
6.14
since the section
\sigma_{\vol}\in\Gamma(\mathscr{L})
is nowhere vanishing over the regular part. Therefore,
\delta_{\mathscr{L}}
is determined by its values on
\mathcal{W}
where we claim that it coincides with the parity character of
\mathcal{W}

(9.6)

\delta_{\mathscr{L}}|_{\mathcal{W}}=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}.

This identity, together with (
9.5
), implies that (iv) holds. In order to prove (
9.6
) it is enough to check it for an abstract reflection
\tau_{{\widehat{x}}}=p_{*}\hat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}
associated to subregular point
{\widehat{x}}
, since the conjugate of these generate
\mathcal{W}
. Working at the level of
\widehat{M}
, and denoting by
y=1_{{\widehat{x}}}\in\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{lin}}
the point represented by the constant path at
{\widehat{x}}
,
we will prove that
\hat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}
acts on the fiber

\mathscr{L}_{y}=H^{2k}(\Hol({\widehat{x}},-)),

as minus the identity, i.e., changing the orientation of the fiber.
The element
\hat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}

belongs to the group of Deck transformations of the cover
\Hol({\widehat{x}},-)\to\widehat{S}_{{\widehat{x}}}
. Therefore the statement that we have to prove is equivalent to show that
\hat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}
is orientation reversing. Consider the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Hol({\widehat{x}},-)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{t}}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\widehat{S}_{{\widehat{x}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{S_{x}}

We observe that:

(a)

The fiber
q^{-1}({\widehat{x}})
is a finite union of spheres.
Indeed,
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)\cong\mathbb{RP}^{2}

and therefore
q^{-1}({\widehat{x}})
is a (possibly non-connected) covering space of
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
. Both the total space
\Hol({\widehat{x}},-)
and the base
S_{x}
are orientable, and thus so the fiber.

(b)

The action of
\hat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}
on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{y}
in
q^{-1}({\widehat{x}})
through
y
is the antipodal map.
Indeed, the restriction

q:\mathbb{S}^{2}_{y}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2}

is a covering map whose Deck transformation group corresponds to the generator of the fundamental group
\hat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}
.

Now, since
\hat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}
is represented by a loop in the fiber of
{\mathscr{R}}
, its action on
\Hol({\widehat{x}},-)
lifts the identity on
S_{x}
. This, combined with (b), implies that
\hat{\tau}_{{\widehat{x}}}
reverses the orientation on
\Hol({\widehat{x}},-)
.

Finally, item (ii) follows from the other items.
∎

Remark 9.6
.

The proof also shows that
\mathscr{L}
is trivializable over
B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
. Indeed, from the proof this is equivalent to
\delta_{\mathscr{L}}|_{K^{{\mathrm{aff}}}}=1
. This follows by observing that, since
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}
descends to
B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
, we must have

{\mathscr{V}}_{0}(\widetilde{b}\,u)={\mathscr{V}}_{0}(\widetilde{b}),

whenever
u\in K^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
. Since
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}
is not identically zero, we obtain from (
9.5
) that
\delta_{\mathscr{L}}=1
on
K^{{\mathrm{aff}}}
.

10.
The Duistermaat-Heckman and the Weyl Integration Formulas

In this section we discuss measure theoretic aspects of Poisson manifolds of s-proper type, extending the Duistermaat-Heckman formula given in
[
12
]
to non-regular Poisson manifolds.

We will be working with Radon measures and, when working on manifolds,
we will focus mainly on
geometric measures
, by which we mean measures associated to densities. In general, Radon measures will be denoted by
\mu
and positive densities will be denoted by
\rho
. A measure induced by a density
\rho
will be denoted by
\mu_{\rho}
. Also, we denote by the symbol
\mathcal{D}
density bundles associated with a vector bundle. We refer to
[
15
]
and
[
21
, Section 3.13]
for details on measures and densities.

For the rest of the section we fix such a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
together with an s-proper integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows(M,\pi)
and we consider the leaf space
B
together with the canonical projection

p:M\to B.

We define two measures on
B
. The first one arises from the fact that
B
has an integral affine orbifold structure and, therefore, it
carries a natural measure (see below). We call it the
Lebesgue measure
and we denote it by

(10.1)

\boldsymbol{\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{B}}.

The second measure is obtained from the Liouville measure
\mu_{\Omega}
on
\mathcal{G}
, induced by the density associated to the Liouville form

\frac{\Omega^{{\mathrm{top}}}}{{\mathrm{top}}!}\in\Omega^{{\mathrm{top}}}(\mathcal{G})
. Noticing that the map
p\circ\mathbf{s}=p\circ\mathbf{t}:\mathcal{G}\to B
is proper, one can push
\mu_{\Omega}
to a measure on the leaf space, denoted

\boldsymbol{\mu^{DH}_{B}}=p_{*}s_{*}(\mu_{\Omega}).

We call it the
Duistermaat-Heckman measure
on
B
.

The last ingredient for stating the main theorem in this section is the
square volume polynomial

{\mathscr{V}}^{2}:B\to\mathbb{R}
. It coincides with the function

{\mathscr{V}}^{2}(b)=\begin{cases}(\iota(b)\vol(S_{b},\omega_{S_{b}}))^{2},&b\in B^{\mathrm{reg}}\\
0,&b\notin B^{\mathrm{reg}}\end{cases}

where
\iota:B\to\mathbb{N}
counts the number of connected components of the isotropy group of
\mathcal{G}
at a/any
x\in S_{b}
(see also Remark
10.3
).

Theorem 10.1
.

If
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is an s-connected, s-proper integration of
(M,\pi)
then

(10.2)

\boldsymbol{\mu^{DH}_{B}}={\mathscr{V}}^{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{B}},

where
{\mathscr{V}}^{2}:B\to\mathbb{R}
is the square volume polynomial.

Remark 10.2
.

It may be instructive to the reader to notice that
\boldsymbol{\mu^{DH}_{B}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{B}}
are associated with the two interpretations of
B
as a leaf space of
(M,\mathcal{F})
and as a leaf space of
(\widehat{M},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
, respectively. Recall also from Section
9.2
that, although both
\iota
and the symplectic volumes of the leaves of
(M,\pi)
yield functions
\iota
and
\vol
defined on the entire
B
, the reason why
{\mathscr{V}}^{2}
appears in the final formula is its smoothness.

Remark 10.3
.

There is very little difference between the function
{\mathscr{V}}^{2}
and the function
{\mathscr{V}}_{0}^{2}
introduced in the previous section: one is a rescaling of the other. The scaling factor arises from the fact that we use two orbifold structures on
B
: in this section we use the one whose atlas is the foliation groupoid
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})
in (
5.3
), while in the previous section we used the classical orbifold structure, i.e., whose atlas is the holonomy groupoid
\Hol(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi})
.

The fact that
\iota(b)/\iota_{0}(b)
is (locally) constant can be seen as an instance of a general remark: for any (
s
-connected) proper foliation groupoid
\mathcal{B}
, the kernel of the canonical map
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows\Hol(\mathcal{F})
is a bundle of groups of (locally) constant cardinality. This follows, e.g., from the local normal form for proper Lie groupoids, which reduces the statement to one about linear actions of finite groups.

In our case, the resulting rescaling factor can be read off at any point
x\in M
: it is the cardinality of
Z_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}(T)/T
, where
T\subset\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a maximal torus (compare with Section
5.6
). At a regular point
x
, this is the subgroup of
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{x})
consisting of elements which act trivially on
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}
.

We now discuss the measure (
10.1
) in detail. The key ingredient in its construction is the density arising from the integral affine lattice
\Lambda\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}
given by

(10.3)

\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}\in\Gamma(\widehat{M},\mathcal{D}_{\nu}),\quad\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}|_{x}:=|\lambda_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\lambda_{n}|

where
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
is any basis of
\Lambda_{x}\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{t}}_{x}=\nu_{x}^{*}
. This density has the property that it is invariant under the holonomy of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}
and, therefore, can be thought of as a density on the orbifold
B
.
Next, we need the generalisation to orbifolds of the construction
of measures associated to densities. If we use étale orbifold atlases, this is
rather straightforward, as explained in the discussion leading to Proposition 6.1.3 in
[
12
]
and produces (
10.1
) starting from
\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}
. However, it is useful to have a concrete description for all, not necessarily étale, orbifold atlases, as explained also in
[
12
]
. The next proposition provides a summary of what we need here.

Proposition 10.4
.

Let
B
be an orbifold with atlas
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows M\to B
, where
\mathcal{B}
is a proper foliation groupoid with underlying foliation denoted
\mathcal{F}
and normal bundle
\nu
.
Then any positive density
\rho_{\nu}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}_{\nu})
that is invariant under the holonomy of
\mathcal{F}
gives rise to a measure
\mu_{\rho_{\nu}}
on
B
such that
for any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(M)
,

\int_{M}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\rho_{M}}(x)=\int_{B}\left(\iota(b)\int_{O_{b}}f(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{O_{b}}(y)\right)\,d\mu_{\rho_{\nu}}(b),

where
\mu_{\rho_{M}}
and
\mu_{O_{b}}
are measures associated to densities on
M
and on the orbits
O_{b}
obtained from any decomposition

\rho_{\nu}=\rho^{*}_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\rho_{M}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{TM}\cong\mathcal{D}_{\nu}

as follows:

(a)

\rho_{M}
is a density on
M
;

(b)

\rho^{*}_{\mathcal{F}}
is the dual of a strictly positive density
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{F}}
;

(c)

\mu_{O_{b}}
is associated with the density
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}|_{O_{b}}\in\mathcal{D}_{TO_{b}}
;

(d)

\iota(b)
is the number of elements of the isotropy groups
\mathcal{B}_{x}
with
x\in O_{b}
.

Furthermore, this property characterizes uniquely
\mu_{\rho_{\nu}}
.

Remark 10.5
.

The density
\rho^{*}_{\mathcal{F}}
, combined with the isomorphisms
t^{*}\mathcal{F}|_{S_{x}}\cong T\mathcal{B}(x,-)
induced by right translations, give rise to densities on the
s
-fibers
\mathcal{B}(x,-)
. The associated measures will be denoted
\mu_{\mathcal{B}(x,-)}
. It follows that the numbers
\iota(b)
can be absorbed into the integration as follows:

\iota(b)\int_{O_{b}}f(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{O_{b}}(y)=\int_{\mathcal{B}(x,-)}f(t(g))\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{B}(x,-)}(g),

where
x
is any point in the orbit
O_{b}
.

When
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows M
is
\mathbf{s}
-proper, the fact that the
\mu_{\mathcal{B}(x,-)}
are induced by a smooth family of densities implies that the volumes can be arranged into a smooth function

(10.4)

\vol^{\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}:B\to\mathbb{R},\quad b=p(x)\mapsto\vol\left(\mathcal{B}(x,-),\mu_{\mathcal{B}(x,-)}\right)=\iota(b)\vol(O_{b},\mu_{O_{b}}).

Then the proposition implies that, in the
\mathbf{s}
-proper case,
\mu_{\rho_{\nu}}
could be defined by

(10.5)

\mu_{\rho_{\nu}}=\frac{1}{\vol^{\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}}\,p_{*}(\mu_{\rho_{M}}).

Proof of Theorem
10.1
.

We will apply Proposition
10.4
to the foliation groupoid

\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows\widehat{M}
that serves as orbifold atlas for
B
(see Section
5.3
).

The Liouville form
\frac{\Omega^{{\mathrm{top}}}}{{\mathrm{top}}!}\in\Omega^{{\mathrm{top}}}(\mathcal{G})
can be pushed down via the proper map
\mathbf{s}:\mathcal{G}\to M
to a density

\boldsymbol{\rho^{DH}_{M}}:=\int_{s}\frac{|\Omega^{{\mathrm{top}}}|}{{\mathrm{top}}!}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}_{TM}).

The associated measure, which is also the pushforward of the Liouville measure, is denoted
\mu^{DH}_{M}
and we call it the
Duistermaat-Heckman measure
on
M
. We can use
\rho^{DH}_{M}
to construct a canonical decomposition of the density (
10.3
), as in Proposition
10.4
. To that end we use the exact sequence of vector bundles over
\widehat{M}

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widehat{\nu}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.6528pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 69.6528pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widehat{L}_{\pi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 110.38467pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 110.38467pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 151.11655pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 151.11655pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

together with the isomorphism
\widehat{L}_{\pi}\cong{\mathscr{R}}^{*}T^{*}M
– see (
4.13
) – to identify

\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}\cong\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{L}_{\pi}}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{\nu}}\cong{\mathscr{R}}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{T^{*}M}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{\nu}}.

Making use of
\rho^{DH}_{M}
and its dual
\rho^{DH,\vee}_{M}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}_{T^{*}M})
,
we can now define

\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}:=\left(\rho^{DH,\vee}_{M}\circ{\mathscr{R}}\right)\otimes\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}).

Similarly, using the identification
\mathcal{D}_{T\widehat{M}}\cong\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{\nu}}
arising from the sequence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
