29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.23187pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.23187pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.02008pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 113.02008pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widehat{\nu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 148.57565pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 148.57565pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

we define

\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{M}}:=\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}\otimes\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{D}_{T\widehat{M}}).

By construction, these two densities provide a decomposition of
\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}
as in Proposition
10.4
.
In particular,
\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}
induces measures on the leaves
\widehat{S}_{b}
and on the
s
-fibers
\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)
, denoted now

(10.6)

\mu_{\widehat{S}_{b}}^{{\mathrm{aff}}},\quad\mu_{\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}.

Notice that these measure are defined for every point in
B
, regular or not.
For the volume function (
10.4
) associated with
\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}
one finds:

Lemma 10.6
.

The function
\vol^{\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}}:B\to\mathbb{R}
is identically
1
.

Proof.

The function
\vol^{\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}}
is smooth so it suffices to prove it is
1
on
B^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
. Therefore we may assume that
M
is regular and make
use of the results from
[
12
]
. We will prove something stronger, namely that

(10.7)

(\iota\cdot\vol)\cdot\,\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}=\frac{|\omega_{\mathcal{F}_{\pi}}^{{\mathrm{top}}}|}{{\mathrm{top}}!},

where
\vol(b)
is now the symplectic volume of
S_{b}
. In the regular case, the density
\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}
coincides witj
\rho^{DH,\vee}_{M}\otimes\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}
. Hence, we can apply
[
12
, Lemma 6.3.3 ]
to rewrite the left hand side of the last equality as

\left(\frac{1}{\iota\cdot\vol}\cdot\,\rho^{DH}_{M}\right)^{\vee}\otimes\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}=\rho_{M}^{\vee}\otimes\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu},

with
\rho_{M}=\frac{|\omega_{\mathcal{F}_{\pi}}^{{\mathrm{top}}}|}{{\mathrm{top}}!}\otimes\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}
(which is equation (6.2) in loc. cit.). Noticing that now
{\mathscr{R}}={\mathrm{id}}
, we are left with showing that under the isomorphism
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{F}}\cong\mathcal{D}_{T^{*}M}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{\nu}
we have

\rho_{M}^{\vee}\otimes\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\nu}=\frac{|\omega_{\mathcal{F}_{\pi}}^{{\mathrm{top}}}|}{{\mathrm{top}}!}

The leafwise symplectic form
\omega_{\mathcal{F}_{\pi}}
, gives an identification
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}\cong\mathcal{F}_{\pi}^{*}
and from the decomposition induced by

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\nu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
,
we see that the last identity becomes precisely the definition of
\rho_{M}
– again equation (6.2) in
[
12
]
.
∎

Denoting by
\boldsymbol{\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{M}}}
the measure on
\widehat{M}
induced by
\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{M}}
, the previous lemma combined with Remark
10.5
(cf. equation (
10.5
)) implies that

\boldsymbol{\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{B}}=p_{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{M}}}).

Therefore, to complete the proof of theorem we only need to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 10.7
.

One has the following equality of measures on
M
:

\boldsymbol{\mu^{DH}_{M}}={\mathscr{V}}^{2}\cdot{\mathscr{R}}_{*}(\boldsymbol{\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{M}}})

Proof.

We have to prove that
\int_{M}f\rho^{DH}_{M}=\int_{\widehat{M}}{\mathscr{V}}^{2}\cdot{\mathscr{R}}^{*}(f)\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{M}
for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(M)
,
Since both sides involve integration of
smooth
densities (see Remark
10.2
) nothing changes if we remove from
M
and
\widehat{M}

subspaces of (Lebesque) measure zero. We deduce that it suffices to prove the identity from the statement
over
\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{reg}}}\cong M^{{\mathrm{reg}}}
. Therefore we may now assume that
M
is regular, and prove that

\rho^{DH}_{M}=(\iota\cdot\vol)^{2}\cdot\,\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{M}
.
But this is just a restatement of Lemma 6.3.3 from
[
12
]
, as the equation (
10.7
) and the definition of

\rho_{M}
((6.2) in loc.cit. again) show.
∎

This completes the proof of Theorem
10.1
.
∎

From Proposition
10.4
we deduce the following
Weyl integration formula
:

Corollary 10.8
.

For all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(M)
one has

\int_{M}f(x)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}\mu^{DH}_{M}}(x)=\int_{B}\left(\int_{\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)}f({\mathscr{R}}(t(g)))\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}(g)\right){\mathscr{V}}^{2}(b)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}\mu^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{B}}(b).

Corollary 10.9
.

One has a unitary isomorphism of
L^{2}
-spaces:

L^{2}(B,\mu_{B}^{DH})\cong L^{2}(B,\mu_{B}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}),\quad\phi\mapsto\phi\cdot|{\mathscr{V}}|.

Example 10.10
.

Let us consider the linear Poisson structure on the dual of a compact Lie algebra
M=\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Fixing an integrating groupoid
\mathcal{G}=T^{*}G\cong G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, the resolution
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}=G/T\times_{W}\mathfrak{t}^{*}
has the integration
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}=G\ltimes\widehat{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
, and the induced foliation groupoid
\mathcal{B}
is (see
8.3
):

(G/T\times G/T\times\mathfrak{t}^{*})/W\rightrightarrows(G/T\times\mathfrak{t}^{*})/W.

In particular, we obtain an identification

(10.8)

\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)\cong G/T.

The measures and densities above become the following:

•

\rho_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}^{DH}=\mathrm{d}X^{*}
, the constant density on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
determined by the Haar density on
G
; i.e., if one
G
-translates its dual
\mathrm{d}X\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{g}}
then one obtains the Haar density of
G
.

•

\rho_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}}^{\mathrm{aff}}=\frac{1}{|W|}\mathrm{d}Y^{*}
,
where
\mathrm{d}Y^{*}
is the constant density on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
associated to the lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathfrak{t}
. Note that the dual of
\mathrm{d}Y^{*}
produces the Haar density of
T
.

•

\rho_{\mathcal{B}({\widehat{x}},-)}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}=\mathrm{d}(gT)
, the quotient
of the Haar density of
G
modulo the Haar density of
T
, where one uses identification (
10.8
).

Moreover, in this case one has
{\mathscr{V}}^{2}(Y^{*})=|\det(\ad Y^{*})_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{t}}|
, as in
[
22
]
, and therefore our integral formula becomes

\int_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}f(X^{*})\,\mathrm{d}X^{*}=\frac{1}{|W|}\int_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}\left(\int_{G/T}f(\Ad_{g}(Y^{*}))\,\mathrm{d}(gT)\right)|\det(\ad Y^{*})_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{t}}|\,\mathrm{d}Y^{*}.

Comparing with
[
22
, Corollary 3.14.2]
, notice that we are using more specific densities
on
G
and
T
for which the constant
c
there equals
1
.

Remark 10.11
.

The Duistermaat-Heckman formula discussed here can be seen as a formula associated to the Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
\mathbf{t}:(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\to M
. It implies a similar formula for other free Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces
\mu:(P,\Omega)\to M
by applying it to the corresponding gauge groupoids. The more general version, for Hamiltonian spaces that are not necessarily free, will be discussed somewhere else (see also
[
35
,
46
]
).

Remark 10.12
.

While the push-forward of measures is well-behaved, the situation is more subtle when pushing forward densities along maps that are not submersions.
That is the reason that the equality in the statement of Lemma
10.7
is one of measures and cannot be formulated as an equality of densities on
M
.

On the other hand, the lemma can be understood as the equality of densities on
\widehat{M}

{\mathscr{R}}^{*}(\rho^{DH}_{M})={\mathscr{V}}^{2}\cdot\rho^{{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\widehat{M}}

For the left hand side we use that densities can be pulled back along maps that are local diffeomorphisms on dense open sets, like our
{\mathscr{R}}:\widehat{M}\to M
is. The price to pay is that the pull-back of smooth densities may be only continuous (but that is not a problem for inducing measures). However, the equality says that the left hand side is also smooth.

11.
Compactness and Completeness

In this section, we establish structural properties of compact PMCTs and complete, s-proper PMCTs. We achieve this by combining the theory developed in the previous sections with a new ingredient: the factorization properties of polynomials in integral affine manifolds and orbifolds, which we apply to the volume polynomial of Poisson manifolds of s-proper type. For example, we will show that:

Theorem 11.1
.

Any Poisson manifold of compact type is regular.
Equivalently, any compact symplectic groupoid is regular.

For a compact symplectic manifold all powers of the cohomology class of the symplectic form must be non-trivial. The theorem says that compactness in multiplicative symplectic geometry severely constrains the groupoid multiplication. Equivalently, it severely constrains the underlying Poisson geometry.

Remark 11.2
.

Theorem
11.1
is false for twisted Dirac manifolds of compact type. Equivalently, there exist compact twisted presymplectic groupoids which are non-regular. We will discuss this failure at the end of this section.

If the Markus Conjecture holds for integral affine orbifolds (see Section
12
), then PMCTs of compact type are complete. For complete, s-proper Poisson manifolds we obtain the following structural theorem:

Theorem 11.3
.

If
(M,\pi)
is a complete, s-proper Poisson manifold, then it has a unique minimal infinitesimal stratum
\Sigma_{0}
. Its Weyl group coincides with the classical Weyl group of the isotropy Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
, for any
x\in\Sigma_{0}
.

This result – and its more detailed version given later in this section – suggests that all the information about a complete, s-proper Poisson manifold is encoded in the first jet of
(M,\pi)
along the minimal stratum
\Sigma_{0}
.

The previous results provide deep insight into the Poisson geometry of PMCTs and its relation to Lie theory. Hence, one can use them to deduce some classical results in Poisson geometry related to Lie theory. For example, we have the following somewhat surprising application of Theorem
11.1
.

Corollary 11.4
.

There exists no Lie bialgebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{*})
with both
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
semisimple Lie algebras of compact type.

11.1.
Polynomial functions on integral affine orbifolds

An affine manifold
N
has a well-defined subalgebra of polynomial functions

{\mathrm{Pol}}(N)\subset C^{\infty}(N).

Namely, we say that
P\in C^{\infty}(N)
is a
polynomial
if its pullback to every
affine chart of
N
is a polynomial. Assuming that
N
is connected, the degree of a polynomial is the degree on its pullback to any affine chart. It is well-defined since affine transformations on
\mathbb{R}^{q}
preserve the degree of polynomials in
\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{q}]
.

The developing map of an affine manifold provides useful information on its algebra of polynomial functions.

Proposition 11.5
.

For an affine manifold
N
, its developing map
\dev:N^{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
gives an isomorphism of rings

(\dev^{*})^{-1}\circ p^{*}:{\mathrm{Pol}}(N)\to(\mathbb{R}[x^{1},\dots,x^{q}])^{\Gamma^{\mathrm{aff}}},

where
p:N^{\mathrm{aff}}\to N
is the affine holonomy cover and
\Gamma^{\mathrm{aff}}
the affine holonomy group.

Proof.

For the proof we will use the abbreviated notations
\widetilde{N}:=N^{\mathrm{aff}}
and
\Gamma:=\Gamma^{\mathrm{aff}}
. We will show that both

p^{*}:{\mathrm{Pol}}(N)\to{\mathrm{Pol}}(\widetilde{N})^{\Gamma},\quad\dev^{*}:\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{q}]^{\Gamma}\to{\mathrm{Pol}}(\widetilde{N})^{\Gamma}

are isomorphisms.

For the first map we note that pullback by the covering map is an isomorphism

p^{*}:C^{\infty}(N)\to C^{\infty}(N)^{\Gamma}
.
It sends polynomials to polynomials, and any invariant polynomial comes from one in
N
. Hence the result follows.

For the second map we recall that an affine manifold has a canonical real analytic structure, and that real analytic functions on a (connected) manifold agree if and only if they agree on an open subset.
We apply this first to
\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{q}]
to deduce
that two polynomials on
\mathbb{R}^{q}
agree if and only if they agree in the image of the developing map. This implies that

\dev^{*}:\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{q}]\to{\mathrm{Pol}}(\widetilde{N})

is injective. To prove surjectivity we restrict any given

P\in{\mathrm{Pol}}(\widetilde{N})
to a connected open subset
U\subset\widetilde{N}
on which the developing map is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Then
P|_{U}
is the pullback by
\dev
of the restriction to
\dev(U)
of a polynomial
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{q}]
. Therefore,
\dev^{*}P_{0}=P
.
Finally, since
\dev
is equivariant with respect to the actions of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma
, the restriction to the respective subalgebras of invariants

\dev^{*}:\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{q}]^{\Gamma}\to{\mathrm{Pol}}(\widetilde{N})^{\Gamma}

is an isomorphism.
∎

Every hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the zero set of a degree one polynomial. The polynomial is unique up to rescaling by a non-zero real number. We say that the hyperplane is integral if the polynomial can be chosen to have rational degree one coefficients. In this case, up to sign, it is the zero set of a unique polynomial whose degree one coefficients are coprime integers.

For an integral affine manifold
N
, by an
integral hyperplane
of
N
we mean a connected, codimension one, integral affine submanifold. A degree one polynomial on
N
is called
primitive
if its pullback via
some
integral affine chart is a degree one polymonial for which non-constant coefficients are coprime integers. Since an integral change of coordinates has linear part a matrix with determinant
\pm 1
, it follows that this holds for any integral affine chart.

Proposition 11.6
.

Let
N
be a integral affine manifold with trivial affine holonomy. Then any
P\in{\mathrm{Pol}}(N)
has polynomial factorization

P=L_{1}\cdots L_{k}\cdot Q,

where the
L_{i}
’s are primitive degree one polynomials with non-empty vanishing locus and the vanishing locus of
Q
does not contain an integral affine hyperplane. The factorization is unique up to sign changes and the ordering of the primitive degree one polynomials.

Proof.

Since the affine holonomy is trivial, by Proposition
11.5
, every
P\in{\mathrm{Pol}}(N)
has a unique factorization into irreducible polynomials (up to units and order). An irreducible polynomial on
N
of degree greater than one cannot vanish on a hyperplane: if it were to vanish, its restriction to a chart around a point in the hyperplane would have a degree one factor, and so using the developing map, we see that we could factor out a linear term. Therefore, in the factorization of
P
only the linear terms can vanish on a hyperplane. We can single out those vanishing on integral hyperplanes (if any), and normalize them to be primitive (we may get the same primitive factor more than once). If
Q
denotes the product of the remaining polynomials in the factorization of
P
then, by construction, it does not vanish on any integral affine hyperplane. The uniqueness in the statement is a consequence of the uniqueness of factorizations in
{\mathrm{Pol}}(N)
.
∎

For a classical integral affine orbifold
B
by an
integral hyperplane
we mean a connected, codimension one, integral affine suborbifold. A polynomial on
B
is a function whose pullback to
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
(an integral affine manifold) is a polynomial. If the pullback polynomial is primitive of degree one we say that the original polynomial on the orbifold is primitive of degree one.

Proposition 11.7
.

Let
B
be a classical integral affine orbifold. Then for any
P\in{\mathrm{Pol}}(B)
there exists a finite orbifold cover
q:\widetilde{B}\to B

and a factorization:

(11.1)

q^{*}P=L_{1}\cdots L_{k}\cdot Q,

where the
L_{i}
’s are primitive degree one polynomials with non-empty vanishing locus and the vanishing locus of
Q
does not contain an integral affine hyperplane.

Proof.

We will call a factorization as in the statement a
primitive factorization
. By Proposition
11.6
, the the pullback of
P\in{\mathrm{Pol}}(B)
to the universal covering space
p:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to B
has a primitive factorization:

\widetilde{P}=p^{*}P=L^{\prime}_{1}\cdots L^{\prime}_{k}\cdot Q^{\prime}.

We may further assume that there are no degree one primitive polynomials with opposite sign and that all repeated primitive polynomials are in contiguous positions. The polynomial
\widetilde{P}
is
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
-invariant, so given any element
a\in\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
, by the properties of the vanishing sets of the factors and uniqueness of factorization we have:

a^{*}\widetilde{P}:=\varepsilon_{1}(a)L^{\prime}_{\sigma_{1}(a)}\cdots\varepsilon_{k}(a)L^{\prime}_{\sigma_{k}(a)}\cdot\varepsilon(a)Q^{\prime},

where

(\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{k},\sigma):\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\to\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{2}\rtimes\mathrm{S}_{k},\quad\varepsilon:\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}

define representations of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
on the group of signed permutations and on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, respectively. Let us denote by
H\subset\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
the kernel of the homomorphism

\left((\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{k},\sigma),\varepsilon\right):\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\to(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}\rtimes\mathrm{S}_{k})\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Since
H
has finite index we obtain the finite orbifold cover
q:\widetilde{B}:=\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}/H\to B
, together with a polynomial defined on it with the properties in the statement.
∎

It is a non-trivial classical fact that every polynomial on a compact integral affine
manifold
is constant (see
[
26
]
). Note that this fact is obvious if the manifold is known to be complete and this still holds for compact, complete orbifolds. Without the assumption of completeness we are able to prove the following result which suffices for our purpose.

Corollary 11.8
.

A non-trivial polynomial on a compact integral affine orbifold
B
cannot vanish on an integral hyperplane.

Proof.

Let
P\in{\mathrm{Pol}}(B)
be a non-trivial polynomial that vanishes on an integral hyperplane. We apply Proposition
11.7
to
P
, obtaining a finite orbifold cover
q:\widetilde{B}\to B
where
q^{*}P
has a factorization (
11.1
) with
k\geq 1
. Since
\widetilde{B}
is compact, any such primitive factor
L
has a critical point and we find a contradiction: if we consider the pullback
L^{\prime}
of
L
to the orbifold universal covering space, we have by Proposition
11.5

L^{\prime}=\dev^{*}L_{0},

where
L_{0}\in\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{q}]
is a degree one polynomial. But a degree one polynomial in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
has no critical points, and, therefore,
L
cannot have critical points.

∎

11.2.
Consequences for PMCTs

We will now use the volume polynomial to draw important consequences for PMTC’s.

11.2.1.
PMCTs of compact type

We give a quick proof that every Poisson manifold of compact type must be regular.

Theorem 11.9
.

If
(M,\pi)
is a compact Poisson manifold that admits a proper integration, then
\pi
is regular.

Proof.

Observe that if
(M,\pi)
is proper with leaf space
B
, then it follows from Corollary
5.9
that the subregular infinitesimal strata are integral hyperplanes in the classical integral affine orbifold
B
. This subregular locus is non-empty iff
\pi
is non-regular.

So assume that
(M,\pi)
is proper, compact and non-regular. First, we apply Theorem
4.2
to construct the Weyl resolution of
(M,\pi)
. By Theorem
5.7
, the leaf space
B
of
(M,\pi)
inherits
an integral affine orbifold structure. Second, we apply Theorem
9.5

to deduce that the square of the volume defines a polynomial
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{2}:B\to\mathbb{R}

whose zero set is the singular part of
B
. Third, because
(M,\pi)
is non-regular a subregular stratum
B_{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{S}(B)
exists and is an integral affine suborbifold of
B
contained in its singular part. Therefore we have produced a polynomial on
B
with properties that contradict the statement of Corollary
11.8
.
∎

Corollary 11.10
.

There exists no Lie bialgebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{*})
with both
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
semisimple Lie algebras of compact type.

Proof.

Any Lie bialgebra integrates to Poisson-Lie group structures
(G,\pi_{G})
and
(G^{*},\pi_{G^{*}})
on the 1-connected integrations of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Under the assumption in the statement, both groups are compact. Therefore (see, e.g.,
[
29
, Section 11.4]
) the 1-connected integration of the double
\mathfrak{d}:=\mathfrak{g}\Join\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a double Lie group
D:=G\Join G^{*}
carrying two distinct symplectic groupoid structures that integrate
(G,\pi_{G})
and
(G^{*},\pi_{G^{*}})
. Since
D
is compact, by Theorem
11.9
,
(G,\pi_{G})
and
(G^{*},\pi_{G^{*}})
must be regular. This forces
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}=\{0\}
.
∎

11.2.2.
Complete, s-proper PMCTs

Recall that a Poisson manifold of proper type is complete if its leaf space
B
is a complete integral affine orbifold, i.e., if the developing map
\dev:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
is a diffeomorphism. Notice also that the connected components of the preimages of the subregular strata in the orbifold universal covering space
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
are contained in the hyperplanes defined the reflections of the Weyl group of the Poisson manifold. The latter are closed integral affine hyperplanes.

This allows us to show the following structural theorem for this class of Poisson manifolds. In the statement we use the decomposition
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)=\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)\rtimes\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})
from Proposition
6.14
.

Theorem 11.11
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a complete, s-proper, Poisson manifold, and denote its leaf space by
B
. Then:

(i)

There exists a unique minimal infinitesimal stratum
(\Sigma_{0},\pi_{0})
, which is a regular, complete, s-proper Poisson manifold with
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})=\pi_{1}(M)
;

(ii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
denote the isotropy group of any point
x\in\Sigma_{0}
. Then

\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)\cong W,

where
W
is the classical Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
relative to a maximal torus
\mathfrak{t}
;

(iii)

There is an isomorphism of integral affine manifolds

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\cong\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{\Sigma_{0}}\times\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{\mathrm{ss}},

where
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathrm{ss}}
is a maximal torus of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{ss}}:=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
.
Under this isomorphism, the action of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)=\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)\rtimes\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})
has the following properties:

-

\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
acts trivially on the first factor and via the classical action on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{\mathrm{ss}}
;

-

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})
leaves
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{\Sigma_{0}}\times\{0\}
invariant and its action on it factors through the action of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{\Sigma_{0}})
.

Proof of Theorem
11.11
.

Consider the pullback of the square of the volume polynomial to
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{q}

p^{*}\mathcal{V}_{0}^{2}:\mathbb{R}^{q}\to\mathbb{R}

This is a polynomial has a function that vanishes precisely on the singular part of
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{q}
. By Proposition
7.7
the singular part agrees with the union of the hyperplanes of the reflections of the Weyl group of the Poisson manifold of proper type. The factorization of
p^{*}\mathcal{V}_{0}^{2}
provided by Proposition
11.6
shows that the number of these hyperplanes is bounded by the number of degree one primitive factors
L_{1},\dots,L_{k}
, and hence are finite in number. It follows that the number of chambers of the corresponding hyperplane arrangement in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
is also finite, and hence the Weyl group is finite.

Now, applying Lemma
3.30
, one obtains a
\mathcal{W}
-invariant decomposition of integral affine subspaces

(11.2)

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}\oplus V,

where one can identify the fixed point set with the intersection of all root hyperplanes

(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}=\bigcap_{r\in\mathcal{W}}\mathcal{H}_{r}.

Note that this fixed-point set
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}
is stable under the action of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
since this action preserves the set of hyperplanes.
It follows that
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}
is the preimage of an infinitesimal stratum
B_{\Sigma_{0}}
under the covering projection
p:\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to B
. Moreover, all other infinitesimal stratum have dimension greater than
\Sigma_{0}
. By Theorem
3.13
, we conclude that item (i) holds, with the exception of the statement about
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})
.

If
x\in\Sigma_{0}
, then
{\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x)\cong G/N(T)
where
G
is a compact Lie integrating the isotropy Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
and
T\subset G
is a maximal torus. Hence, item (ii) follows from the last statement in Proposition
7.10
, since
{\widehat{x}}
is a fixed point of
\mathcal{W}
. It also follows that the
\mathcal{W}
-invariant decomposition (
11.2
) can be written as

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}\oplus\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{\mathrm{ss}},

that the action of
\mathcal{W}=W
on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{\mathrm{ss}}
is the classical action.

Since
B
is assumed complete, we have

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=B^{\mathrm{aff}},\quad{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{aff}},\quad\Gamma^{\mathrm{aff}}=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B),\quad\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\cong\mathcal{W}\cong W,

where the last isomorphisms follow from Proposition
6.8
and item (ii). The main diagram (
5.5
) then yields:

(11.3)

p^{-1}({\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0})\quad

\quad{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0}\quad

\quad\Sigma_{0}\quad

\textstyle{{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=\widehat{M}^{{\mathrm{aff}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=B^{{\mathrm{aff}}}=\mathbb{R}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}

(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}\quad

B_{\Sigma_{0}}\quad

B_{\Sigma_{0}}\quad

Here the map
p:{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to\widehat{M}
is a covering map with covering group
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
and

p^{-1}({\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0})=q^{-1}((\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}})).

We now prove item (iii). Recall that, by Theorem
5.8
,
B_{\Sigma_{0}}
is a suborbifold of
B
, which therefore has the (ineffective) atlas

(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}\rtimes\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\rightrightarrows(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}.

Effectivization of this action amounts to quotient by the subgroup
N\subset\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
consisting of elements that fix
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}
. This results on the effective atlas for
B_{\Sigma_{0}}

(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}\rtimes\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)/N\rightrightarrows(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}.

Since
(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}
is simply connected, this implies – cf. (
6.6
) – that

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{\Sigma_{0}})\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)/N,

and that we can identify

(\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}\cong\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{\Sigma_{0}}.

Obviously,
\mathcal{W}\subset N
, so the splitting in Proposition
6.14
gives a surjective morphism

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)/\mathcal{W}\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)/N\cong\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{\Sigma_{0}}).

In particular, the action of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})
on
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}_{\Sigma_{0}}
factors through the action of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{\Sigma_{0}})
.

Finally, to prove the first identity in item (i), we claim that:

Lemma 11.12
.

The inclusion
i:{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0}\hookrightarrow\widehat{M}
induces an isomorphism

\pi_{1}({\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(\widehat{M}).

To prove this lemma, observe that

p^{-1}({\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0})
is connected and
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
-invariant, since it equals
q^{-1}((\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})^{\mathcal{W}}))
and
q:{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is a
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
-equivariant. It follows that

(11.4)

p:p^{-1}({\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0})\to{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0},

is a covering with covering group
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
.
Also,
q:{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\to\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
is a trivial fibration since the base
\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{q}
is contractible (by completeness) and the fibers are compact and connected (by s-properness). It follows that
p^{-1}({\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0})
is a deformation retract of
{\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
.
Therefore, the top left square of (
11.3
) induces a diagram of fundamental groups

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(p^{-1}({\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}({\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}({\widehat{M}}^{{\mathrm{orb}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

where the left vertical arrow is an isomorphism, so the lemma holds.

Now, to prove (iii), notice that the restriction of the resolution
{\mathscr{R}}:{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0}\to\Sigma_{0}
is a submersion with compact connected fibers diffeomorphic to
G/N(T)
. It follows that we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{2}(\Sigma_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}({\mathscr{R}}^{-1}(x))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}({\widehat{\Sigma}}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{R}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

and we already know that the left two vertical arrows are isomorphisms. Hence, we obtain the first isomorphism of item (iii):

\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(M).

∎

11.3.
A class of complete, s-proper, PMCTs

We consider a class of Poisson manifolds obtained by fixing the following data:

(a)

A Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, semi-simple of compact type;

(b)

A finite group
\Gamma\subset\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
;

(c)

A compact symplectic manifold
(S,\omega_{S})
with fundamental group
\Gamma
.

Letting
q:\widetilde{S}\to S
denote the universal covering space with symplectic form
\omega_{\widetilde{S}}:=q^{*}\omega_{S}
, the class of Poisson manifolds we are interested take the form

M=\widetilde{S}\times_{\Gamma}\mathfrak{g}^{*},

where the Poisson structure
\pi_{M}
is the quotient of the product
\omega_{\widetilde{S}}^{-1}\times\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
.

Claim:

(M,\pi_{M})
is a complete, s-proper, Poisson manifold.

We will prove this claim by showing that
(M,\pi_{M})
is a (global) local linear model, as in Section
2.2
. The base of its principal bundle
P
will be the compact symplectic manifold
S
and the structural group
G
will be the union of all connected components of
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
intersecting
\Gamma
. Classical Lie theory grants that
G
is a possibly disconnected compact Lie group integrating
\mathfrak{g}
.

To see how
M
arises from such linear local model, consider the symplectic manifold
\widetilde{S}\times T^{*}G
with symplectic form
{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\omega_{\widetilde{S}}+{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{can}}
. It carries a symplectic action of
\Gamma
defined by

\gamma\cdot(s,\alpha):=(s\gamma^{-1},\gamma\alpha),

where the action on the factor
T^{*}G
is via the lift of the left action of
G
on itself. On the other hand, it carries a symplectic action of
G
given by

g\cdot(s,\alpha):=(s,\alpha g^{-1}),

where now we use the lift of the right action of
G
on itself. Note that these are free and proper commuting actions, and their orbit spaces make up the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\times T^{*}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma\,\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{G\,\circlearrowright}
\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\times_{\Gamma}T^{*}G{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\cong\widetilde{S}\times_{\Gamma}G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}

\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright\,G}
\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma\,\circlearrowright}
\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\times_{\Gamma}\mathfrak{g}^{*}}

The isomorphism on the upper right corner comes from trivializing the cotangent bundle using left translations. Since the actions are symplectic, there are induced symplectic/Poisson structures on each space of the previous diagram:

\textstyle{({\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{*}\omega_{\widetilde{S}}+{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{can}})^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}})^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\omega_{\widetilde{S}}^{-1}\times\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{M}}

Here, on the right upper corner, we have the symplectic form

\omega^{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin}}:=p^{*}\omega_{S}-\mathrm{d}\langle\theta,\cdot\rangle\in\Omega^{2}(P\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}),

where
\theta
denotes the flat connection on the (connected) principal
G
-bundle

P:=\widetilde{S}\times_{\Gamma}G\to S,

whose pullback to
\widetilde{S}\times G
is the trivial connection. This is precisely the closed,
G
-invariant, 2-form (
2.5
) of the local model, which in this case is globally symplectic since the connection is flat. This shows that
(M,\pi_{M})
is a linear local model and proves the claim.

Since
(M,\pi_{M})
is a linear local model, it follows from Example
6.19
that it has

(a)

complete leaf space
B
with orbifold fundamental group

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)\cong N(T)/Z_{G}(T),

where
T\subset G
is a maximal torus, while its Weyl group is

\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)\cong W=(N(T)\cap G^{0})/T.

(b)

\widetilde{B}^{{\mathrm{orb}}}=\mathfrak{t}^{*}
, where
\mathfrak{t}
is the Lie algebra of
T
, and the
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B)
-action is the adjoint action of
N(T)/Z_{G}(T)
.

To connect with the results of Theorem
11.3
, note that the minimal stratum of
(M,\pi)
consists of a single symplectic leaf,

\Sigma_{0}=\widetilde{S}\times_{\Gamma}\{0\}\cong S,

since any other leaf has dimension strictly larger than
S
. Any point in this stratum has isotropy isomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}
, so (a) and (b) agree with the results of Theorem
11.3
.

Finally, observe that the split short exact sequence of Proposition
6.14
becomes

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(N(T)\cap G^{0})/T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N(T)/Z_{G}(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G/Z_{G}(T)G^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

)The group
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})\cong G/Z_{G}(T)G^{0}
is non-trivial if and only if some element in
\Gamma
acts non-trivially on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}/G^{0}
. In this case
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B^{\mathrm{reg}})
is non-trivial while
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(B_{\Sigma_{0}})
is trivial. For a concrete example, one can take any
\Gamma\subset\Aut(\mathfrak{so}(2m))
, with
m\geq 4
, which is not contained in the connected components of the identity.

11.4.
Remarks on Theorem
11.1
and twists.

The crucial ingredient in the proof of Theorem
11.1
is the polynomial nature of the symplectic volume function. In particular, this theorem does not hold for general twisted Dirac structures, as shown for instance by the example of the Cartan-Dirac structures on compact Lie groups
G
. For these, as we saw in
Section
8.2
,
the twisted presymplectic leaves are the conjugacy classes, so as long as
G
is not abelian, the twisted Dirac structure is never regular.

Lying between Poisson and general twisted Dirac structures one has the class of
\phi
-twisted Poisson structures. For the latter, the Poisson condition for
\pi
is replaced by
[\pi,\pi]=\pi^{\sharp}(\phi)
. Theorem
11.1
fails even for
\phi
-twisted Poisson structures.

Example 11.13
.

A
\phi
-twisted Poisson can be seen as
\phi
-twisted Dirac structures
L
satisfying
L\cap TM=\{0\}
. It follows that, at the global level,
\phi
-twisted Poisson structures correspond to
\phi
-twisted presymplectic groupoids
(\Sigma,\Omega)
with
non-degenerate
presymplectic form
\Omega
. Hence, for example, the Cartan-Dirac structure in the case of
G=\mathrm{SU}(2)
becomes a non-regular twisted Poisson structure that integrates to a compact groupoid.

The foliation of the Cartan-Dirac structure on
\mathrm{SU}(2)=\mathbb{S}^{3}
in the previous example consists of two zeros at the north and south poles (i.e.,
I
and
-I
) and a family of 2-spheres. Hence, the twist
\phi
(the Cartan 3-form), actually vanishes on the leaves, so
[\pi,\pi]=0
. However, as an untwisted Poisson manifold,
(\mathbb{S}^{3},\pi)
is non-integrable: the symplectic area of the spheres has a critical point.

Generalizing this example, assume that
\pi
is an honest Poisson structure on
M
and that one has a
closed 3-form
\phi\in\Omega^{3}(M)
that vanishes on its symplectic leaves, that is, satisfying

\phi(\pi^{\sharp}\alpha,\pi^{\sharp}\beta,\pi^{\sharp}\gamma)=0,

for all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\Omega^{1}(M)
.
We call such a 3-form a
fake twist of
\pi
. It allows us to interpret
\pi
both as a Poisson structure as well as a twisted one. The example above shows that a fake twist can turn a possibly non-regular (even non-integrable!) Poisson structure to one of compact type.

The regular case is easier to handle and sheds light on the difficulty of the non-regular case as well. The key remark is that, starting with a regular Poisson structure
\pi
and a fake twist
\phi
, when interpreted as a
\pi
-twisted Poisson structure, the corresponding foliation remains the same. Actually, the resulting algebroid of the
\phi
-twisted
\pi
is still
T^{*}M
, with the same anchor
\rho=\pi^{\sharp}
, but with the modified Lie bracket

(11.5)

[\alpha,\beta]_{\pi,\phi}=[\alpha,\beta]_{\pi}+i_{\pi^{\sharp}(\alpha)}i_{\pi^{\sharp}(\beta)}\phi.

Therefore, for compactness to be achieved from the twisted perspective, the original symplectic foliation must be of compact type and must admit a transverse integral affine structure. In fact, this is all one needs.

Proposition 11.14
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a regular Poisson manifold whose foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
is of
\mathcal{C}
-type and admits a transverse integral affine structure. Then there exists a fake twist
\phi
such that
\pi
is of
\mathcal{C}
-type when viewed as a
\phi
-twisted Poisson structure.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\omega}\in\Omega^{2}(M)
be an extension of the leafwise symplectic form with the property that

\widetilde{\omega}(\pi^{\sharp}(\alpha),X)=\alpha(X)
for all
X
and
\alpha
(it exists, since
\pi
is regular) and set
\phi=\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}
. Note that the extension
\widetilde{\omega}
determines a splitting of the short exact sequence:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\nu^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\pi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{*}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\mathcal{F}_{\pi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\cdot}\widetilde{\omega}}
\textstyle{0}

The middle term will be interpreted as the
the algebroid
A^{\phi}
corresponding to
\pi
viewed as a
\phi
-twisted Poisson, as discussed above, with Lie bracket (
11.5
). We claim that the splitting of the sequence becomes an algebroid splitting of
A^{\phi}
. In other words,

i_{[X,Y]}\widetilde{\omega}=[i_{X}\widetilde{\omega},i_{Y}\widetilde{\omega}]_{\pi,\phi},

for any
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{F}_{\pi})
. To see this we can assume that
X=\pi^{\sharp}(\alpha)
,
Y=\pi^{\sharp}(\beta)
, so that
i_{X}\widetilde{\omega}=\alpha
and
i_{Y}\widetilde{\omega}=\beta
. Then:

\displaystyle[i_{X}\widetilde{\omega},i_{Y}\widetilde{\omega}]_{\pi,\phi}

\displaystyle=[\alpha,\beta]_{\pi}+i_{\pi^{\sharp}(\alpha)}i_{\pi^{\sharp}(\beta)}\phi

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\pounds}_{\pi^{\sharp}(\alpha)}\beta-i_{\pi^{\sharp}(\beta)}\mathrm{d}\alpha-i_{\pi^{\sharp}(\beta)}i_{\pi^{\sharp}(\alpha)}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\pounds}_{X}i_{Y}\widetilde{\omega}-i_{Y}\boldsymbol{\pounds}_{X}\widetilde{\omega}=i_{[X,Y]}\widetilde{\omega}

We deduce that the algebroid
A^{\phi}
is isomorphic to the semi-direct product algebroid
T\mathcal{F}_{\pi}\ltimes\nu^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\pi})
, which is just the algebroid associated with the Dirac structure defined by
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
. This algebroid admits an integration of
\mathcal{C}
-type, namely
\Hol(\mathcal{F}_{\pi})\ltimes\nu^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{\pi})/\Lambda
, where
\Lambda
is the transverse integral affine structure (see
[
11
, Section 4.4]

∎

12.
Open Problems

In this closing section we list some questions and open problems that arise naturally from our work and that we believe are interesting and important to further understand the geometry of PMCTs and DMCTs.

Finite Coxeter groups of non-Lie type and the Weyl group

Theorem
7.5
shows that the Weyl group of a Poisson manifold of proper type is a Coxeter group. The examples discussed in Section
8.3
show that such a Poisson manifold can have a Weyl group that is not isomorphic to either a classical Weyl group of a compact Lie algebra or an affine Weyl group of some compact Lie group. We note that all the examples in that section have Weyl groups of infinite order.

Recall that finite Coxeter groups are classified, and among them, there exists the class of reflection groups of Lie type, i.e., those that arise as Weyl groups of some compact Lie algebra. This raises the following problem.

Open Problem 1
.

Is there a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
of proper type with a finite Weyl group
\mathcal{W}(M,\pi)
that is not of Lie type?

For example, can one find an example of a Poisson manifold of proper type with Weyl group isomorphic to
D_{5}
?

Remark 12.1
.

After the submission of this paper and posting it in the arXiv, Joshua Mundiger send us a solution to this problem, showing that the answer is negative – see Appendix
A
.

Duistermaat-Heckman theory for Dirac manifolds of compact types

Our treatement of Duistermaat-Heckman theory in Sections
9
and
10
focussed entirely on the Poisson case. However, one can wonder what happens in the general Dirac setting. On the one hand, Weyl integration formulas have versions both for Lie algebras and for Lie groups, see e.g.
[
22
]
. On the other hand,
the work of Alekseev, Meinrenken and Woodward
[
3
]
develops Duistermaat-Heckman theory for Lie-group valued moment maps. The discussion in
[
1
]
shows that a generalization of the theory to the general Dirac setting is not straightforward – but also provides some ideas on how to proceed.

Open Problem 2
.

Extend the Duistermaat-Heckmann theory to the setting of Dirac manifolds of compact types.

Reductive holomorphic Poisson structures

The complex counterparts of compact Lie algebras (respectively, compact Lie groups) are complex reductive Lie algebras (respectively, complex reductive Lie groups). Many results that hold for compact Lie algebras and groups have analogues in the context of complex reductive Lie algebras and groups. Since many of the results in this paper can be interpreted as generalizations of classical results for compact Lie algebras and Lie groups to Poisson geometry, it is natural to ask the following:

Open Problem 3
.

Define the notion of a reductive holomorphic Poisson/Dirac manifold and establish an analogue of the theory of Poisson manifolds of proper type in the holomorphic context.

For example, reductive holomorphic Poisson (respectively, Dirac) manifolds should include, as examples, holomorphic symplectic manifolds and holomorphic linear Poisson structures on the duals of complex reductive Lie algebras (respectively, the Cartan-Dirac structures on complex reductive Lie groups). The work of Evens and Lu on the Grothendieck-Springer resolution for complex semi-simple Poisson-Lie groups
[
23
]
should also provide some clues to solve this problem.

Markus conjecture for integral affine orbifolds

Recall that for an affine manifold the Markus conjecture states that a compact affine manifold is complete iff it has a parallel volume (see, e.g.,
[
26
]
)). For integral affine manifolds a parallel volume always exist, and so the conjecture says that every compact integral affine manifold is complete.

Our results concerning polynomial functions on integral affine orbifolds in Section
11.1
suggest that the conjecture should hold for such orbifolds.

Open Problem 4
.

Show that every compact integral affine manifold is complete.

It is proved in
[
26
]
that every polynomial on a compact integral affine
manifold
is constant. This result would follow easily from the Markus conjecture. Similarly, solving the above question would imply that every polynomial on a compact integral affine
orbifold
is constant. Corollary
11.8
provides some evidence for this conjecture.

For us, a positive answer to this problem would have the consequence that every compact PMCT (or DMCT) is complete. It is an intriguing question if, conversely, one can use Poisson geometry to give a proof of the Markus conjecture.

Classification of complete s-proper PMCTs

Theorem
11.11
provides structural results for complete, s-proper, PMCTs. The results in that theorem – see also Section
11.3
– suggest that the following characterization of such PMCTs should hold:

Open Problem 5
.

Given a complete, s-proper, Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
show that it is isomorphic to

(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{0}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\mathrm{ss}})/\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0}),

where
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{0}
is the universal covering space of the minimal strata and
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{ss}}=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
is the semisimple part of the isotropy Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of any point in
\Sigma_{0}
.

Notice that one can think of this as a global linearization result around the minimal strata. A special case of this is the classical linearization result of Ginzburg-Weinstein
[
25
]
. In fact, let
(G^{*},\pi_{G^{*}})
be the dual of a compact, semi-simple, Poisson-Lie group
(G,\pi_{G})
. Since the dressing action of
G
on
G^{*}
is complete, it follows that
G^{*}
is a s-proper, complete, Poisson manifold. The minimal strata
\Sigma_{0}
consists of the identity of
G^{*}
. So if the result above holds, one obtains a Poisson isomorphism

G^{*}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{*},

Hence, one recovers the global linearization result of Ginzburg and Weinstein.

Multiplicity free spaces and symplectic gerbes

Recall that, given a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
, a
symplectic realization
consists of a symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
and a surjective submersion
\mu:(X,\omega)\to(M,\pi)
that is a Poisson map. It is called
isotropic
when its fibers are isotropic submanifols. Regular Poisson manifolds are characterized among Poisson manifolds by the existence of isotropic realizations.

In
[
19
]
, Dazord and Delzant showed that an isotropic realization
\mu:(X,\omega)\to(M,\pi)
, with
\mu
proper, induces a transverse affine structure on
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
. Conversely, given a transverse integral affine structure
\Lambda
on
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
, they showed that the existence of a proper isotropic realization of
(M,\pi)
inducing
\Lambda
is obstructed by a certain class.

In
[
12
]
, we extended the Dazord-Delzant theory. In one direction, we showed that a proper isotropic realization
\mu:(X,\omega)\to(M,\pi)
of a Poisson manifold comes with a canonical proper symplectic integration of
(M,\pi)
. It is characterized as the smallest symplectic integration that acts on the realization, and its isotropy bundle has connected component of the identity
T^{*}M/\Lambda
, where
\Lambda
is the transverse integral affine structure determined by the given realization. In the opposite direction, we showed that any regular, proper symplectic groupoid defines a symplectic gerbe over its leaf space, which is classified by a symplectic version of the Dixmier-Douady class. Moreover, we proved that the latter class pulls back to the class of Dazord and Delzant, and that it is trivial if and only if there exists a complete isotropic realization providing a symplectic Morita equivalence with a symplectic torus bundle.

One would like to extend these results beyond the regular case. The natural generalization of isotropic realizations is
multiplicity-free
symplectic realizations: a symplectic realization
\mu:(X,\omega)\to M
of
(M,\pi)
such that each isotropy Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
acts transitively on the fibers of
\mu
. However, one must relax the condition that
\mu
be a submersion; otherwise one remains in the regular case.

Open Problem 6
.

Develop the theory of (non-singular) multiplicity-free realizations of Poisson manifolds of proper type.

One possible approach is to consider pre-symplectic “isotropic realizations” of the Weyl resolution of
(M,\pi)
. In fact, one expects that a suitable version of the Weyl resolution can be constructed for the entire realization. The correct notion should allow to extend the above notions and results to the non-regular case, including versions of the Dazord-Delzant class, symplectic gerbes, and their Dixmier-Douady classes for non-regular PMCTs.

Appendix A
Finite Weyl Groups of PMCTs are of Lie type

by
Joshua Mundinger

The purpose of this appendix is to show that if
N
is a simply connected integral affine manifold and
W
is a finite integral affine reflection group on
N
(Definition
7.1
), then
W
is a Weyl group. This solves Open Problem 1.

Lemma A.1

(
[
27
]
, p. 39)
.

Suppose that
W
is a finite Coxeter group, generated by reflections
s_{\alpha}
with
m_{\alpha\beta}=ord(s_{\alpha}s_{\beta})
. Then
W
is a Weyl group if and only if
m_{\alpha\beta}\in\{2,3,4,6\}
for all
\alpha\neq\beta
.

Recall from §
7.1
that a
geometric reflection
on a connected manifold
N
is a smooth involution
r:N\to N
such that the fixed-point set
N^{r}
is a codimension one submanifold separating
N
. By
[
18
, Lemma 10.1.3]
,
N\setminus N^{r}
has exactly two components which are exchanged by
r
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
N
be a simply connected manifold and let
s
and
t
be geometric reflections on
N
. If
st
has finite order, then
N^{s}\cap N^{t}
is not empty.

Proof.

Suppose
N^{s}\cap N^{t}
is empty.
Let
U=N\setminus N^{s}
and
V=N\setminus N^{t}
.
Since
s
and
t
are geometric reflections on a connected manifold,

U
has two components
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
exchanged by
s
, and
V
has two components
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
exchanged by
t
.
Consider the Mayer-Vietoris sequence associated to open covering
N=U\cup V
. Since
H_{1}(N)=0
,
N\setminus(N^{s}\cup N^{t})=U\cap V
has exactly three components, say
W_{1},W_{2},W_{3}
, which are each contained in a unique pairwise intersection
U_{i}\cap V_{j}
for some
i
and
j
.
Without loss of generality,

\displaystyle W_{1}

\displaystyle\subseteq U_{1}\cap V_{1},

\displaystyle W_{2}

\displaystyle\subseteq U_{2}\cap V_{1},

\displaystyle W_{3}

\displaystyle\subseteq U_{2}\cap V_{2}.

Thus
U_{1}\cap V_{2}
is empty, so since
U_{1}
and
V_{2}
are connected,
U_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
and
V_{2}\subseteq U_{2}
.
Thus
W_{1}=U_{1}
and
W_{3}=V_{2}
.

Figure 3.
The connected components of
N\setminus(N^{s}\cup N^{t})
.

We proceed by ping-pong. Now
st(W_{2})\subseteq s(t(V_{1}))\subseteq s(V_{2})\subseteq s(U_{2})=U_{1}=W_{1}
.
Similarly,

st(W_{1})\subseteq s(t(V_{1}))=s(V_{2})\subseteq s(U_{2})=U_{1}=W_{1}
.
Hence
(st)^{n}(W_{2})\subseteq W_{1}
for all
n>0
, so
st
has infinite order.
∎

Theorem A.3
.

Let
N
be a simply connected integral affine manifold and
W
be a finite integral affine reflection group on
N
.
Then
W
is a Weyl group.

Proof.

Say
\{s_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
is a generating set of reflections for
W
and
H_{\alpha}=N^{s_{\alpha}}
.
Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be distinct elements of
I
.
Since
W
is finite, Lemma
A.2
implies that
H_{\alpha}\cap H_{\beta}\neq\varnothing
. Let
x\in H_{\alpha}\cap H_{\beta}
.
Define
\sigma_{\alpha}=\mathrm{d}_{x}s_{\alpha}
,
\sigma_{\beta}=\mathrm{d}_{x}s_{\beta}
. Then
\sigma_{\alpha}
and
\sigma_{\beta}
are reflections on
V=T_{x}N
.
Since
W
acts by integral affine transformations, both
\sigma_{\alpha}
and
\sigma_{\beta}
fix some lattice
L\subseteq V
.
Thus
{\mathrm{tr}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\sigma_{\beta})\in\mathbb{Z}
.
On the other hand,
\sigma_{\alpha}\sigma_{\beta}
is a product of two reflections, so it has a fixed subspace of codimension 2 and is a rotation by some angle
\theta
on a complementary plane.
Then
{\mathrm{tr}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\sigma_{\beta})=2\cos(\theta)+(\dim V-2)
, so
2\cos\theta\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus
\theta=2\pi/m
for
m\in\{2,3,4,6\}
and
(\sigma_{\alpha}\sigma_{\beta})^{m}=1
.
Since
s_{\alpha}
and
s_{\beta}
are integral affine transformations on
N
,
(s_{\alpha}s_{\beta})^{m}
is the identity on a neighborhood of
x
.
Again since
(s_{\alpha}s_{\beta})^{m}
is integral affine, following paths in
N
from
x
shows that
(s_{\alpha}s_{\beta})^{m}
is the identity at all points of
N
.
Hence
m_{\alpha\beta}=m\in\{2,3,4,6\}
. Lemma
A.1
implies
W
is a Weyl group.
∎

The hypothesis of simple connectivity in Theorem
A.3
is necessary:

Example A.4
.

For all
m>1
, the dihedral group
D_{m}
acts on
S^{1}\cong\mathbb{R}/m\mathbb{Z}
by integral affine reflections, but
D_{m}
is not a Weyl group unless
m\in\{2,3,4,6\}
.
For consider
\tilde{N}=\mathbb{R}
with standard integral affine structure.
Let
\Gamma=D_{\infty}=\langle s,t\mid s^{2}=t^{2}=1\rangle
be the infinite dihedral group, generated by reflections
s,t
about the points
0
and
1
of
\tilde{N}
. If
N=\tilde{N}/\langle(st)^{m}\rangle
, then
N
is an integral affine manifold and the dihedral group
D_{m}=\Gamma/\langle(st)^{m}\rangle
acts on
N
as a finite integral affine reflection group.

List of Notations

The following notations are used throughout this paper:

-(M,\pi)
:

manifold
M
with Poisson bivector
\pi\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(M)
(section
1
);

-\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
:

symplectic foliation of
(M,\pi)
. Its leaves are denoted
(S,\omega_{S})
(section
1
);

-(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
:

symplectic groupoid integrating
(M,\pi)
; its source/target maps are denoted
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}:\mathcal{G}\to M
(section
1
);

-(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\pi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}})
:

linear Poisson structure on the dual of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
(section
2.1
);

-(Q/G,\pi_{\mathrm{red}})
:

Hamiltonian local model associated with Hamiltonian
G
-space
\mu:(Q,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(section
2.1
);

-(\mathcal{M},\pi_{\mathrm{lin}})
,
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{lin}},L_{\mathrm{lin}})
:

Poisson and Dirac linear local models (section
2.2
);

-(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{lin}},\Omega_{\mathrm{lin}})
:

canonical (pre-)symplectic groupoid integrating the linear local models (sections
2.2
and
2.3
);

-\mathfrak{g}_{x}(M,\pi)

at

We also use some standard notations. For a linear subspace
W\subset V
we denote by
W^{0}\subset V^{*}
its annihilator, while
V^{\Gamma}
denotes the fixed-point set of a
\Gamma
-action. For a Lie group
G
we denote by
G^{0}
the component containing the identity and by
Z(G)
its center, while
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})
denotes the center of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. We also denote by
Z_{G}(H)
and by
N(H)
the centralizer and the normalizer of a subgroup
H\subset G
. For a compact Lie group
G
, we denote a maximal torus by
T
and by
W=N(T)/T
its Weyl group. At the Lie algebra level, a maximal torus is denoted by
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}
and the Grassmannian of maximal torus is denoted by
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})
. We use the symbol
\nu(\cdot)
to denote normal spaces and normal bundles to submanifolds and foliations.
